- *If asked about the Beast's capabilities:* "The shoulders are the weak point. Cut the tendons behind the shoulder blades and the arms lose their reach. The spine goes first in the transformation, then the jaw — when the jaw changes, I cannot speak. You will have a window while I am still partially in control. Use it."
- *If asked about what comes after:* "I have left instructions for Maranthe — my seneschal. She holds the key to the Great Ancillary, a temple at the center of Nexus that has been sealed since my companions and I returned. After I fall, she will open the way. Go inside. What you find there will explain more than I ever could."
- *If asked about Maranthe:* "She does not know what I am doing. She suspects more than she has ever said — she is not a fool, and the pattern of my absences and the Beast's appearances is not as well hidden as I have pretended. But she has never asked, and I have never told her. I left her a letter. She will find it soon." A pause. "She will try to stop me. That is why the doors are sealed."
- *If asked why he did not tell them sooner:* "Because you would have tried to save me. I have watched you. You are not the kind of people who kill a man when there might be another option. I could not risk the delay. The full moon is in four days."
- *If asked about the forty-three:* The number catches in his throat. Forty-three people killed by the Beast — that he knows of. Forty-three people who would be alive if he had done this sooner.

Casomir answers questions for as long as the party wishes to ask them. When the conversation reaches a natural end — or when the first tremor runs through his hands — he straightens and nods.

> *"It's starting," he says. "Thank you. For the Oracle. For this. For what you are about to do."*

**If the party protests, refuses, or attempts to leave:** Casomir has anticipated this. He watches the party argue, plead, or try the sealed doors, and his expression carries the particular grief of a man who predicted exactly this reaction and wishes he had been wrong. He does not argue back. He waits until the party's initial response has spent itself, and then he speaks.

> *"I understand," he says. "I would do the same, in your place. But you cannot leave this room, and I cannot give you more time." He holds up his hands. The tremor is already there — faint, but visible. "I have not taken my suppressants in three days. The transformation will come whether you agree to this or not. The only question is whether it comes while I still have enough control to help you fight it, or whether it comes after I have lost everything and the Beast is at full strength." His voice hardens — not with anger, but with the absolute clarity of a man who has made his decision and will not be moved from it. "I am giving you every advantage I can. Do not waste it."*

If the party continues to resist, Casomir triggers the transformation deliberately — ceasing his mental suppression entirely and allowing the corruption to surge forward. This removes the grace period the party would have had if they had agreed; the transformation begins immediately, and the party enters combat at a disadvantage (see the encounter below). If the party comes around before the transformation begins, play the scene as though they agreed, with whatever time remains for questions.

*GM note: The sealed doors are the key to this scene's dramatic structure. Casomir has removed the party's ability to walk away — not out of cruelty, but because he knows that the merciful option (delay, research, alternatives) is a trap. There is no alternative. The Oracle confirmed it. Every day of delay is a day closer to the full moon and a day in which the Beast might break free in the city. By sealing the arena, Casomir forces the party to confront the situation as it is rather than as they wish it were. Some parties will resent this. Let them. The resentment is honest and earned, and it will make the eventual grief more complex and more real.*

### Scene 3: The Beast

The transformation begins. Whether triggered by Casomir's failing control or forced by his deliberate release, the process is the same — only the timing changes.

**The Transformation**

The transformation is not instantaneous. It begins with small changes — Casomir's hands twisting, his posture shifting, bone grinding against bone beneath the skin — and accelerates over the course of two to three rounds. During this period, Casomir is still partially present. He speaks through gritted teeth, directing the party, calling out weak points, warning them when a surge of the Beast's power is about to hit. He is fighting the transformation with everything he has, buying the party time.

> *The change starts in his hands. The fingers lengthen, the knuckles swelling and splitting the skin, and Casomir looks down at them with an expression that is not surprise — he has seen this hundreds of times — but something closer to resignation. "It's starting," he says, and his voice is still his own. "The spine goes next. When the jaw changes, I won't be able to talk. Listen to me now — the shoulders are the weak point. Cut the tendons behind the shoulder blades and the arms lose their reach. Don't let it—" His back arches. Something cracks. He catches himself on his knees, and when he looks up, one eye is still Casomir's. The other is not.*

*If the party refused and Casomir triggered the transformation early:* The transformation is faster and more violent. Casomir's control is compromised by the deliberate release — he has less resistance to spend, and the Beast surges forward with a ferocity that suggests it has been waiting for this moment. Skip the read-aloud text above and use: *"The change is sudden and brutal. One moment Casomir is standing, his hands raised in something that might be surrender or might be apology. The next, his spine snaps backward at an angle no human body can sustain, and the sound that comes from his throat is not a voice but a tearing, as though something enormous is clawing its way out through too small an opening."* In this case, Phase 1 is shortened to 2 rounds instead of 3, and Casomir's Aid grants only a +1 circumstance bonus instead of +2.

**Encounter: The Emergence of Beast Blavin**

This encounter is designed as a phased fight with three stages, representing the Beast's progressive manifestation. The party is fighting a creature that is growing more powerful as the fight continues — urgency is the defining mechanic.

**Phase 1: The Half-Emerged Beast (Rounds 1–3)**

The Beast is partially manifested — larger than a man but not yet fully formed, its movements jerky and uncoordinated as Casomir resists from within. In this phase, the creature is vulnerable.

- **Stats:** Use a **Large creature** with approximately CR 4 statistics (AC 16, HP 55, two claw attacks at +7 for 1d8+4, bite at +7 for 1d6+4). The Beast has DR 5/— from its partially formed hide but suffers a –2 penalty to attack rolls and Reflex saves as Casomir's resistance impedes its movements.
- **Casomir's Aid:** Once per round, as a free action, Casomir reasserts control momentarily. This manifests as the Beast hesitating, lowering its guard, or exposing a weak point. The GM should describe this as Casomir's voice breaking through — a shouted instruction, a strangled "now!" — and grant the next PC to attack a +2 circumstance bonus to their attack roll.
- **Weak Points:** The shoulders, as Casomir described. A called shot to the shoulder tendons (attack at –4, or a successful Knowledge [nature] or Heal check DC 15 to identify the target without penalty) deals an additional 1d6 damage and reduces the Beast's claw attack bonus by 1 (cumulative, to a minimum of +3).

**Phase 2: The Full Emergence (Rounds 4–6)**

At the start of round 4, the transformation completes. The Beast is fully manifested — a massive, distorted humanoid form, its features a grotesque exaggeration of Casomir's own, radiating heat and the sharp ozone-scent of corrupted mythic power. Casomir's voice goes silent.

- **Stats:** The Beast's statistics improve. HP increases by 20 (to a maximum of 75 minus any damage already dealt), attack bonuses increase by +2, and the DR improves to DR 5/good. It gains a slam attack (+9, 2d6+6) as its body mass increases. Movement speed increases to 40 ft.
- **Casomir's Resistance Fades:** The +2 circumstance bonus from Casomir's aid is no longer available. The Beast acts with full autonomy.
- **Suppression Crystals:** The amber crystals on the walls pulse more intensely, and the chemical smell in the air grows stronger. The suppression system is still functioning, imposing a –1 penalty to the Beast's attack rolls and saving throws. If the Beast takes a full-round action to smash a crystal array (hardness 8, 15 hp), it can destroy one section of the suppression system per round. There are four sections; if all four are destroyed, the penalty is removed and the Beast gains fast healing 2 as its regenerative abilities are no longer suppressed.

**Phase 3: The Killswitch (Conditional)**

If the Beast reaches 30 or fewer HP in Phase 2, it attempts to destroy the remaining suppression crystals and break down the iron doors to escape. If it reaches the doors (hardness 10, 60 hp), the party has approximately 4 rounds before it breaks through.

If the party pulls the killswitch lever (a standard action by a character adjacent to the platform at the center of the room), the alchemical channels in the floor erupt with a thick amber gas that fills the chamber. All creatures in the arena take 2d6 points of nonlethal damage per round from the toxic suppressant (Fortitude DC 15 halves). The Beast's statistics are reduced: –4 to all attack rolls, –2 to AC, movement speed halved, and fast healing (if active) is suppressed. The gas disperses after 5 rounds, or immediately if a character retreats to the staircase and opens the ventilation grate there (a full-round action, Strength DC 12).

*GM note: The killswitch is a safety valve, not a win button. It makes the Beast manageable but poisons the party at the same time. The ideal outcome — from a narrative standpoint — is that the party kills the Beast in Phase 1 or early Phase 2, while Casomir is still partially present. This allows for a mercy killing that carries emotional weight. If the fight drags into late Phase 2 or Phase 3, the Beast is fully in control and the kill is a combat victory, not a mercy — still satisfying, but different in tone. The killswitch exists to prevent a TPK in a chapter where a TPK would end the campaign. Use it if the party is in genuine danger of being wiped out, but not before.*

*The specific stat blocks for this encounter will be provided in the Stat Block Appendix. The numbers above are guidelines — adjust HP, AC, and attack bonuses as needed to produce a fight that is hard, frightening, and survivable for a level 3 party of four. The Beast should drop at least one PC to negative hit points during the fight. It should not drop more than two unless the party makes serious tactical errors.*

**Casomir's Death**

When the Beast is killed, the transformation reverses. The monstrous form contracts, the distorted features smooth, and what is left is Casomir — broken, bleeding, dying, but himself again. If the killing blow was struck during Phase 1, Casomir has a moment of lucidity. He can speak a few words — enough to thank the party, to tell them to find the Ancillary, to say something the GM determines is appropriate to the specific party and the relationships they have built over three chapters.

> *The Beast collapses. The massive frame shudders, contracts, and then Casomir is lying on the stone floor, smaller than you remember, his body torn by the transformation and the wounds the party inflicted on the creature that wore his shape. His breathing is shallow and irregular. One hand reaches for the sword he drove into the floor — not to grasp it, but to touch the hilt, as though confirming that something still belongs to him.*

If Casomir has a moment of lucidity (Phase 1 kill), add:

> *His eyes find you. They are clear — entirely, unmistakably his. "The Ancillary," he says. "Maranthe has the key. She'll know what to do." A breath. Another. "Tell them I held on as long as I could." His hand falls from the sword. The amber light of the suppression crystals dims as the system, no longer needed, powers down. The chemical smell fades. What is left is silence, and a man who kept his word.*

If the Beast was killed in Phase 2 or 3, Casomir does not speak. The party finds him in the wreckage of the creature, already gone. The silence is its own kind of grief.

*GM note: Allow the party time with this moment. Do not rush to the next scene. Casomir's death is the emotional hinge of Phase 1, and the weight of it should be felt before the wonder of the Ancillary replaces it. If a player wants to take Casomir's sword, his armor, or his Oracle's token — let them. These are not magic items (the sword and armor are masterwork magitek equipment, and the token is as described in Chapter 2), but they carry narrative significance that may become important later.*

### Scene 4: Maranthe

The iron doors reset approximately one hour after sealing. When they open, **Maranthe Soldati** is on the other side.

She is not composed. She is not the efficient, steady administrator the party met at the start of this chapter. She is a woman who found a letter on her desk ten minutes after the king of Nexus walked into a sealed basement with the only people in five centuries he trusted enough to kill him, and who has spent the last hour descending through the citadel at a dead run, pulling rank on guards, breaking through locked doors, and arriving too late.

> *The iron doors grind open. The woman standing in the stairwell is breathing hard, her uniform disordered, a crumpled letter clutched in one fist. Her eyes go past you to the arena floor — to the body — and something in her face breaks in a way that makes the violence you just survived feel simple by comparison.*
>
> *"You—" she starts, and stops. She looks at you. She looks at Casomir. She looks at the blood on your weapons and the amber residue on the floor and the suppression crystals dimming on the walls. Her jaw works. When she speaks again, her voice is controlled, but the control is a visible effort, like watching someone hold a door shut against a storm.*
>
> *"He wrote me a letter," she says. "He left it on my desk where I would find it after you went down. He explained everything. The Beast. The corruption. What he was asking you to do." She unfolds the letter, then folds it again, as though the act of reading it is something she cannot do in front of other people. "I tried to reach you. The doors were sealed."*

Maranthe's reaction depends on the party's demeanor and what they tell her. She is not hostile — she understands, intellectually, what Casomir did and why — but she is grieving and angry, and the anger is not entirely rational. She is angry at Casomir for not telling her. She is angry at herself for not pressing the question when the suspicions were there. She is angry at the party for being the ones Casomir trusted with his death when she was the one who had stood beside him for a hundred years. None of this is the party's fault, and she knows it, but grief does not answer to logic.

*If the party explains what happened:* Maranthe listens. She asks sharp questions — how long the fight lasted, whether he spoke at the end, whether he was in pain. She does not ask whether it was necessary. She read the letter. She knows it was.

*If the party shows sympathy or acknowledges her loss:* Maranthe accepts it stiffly. She is not a woman who grieves publicly, and the party's kindness may crack her composure further than she is willing to allow. A brief nod, a tight "thank you," and then she straightens and becomes the seneschal again.

*If the party is callous or dismissive:* Maranthe's anger crystallizes. She does not threaten or obstruct — she is a professional, and the letter makes clear that the party's mission is more important than her feelings — but the relationship is damaged. She will be efficient and helpful for the remainder of the chapter, and she will not be warm.

After the initial confrontation, Maranthe's composure reasserts itself — not fully, and not without visible effort, but enough to function. She has read the letter. She knows what comes next.

> *She refolds the letter and tucks it into her belt. When she looks at you again, her expression has changed — not softened, but settled, as though she has put the grief somewhere she can reach it later and brought the rest of herself back to the work at hand. "He left me instructions," she says. "The passage to the Great Ancillary — I have the key. He gave it to me years ago and told me I would know when to use it." She looks once more at the body on the arena floor, and whatever she sees there is between her and the man she served. Then she turns. "Follow me."*

*GM note: Maranthe's ignorance of Casomir's plan is essential to this scene's emotional honesty. If she had known and agreed, her arrival would be procedural — a scheduled handoff. Instead, her arrival is a reckoning. The party has just killed the king of Nexus in his own basement, and the first person they face afterward is someone who loved him and whom he deliberately excluded from the decision. This is not comfortable. It should not be comfortable. Casomir's final act was selfless and necessary and also a betrayal of the person closest to him, and Maranthe's reaction is the cost of that choice. Play it honestly.*

*Maranthe's long suspicion about the connection between Casomir and the Beast is a detail worth seeding in earlier chapters if the GM wishes. A stray comment from a guard about how the seneschal always seems to know when the barricades are needed. Maranthe's particular attentiveness during conversations about the Beast. A moment in Chapter 1 or 2 where the party notices her watching Casomir with an expression that is not quite worry and not quite understanding. These small details make her arrival in this scene feel earned rather than convenient.*

### Scene 5: The Great Ancillary

Maranthe leads the party to a section of the citadel's lower levels that they passed on their descent to the arena but did not enter — a sealed door of the same ancient stone as the Oracle's sanctum, conspicuously different from the surrounding architecture. She produces a heavy iron key — old, ornate, unmistakably magitek, with crystalline inlays that pulse with a faint inner light.

> *"The passage to the Great Ancillary has been sealed since before I was born," she says. "He gave me this key years ago and told me I would know when to use it." She turns it in the lock. The mechanism engages with a sound that is less mechanical than harmonic — a deep resonance that travels through the stone floor and settles somewhere behind the sternum. The door opens. "He said you would know what to do when you arrived." A pause. "He also said to tell you that he was proud of you. He asked me to write it down so I would say it in those words."*

The door opens onto a corridor of polished stone that descends gently for several hundred yards before opening into the Ancillary itself.

> *The Great Ancillary is not a room. It is a space — vast, circular, and open to something above that is not the sky. The ceiling, if it can be called that, is a dome of darkness studded with points of light that move, slowly, in patterns that are not random. Beneath this dome, suspended by forces that have no visible source, hangs the orrery.*
>
> *Eight spheres of stone and crystal orbit a central sphere at the dome's heart, each one different — one sheathed in ice, one wrapped in cloud, one burning with a steady amber flame, one dark as a moonless night. The central sphere glows with a warm golden light. Above them all, a tenth sphere hangs motionless, radiant, impossibly bright, casting light downward onto the mechanism below. The orrery turns with a faint harmonic resonance — not quite music, not quite silence, but something in between that settles into the bones.*
>
> *Beneath the orrery, inscribed into the floor of the chamber, is a great diagram of interlocking runes. The lines glow with a light that matches the central sphere above — warm, steady, alive. The diagram is large enough for a dozen people to stand within it. The runes are not in any language you can read, but you understand, with a certainty that has no rational basis, that you are meant to step inside.*

When the party enters the rune circle, they are taken outside space and time. The chamber does not vanish — it simply ceases to be relevant. The party stands in a space that has no walls, no floor, no sky, only light and presence. The celestial representatives appear.

**The Celestial Meeting**

The celestials are three — one Archon, one Angel, one Agathion — representing the three races that created the Nine Worlds. They are not physical beings in this space. They are presences, vast and luminous, sorrowful and ancient. They do not introduce themselves with names. They may offer titles or epithets if pressed, but what matters is the impression: these are the architects of everything the party has seen, and they have been waiting for this moment since the system was first conceived.

> *Three presences resolve from the light. They are not humanoid — or they are, and more, and less, shifting between forms that your mind interprets differently each time you try to focus. One carries the weight of law in every line of its being, as though justice itself had taken form and found the burden heavier than expected. One moves like mercy — fluid, grieving, unwilling or unable to look away from the suffering of the world. One is wild but patient, with the stillness of deep forests and the attention span of something that has been watching the world grow since before it had a name for itself.*
>
> *They regard you. The regard is not judgement — you have already been judged, by the Oracle, by Casomir, by the road you walked to reach this place. What the celestials offer is recognition. They see what you are. They see what you may become. And in their vast, sorrowful faces — if faces is the right word — you see something that might be hope.*

The celestials convey the following, through speech or through direct understanding (the GM should use whichever feels appropriate for the tone of their game):

1. **Confirmation.** The party has been chosen. The Oracle's token confirms their worthiness. The death of Casomir — the last of the original champions — marks the beginning of a new cycle. The Nine Worlds were built to produce heroes. The party is what the worlds produced.

2. **The Mission.** The eight outer worlds are each ruled by one of Casomir's former companions, each corrupted by the same force that created the Beast. The Rainbow Road — the network of pathways connecting the worlds — has been shut down for five centuries. To restore it, the party must travel to each world, confront its Warlock, and activate the mythic place of power that serves as the Road's anchor on that world. Each place of power will grant the party a portion of mythic energy and a fragment of the Song of Heaven — the melodic key that opens the next segment of the Road.

3. **The Choice.** The Warlocks are not monsters. They are people — corrupted, damaged, dangerous, but people. Some can be saved. Some cannot. The celestials do not tell the party which is which. They say only that the manner in which the party confronts each Warlock will shape the outcome of the journey and the fate of the worlds themselves. Every death carries weight. Every redemption carries cost.

4. **The Grant.** The celestials bestow mythic tier 1. The GM should describe this as a physical and spiritual transformation — the party feels the power of the system enter them, ancient and purposeful, designed for mortal vessels but originating from something far beyond mortal understanding. Each PC chooses their mythic path at this time (Archmage, Champion, Guardian, Hierophant, Marshal, or Trickster). The celestials present the paths as facets of the same power — the party is not choosing a specialization so much as discovering which aspect of the mythic force resonates with their nature.

5. **The First Fragment.** The celestials teach the party the first fragment of the Song of Heaven — a short melodic phrase, simple but haunting, that settles into their memory with the permanence of something that has always been known and only now remembered. This fragment, when carried to the gate town that once served the world of Diaspola, will reopen that segment of the Rainbow Road.

If the party shows the celestials the Oracle's token, the celestials acknowledge it with something that approaches reverence. They confirm that it is the Oracle's mark of worthiness — the same seal she placed on Casomir's group five hundred years ago. If the party also carries Casomir's token (taken from his body or given to them before his death), the celestials' response deepens. Two tokens in the hands of the same group has not happened before. The significance is not explained, but the celestials' reaction makes clear that it matters.

*GM note: The celestial meeting is not a dialogue — it is a ceremony. The celestials answer questions, but their answers are compressed and allusive, more like the Oracle's speech than ordinary conversation. They do not explain the corruption mechanic, the ally-tracking system, or the specifics of Warlock turning. They speak of choices and consequences in terms that are true but not yet useful. The party will understand them retroactively, as they understood the Oracle's words. The purpose of this scene is to give the party their mission, their power, and their first step — and to make all three feel earned.*

*If a player asks which mythic path is "best":* All paths are viable. The celestials do not recommend. If the party lacks healing, a Hierophant is useful; if the party is melee-heavy, a Champion or Guardian provides mythic combat options; if the party wants mythic spellcasting, Archmage or Hierophant. Marshal is excellent for parties that want a tactical leader. There are no wrong choices — the system adapts to what the champions become.*

### Scene 6: The First Step

The party returns to the physical world in the Great Ancillary. The orrery still turns above them, but something has changed — one of the eight orbiting spheres, the one sheathed in ice, now glows faintly brighter than the others, as though responding to the Song fragment the party carries. The rune circle beneath their feet has dimmed. The ceremony is complete.

Maranthe is waiting outside the sealed door. She does not ask what happened inside — she can see it in them. Something has changed, and whatever it is, she recognizes the shape of it because she saw it described once, in Casomir's private journals, when he wrote about what he and his companions looked like the day they walked out of this same door five centuries ago.

> *"It's done, then," she says. Not a question. She studies you for a moment, and whatever she sees confirms something she has been weighing since the arena. "He left provisions. Maps. Everything you would need." She produces a sealed chest. "He also left you a letter. And he left me a job to do while you are gone — someone has to hold this city together, and he seems to have decided that someone is me." The ghost of something that is not quite a smile crosses her face. "He was always better at delegating than he admitted."*

Maranthe produces a sealed chest containing Casomir's personal provisions for the journey: a set of maps covering the Rainbow Road terminus points for each gate town (drawn from the original expedition's records and updated where possible), a supply of alchemical goods from the citadel's stores (GM determines specifics — healing supplies, alchemical weapons appropriate for level 4 characters, and one or two utility items), and a letter.

The letter is brief. It is addressed to "the ones who come after" and written in Casomir's precise hand:

> *You carry what I could not. The Song will guide you — follow it, and the Road opens. Each world has a place of power. Each place of power has a guardian who was once my friend. Some of them can still be reached. Some cannot. You will know the difference when you stand before them.*
>
> *The path is fixed: Diaspola first, then Tanis, then Auzra, and so through all the worlds. The Song determines the order. Do not attempt to deviate — the Road will not permit it.*
>
> *Trust the Oracle's judgement. She chose you for a reason, even if she did not explain it. She never explains.*
>
> *I held on as long as I could.*
>
> *— Casomir Zukra, King of Nexus, Last of the Ten*

*GM note: The letter serves double duty — it is an emotional capstone to Casomir's arc and a practical reference document that confirms the world sequence for the party. If the party has questions about the order of worlds or the nature of their mission, the letter provides answers without requiring the GM to break the celestials' allusive tone. Casomir, characteristically, prepared for the possibility that the divine briefing would leave his successors confused.*

*Maranthe's role going forward: She becomes the regent of Nexus in Casomir's absence — a position no one appointed her to and everyone accepts because she is the only person capable of holding the city together. The party will encounter her again at the start of Chapter 12 (The Battle of Nexus), where the strength of her leadership and the state of the city's defenses will depend in part on the ally strength the party has accumulated across the eight worlds. She is a recurring NPC — competent, loyal, still grieving, and determined to make Casomir's death mean what he intended it to mean.*

**Milestone:** Level 4 and Mythic Tier 1 are awarded at the conclusion of this chapter. The party should have time to level up, choose mythic paths, and prepare before departing for the Diaspola gate town.

---

## Phase 2: The Eight Worlds

*In which the PCs walk the Rainbow Road, confront the corrupted heroes who came before them, and learn the cost of the power they carry.*

Phase 2 is the campaign's longest section — eight chapters across eight worlds, covering levels 5 through 19 and mythic tiers 2 through 9. Each chapter follows the same structural arc: the party arrives via the Rainbow Road, explores a world in crisis, discovers the Warlock's lever, confronts the Warlock, accesses the mythic place of power, and departs. The specifics vary enormously, but the structure provides a reliable rhythm that the players will internalize and that the GM can use to manage pacing.

The worlds are visited in a fixed sequence determined by the Song of Heaven. The party cannot skip worlds, revisit previously completed worlds during Phase 2, or travel between worlds without returning to Nexus first. Each return to Nexus is an opportunity for resupply, narrative updates on the escalating demon threat, and check-ins with Maranthe Soldati, who is holding the city together in Casomir's absence. These interludes should be brief — a scene or two at most — but they build investment in the hub world before the Battle of Nexus demands it.

The first two worlds — Diaspola and Tanis — are the gentlest introductions to the turning mechanic. Their levers are relatively accessible, their Warlocks relatively sympathetic, and their crises relatively straightforward. This is deliberate. The party needs to learn how turning works before facing the worlds where the mechanic becomes genuinely difficult.

---

## Chapter 4: Diaspola — The Frozen Court *(Levels 4–6, Mythic Tier 2)*

### Chapter Summary

Diaspola is a world of water and warmth turned to ice and silence. Once the most beautiful of the Nine Worlds — a pastoral landscape of gentle hills, warm rain, small lakes, and a great freshwater sea dotted with island retreats — it has been frozen solid for centuries by the unchecked mythic power of its Warlock, Sona Brightspring, the Snow Queen. The freeze was not Sona's intention. She was manipulated into it by **Khaga**, a Nixie witch who leads the corrupted Fey that are the true power on this world. Khaga engineered the entire crisis: fabricating evil spirits to torment Sona, offering ice magic as the solution, and ensuring the magic always needed to be used again because the spirits always seemed to escape. Sona froze her own world trying to protect it, and she has never understood what was done to her.

The party arrives to find a realm of ice inhabited by hardy Halfling communities struggling to survive, a population of corrupted Fey who torment the mortals and answer to Khaga, and a withdrawn, paranoid Snow Queen who has ceded all real authority to her fey handlers. The crisis on Diaspola is not a tyrant to overthrow — it is a victim to rescue. The party must see past the Snow Queen's fearsome reputation, navigate the corrupted Fey's territorial games, locate the Ivory Music Box hidden on a distant island, and return it to Sona. The music box's melody breaks Khaga's spell, and Sona — restored to clarity and fury — destroys Khaga and the corrupted Fey herself. The party does not fight the Warlock. They free her.

This is the campaign's first turning and the most accessible. The lever is hidden but findable. The antagonist is Khaga, not Sona. The emotional beat is liberation, not tragedy. The party should leave Diaspola understanding how the turning mechanic works and feeling the satisfaction of a world visibly healing behind them — a feeling that will become harder to earn on subsequent worlds.

### GM Guidance

**Tone.** Diaspola has the feel of a dark fairy tale — a frozen kingdom, a captive queen, a wicked witch, a magical object that breaks the spell. Lean into this. The Halfling communities have the quality of villages in a winter story: hearth fires, heavy cloaks, suspicion of strangers, folk wisdom about the Fey passed down through generations. The corrupted Fey behave like fairy-tale antagonists: they trick, they curse, they play cruel games with mortals, and they can be bested by those who understand their rules. Khaga herself is a classic manipulator — charming, patient, and absolutely certain that no one can undo what she has built. The tone should be cold and wondrous, dangerous but not hopeless. The world is beautiful even under the ice, and the party should feel that beauty waiting to return.

**Fey Etiquette.** The corrupted Fey on Diaspola are bound by rules of conduct rooted in their nature. They cannot break a promise made under their true name. They cannot refuse a gift freely given if the gift is offered with honest intent, and acceptance binds them to reciprocate with something of equal value (though they will try to redefine "equal value" in their favor). They cannot enter a mortal dwelling uninvited. A bargain struck with a fey is binding on both parties, and the terms are interpreted literally — a fey who promises to "show you the way" will show you a way, not necessarily the safe one. The party can exploit these rules if they learn them. Halfling elders know fragments of fey etiquette passed down through oral tradition. Non-aligned Fey know the rules in full and may share them if approached correctly.

The GM should establish fey etiquette early — ideally during the party's first interactions with the Halflings in Scene 2, before they encounter fey directly — so that the players have tools to work with throughout the chapter. Do not gatekeep fey etiquette behind high-DC Knowledge checks. Make it accessible through roleplay and NPC interaction. The fun is in using the knowledge, not in acquiring it.

**The Snow Queen.** Sona is not a villain. She is a prisoner who does not know she is imprisoned. Every interaction with her should communicate this: the fear in her eyes, the way she flinches at sounds, the desperate gratitude she shows toward Khaga — the only person who has "helped" her for centuries. The party may be tempted to fight their way to Sona and present the lever immediately. This is mechanically possible but narratively hollow. The chapter is designed to give the party time to understand what was done to Sona before they undo it, so that the turning scene carries emotional weight. Do not rush the party past the fey encounters and the Halfling communities. Those scenes are where the empathy for Sona is built.

**The Lever.** The Ivory Music Box is hidden in Sona's old tower on a far island in the frozen sea. The primary path to the music box runs through **Pipkin** — the Old Man of the Forest — who knows the full story of Sona's family and the tower's location. Pipkin tests the party before revealing himself (Scene 3), and the tests are designed to measure character rather than combat ability: compassion, cleverness, and courage. A party that passes Pipkin's tests receives precise directions to the tower and critical context about the music box's significance. A party that fails must rely on the Halflings' fragmentary oral tradition and a longer, more uncertain search across the frozen sea. The fey sentinels guarding the tower are Khaga's agents, placed there specifically to prevent anyone from recovering the box.

**Khaga.** The party does not fight Khaga directly. When the music box plays, Sona's mythic power reasserts itself, and the Snow Queen destroys Khaga in a scene that should feel like a dam breaking — five centuries of suppressed rage and grief released in a single devastating moment. While Sona deals with Khaga, the party fights her corrupted fey retinue in a collapsing-palace encounter (see the encounter in Scene 6).

**The Ember Circle.** Khaga has seeded a false rebel group near the ice palace to lure potential threats into a trap. The party is warned about this in Scene 2, but may choose to investigate it regardless — particularly if they cannot recover the music box. The Ember Circle trap (Scene 7) is a viable but dangerous alternate path into the palace that rewards bold play while punishing carelessness.

**Level Progression.** The party enters at level 4 (mythic tier 1) and should reach level 5 during the chapter — ideally after the island journey and the fey sentinel encounter — and level 6 at the chapter's conclusion. Mythic tier 2 is granted at the Mother of Rivers.

### Scene 1: The Gate Town and the Frozen Threshold

The party travels from Nexus to the Diaspola gate town — the clustered settlement that once surrounded the Rainbow Road terminus leading to Sona's world. The gate town is largely abandoned; those who remain are the stubborn and the poor, people who have made an art of hiding from the demons and living in the margins. The terminus itself is a stone platform at the center of a circular plaza, its surface carved with channels that once glowed with the Road's light. The channels are dark.

> *The gate town is a ruin with residents. The buildings stand, but most are empty — their shutters broken, their gardens gone to thornbrush, their doors marked with the sigils the watch paints on structures they have cleared of demon presence. The few people you see move with the economy of those who have learned that standing still in the open is an invitation. The terminus plaza is at the center of town, a raised stone platform surrounded by buildings that were once taverns, supply shops, and traveler's hostels. Everything faces inward, toward the platform. Everything is waiting for something that stopped coming five hundred years ago.*

The party must perform the Song of Heaven fragment — the melodic piece granted at the Ancillary — at the terminus. How this performance works is at the GM's discretion: it may require one PC to sing or play an instrument, or the entire party may need to participate. The Song is mythic knowledge, not a learned skill; any PC who carries a mythic tier can reproduce their fragment without a Performance check. When the fragment is performed, the channels in the terminus stone ignite with pale light, the air above the platform shimmers, and the Rainbow Road opens — a corridor of shifting, translucent color that stretches upward and away from the platform, curving out of sight.

> *The sound leaves your throat and the stone answers. The channels carved into the platform fill with light — not fire, not electricity, but something older and stranger, a luminance that has color without heat and depth without shadow. The air above the platform bends. You can see through it, but what you see is not the sky above the gate town — it is a road, curving upward through a space that has no horizon, its surface the color of everything and nothing, shifting as you watch. It is beautiful. It is real. And it is waiting for you.*

The journey along the Rainbow Road is brief — minutes, not hours — and should be described as a disorienting, wondrous transit through a space that is not entirely physical. The party emerges on a corresponding terminus platform on Diaspola, and the Road closes behind them. They cannot return until the chapter is complete.

> *The cold hits you first. Not the cold of a winter morning but the cold of a place that has forgotten warmth entirely — a deep, structural cold that reaches through your clothing and into your bones as though your body is an inconvenience it intends to correct. The sky is white. The ground is white. The wind carries ice crystals that sting exposed skin. The terminus platform you stand on is slick with frost, and the buildings around it — a gate town that mirrors the one you left — are buried to their second stories in compacted snow. Somewhere in the distance, something cracks — the sound of ice shifting under its own weight, a noise this world makes constantly, as though it is slowly crushing itself.*

*GM note: The environmental cold on Diaspola is a persistent hazard, not a gimmick. Characters without cold weather gear or magical protection must make Fortitude saves (DC 15, increasing by 1 for every hour of exposure) or take 1d6 nonlethal cold damage per failed save. The Halfling communities know this and will offer or trade cold-weather supplies as their first interaction with outsiders. This is an opportunity for the party to establish goodwill early. Endure elements, resist energy (cold), and similar protections negate the hazard entirely.*

### Scene 2: The Halfling Communities

The nearest Halfling settlement is a cluster of stone buildings partially buried in snow, their chimneys producing thin streams of smoke, their windows shuttered against the wind. The community is called **Hearthhollow** — a name that predates the freeze, when it was a farming village. Now it is a survival camp of approximately two hundred Halflings who subsist on ice fishing, fungal cultivation in the cellars beneath their buildings, and careful rationing of preserved food stores that were never meant to last this long.

The Halflings are suspicious of outsiders. Travelers are rare — the Rainbow Road has been dormant, and the only non-Halflings who visit are the Fey, whose visits are rarely welcomed. A party of armed, full-sized humanoids arriving through the long-dormant terminus will provoke caution, not hostility. The Halflings are pragmatic. If the party has something to offer — combat ability against the Fey, supplies, or simply news from beyond the ice — they will be welcomed, if guardedly.

> *The door opens a crack. An eye appears — brown, sharp, set in a face that is weathered beyond its years. "You came through the Road," the Halfling says. It is not a question. The door opens wider. Behind the speaker, a room lit by a low fire, and faces — a dozen of them, none taller than your waist, all watching you with an intensity that makes clear they have been discussing your arrival since the moment the terminus lit up. "We saw the light. Figured it was the Fey playing another trick." A pause. "You're not Fey." This is also not a question. "Come in. Close the door. The wind gets in."*

**What the Halflings Know:**

The GM should convey the following information through conversation with the Hearthhollow residents. Not all of it should be delivered at once — spread it across multiple interactions as the party earns trust.

- **The Snow Queen** lives in an ice palace on the far side of the land, surrounded by fey courtiers. She rarely leaves. The Halflings fear her but do not hate her — the older residents remember stories, passed down from their grandparents' grandparents, of a time when the Queen was kind. Some believe she still is, somewhere beneath the fear and the ice.
- **The good queen.** Bramble knows the old stories better than anyone in Hearthhollow. Before the ice, Sona Brightspring grew up in a tower on one of the outer islands of the great sea. Her father was a famous sorcerer — a court wizard who served the goodly fey king who ruled Diaspola before the apocalypse. The family loved music. "Her father played for her every evening," Bramble says. "The stories say the music could be heard across the water on still nights. She grew up with it. It was the center of her world." The tower still stands, as far as anyone knows, though no one from the Halfling communities has ventured that far across the frozen sea in generations.
- **Khaga** is the name the Halflings use with reluctance. She is the Nixie witch who speaks for the Queen and commands the corrupted Fey. The Halflings know she is the real power on this world, though they cannot articulate the mechanism. "The Queen does what Khaga says. She has always done what Khaga says."
- **The corrupted Fey** torment the Halfling communities — stealing supplies, cursing individuals who displease them, replacing children with changelings during the Wild Hunt. The Halflings have learned to endure this through a combination of appeasement and knowledge of fey rules. The village elder, **Mayor Bramble Thistledown**, knows fragments of fey etiquette: the true-name binding, the gift rule, the threshold rule. She will share this knowledge if the party demonstrates respect for her people.
- **The Wild Hunt** is due in a matter of weeks. The Queen's annual procession through the frozen land — once festive, now dreaded. During the Hunt, Sona selects four children as "royal guests" who are taken to the ice palace and never return as themselves. The Halflings know the children are replaced by changelings. They do not know how to prevent it.
- **The Old Man of the Forest.** If the party asks about allies, or about anyone who might know more about the queen's past, Bramble grows thoughtful. There is a being the Halflings call **the Old Man of the Forest** who inhabits the woods east of the village. He is fey, but not one of Khaga's — this much is certain, because Khaga's agents fear him. He is a powerful druid who appears in different guises: an old woodcutter, a traveling peddler, a hermit gathering mushrooms, and several more. Bramble goes into the woods to speak with him sometimes, though she cannot predict when or where he will appear. "He shows himself when he wants to, and not before. He has helped us — never directly, never in a way that Khaga could trace back to him, but supplies appear after he visits, and the fey patrols give our village a wider berth than the others." She warns the party that the woods have recently become home to a vicious creature — something large and predatory that the Halflings have heard but not seen. They have shunned the forest since it arrived. Bramble suspects the creature is one of Khaga's pets, placed there to discourage anyone from seeking the Old Man.
- **The hotheads.** Bramble mentions, with visible disapproval, that a group of young Halflings in **Frostmere** — a village closer to the ice palace — have been talking openly about striking against Khaga directly. "They call themselves the Ember Circle. Bold talk. Too bold. They say they have a way into the palace basement — a route through the old service tunnels that Khaga does not know about — and they are recruiting anyone with fighting ability for a nighttime assault." Bramble does not trust it. "Those tunnels have been sealed for centuries. How would a group of young hotheads know about them? And why would Khaga's spies — who hear everything — let them talk so freely?" She cannot prove it is a trap, but her instincts say it is. The party may choose to investigate the Ember Circle regardless — see Scene 7: The Ember Circle Trap.

**Fey Etiquette — What Bramble Knows:**

The elder's knowledge is fragmentary but accurate. She can teach the party the following rules, presented as folk wisdom rather than academic knowledge:

- "Never accept a fey's gift unless you understand what it costs. They cannot give freely — there is always a price, and the price is in the terms."
- "If a fey tells you its true name, you own a piece of it. Make it promise under its own name and it cannot break the promise. But it will never tell you its name willingly."
- "They cannot cross your threshold uninvited. If you are inside a dwelling — any dwelling, even a ruin — and you have not invited them, they cannot enter. This does not stop them from collapsing the building."
- "A gift given honestly — no trick, no hidden purpose — must be accepted. They hate this. It binds them to reciprocate, and the reciprocation must be of equal value. They will try to redefine 'equal value' in their favor."

### Scene 3: The Old Man of the Forest

The woods east of Hearthhollow are dense, dark, and unwelcoming — a forest of ancient pines half-crushed under centuries of accumulated ice, their trunks twisted by the weight, their canopy so thick that the pale sky is reduced to fragments. The Halflings have avoided these woods since a large predator took up residence several months ago, and the silence beneath the trees carries a quality that suggests the forest knows it is being left alone and prefers it that way. This is where the Old Man lives.

**Pipkin** — for that is his true name, though no one on Diaspola has spoken it in centuries — is a Brownie of extraordinary age and considerable power. He was once a member of the goodly fey king's court, a servant and companion to Sona's father, and he watched the corruption of the fey and the freezing of the world with a grief that has not diminished in five hundred years. He has survived Khaga's purges through cunning, magical ability, and a willingness to be underestimated. To the corrupted fey he is a rumor, a nuisance, a trickster who steals supplies and leaves false trails. To the Halflings he is something closer to a folk hero — an unseen benefactor who keeps the worst of the fey at bay through methods no one fully understands.

Pipkin suspects that the reopening of the Rainbow Road may herald the arrival of new mythic heroes. He has been waiting for this possibility for a very long time. But he is old, and careful, and he has seen too many good intentions fail to take anything on faith. Before he reveals himself and shares what he knows, he will test the party — not to prove their strength, but to measure their character.

**Entering the Woods**

If the party ventures into the forest, they find the Halflings' warnings justified. The woods are oppressive — cold, dark, and eerily quiet except for the occasional crack of ice-laden branches and the distant sound of something large moving through the undergrowth. The trails are overgrown and the landmarks unfamiliar. After an hour of travel, the party begins to feel watched. This is not paranoia — Pipkin has been tracking them since they crossed the tree line.

The three tests occur sequentially along the forest path. Each test is presented by Pipkin in a different guise. The party does not know they are being tested, and they do not know that the three figures they encounter are the same being. A Sense Motive check (DC 22) or *true seeing* reveals the disguise, but Pipkin does not punish the party for seeing through it — he simply acknowledges the insight with a wry nod and proceeds with the test regardless. The tests can be completed in any order if the party deviates from the path, but the sequence below represents the natural progression through the forest.

**Test 1: The Test of Compassion**

The party encounters a figure huddled at the base of a tree — a small, elderly Halfling woman wrapped in rags, shivering violently, her lips blue and her fingers blackened by frostbite. She is weeping softly. A crude camp is visible nearby: a collapsed lean-to, a dead fire pit, and the scattered remains of a food cache that has been torn apart by animals. She appears to be a refugee from one of the outlying settlements — someone who fled into the woods and could not find her way back.

This is Pipkin's first guise. The "woman" is convincing — the cold, the injuries, and the distress are all real in appearance, and a Heal check (DC 12) confirms that the frostbite is genuine (Pipkin's illusions are thorough). She begs the party for help: warmth, food, healing, shelter. She claims she has been lost for two days and cannot feel her hands.

*What Pipkin is measuring:* Whether the party stops. Whether they share resources. Whether their first instinct is to help someone who offers them nothing in return and represents a delay to their mission. The test is not complex — it is simply a question of whether the party treats a suffering person as a person or as an obstacle.

- **Pass:** The party provides meaningful aid — sharing rations, using healing magic, offering a cloak, building a fire, or offering to guide her to safety. The quality of the aid matters less than the willingness to provide it. After the party has demonstrated genuine compassion, the "woman" thanks them with a warmth that seems out of proportion to the gesture, presses a small carved wooden token into the hand of whoever helped her first (a pine cone, intricately detailed — this is Pipkin's calling card, though the party does not know that yet), and then quietly slips away when the party's attention is elsewhere. She is simply gone. The token remains.
- **Fail:** The party ignores the woman, interrogates her aggressively, or offers only perfunctory assistance before moving on. Pipkin does not punish this — he simply disappears when the party is not looking, leaving behind a faint impression of disappointment in the snow. No token.

**Test 2: The Test of Cleverness**

Deeper in the forest, the path opens into a small clearing where a figure sits on a fallen log beside a fire that burns without fuel — the flames lick upward from bare stone, producing warmth but consuming nothing. The figure is an old man: weathered, sharp-eyed, dressed in a patchwork cloak of animal furs that has seen better centuries. He is whittling a piece of wood with a knife that appears to be made of ice. He looks up as the party approaches and grins — a grin that is friendly and challenging in equal measure.

> *"Travelers," he says, as though the word amuses him. "In my forest. That hasn't happened in a long time." He gestures to the fire. "Sit. Warm yourselves. I have a proposition."*

This is Pipkin's second guise. He introduces himself as **Thorn** — a hermit who has lived in these woods "longer than the ice, longer than the queen, longer than most things worth remembering." He offers the party a game: a contest of riddles. His terms are simple. He will ask the party three riddles. If they can answer all three correctly, or if they can pose a riddle that stumps him, they win his respect and he will share something valuable — "a story worth more than gold in this frozen land." If they fail, they owe him a favor to be named later (fey bargain rules apply — the favor will be fair but inconvenient).

**Thorn's Three Riddles:**

*Riddle 1:* "I have cities but no houses, forests but no trees, rivers but no water. What am I?"
**Answer:** A map.

*Riddle 2:* "The more you take from me, the larger I grow. The more you give to me, the smaller I become. What am I?"
**Answer:** A hole (or a pit, a grave, etc.).

*Riddle 3:* "I am not alive, yet I grow. I have no lungs, yet I need air. I have no mouth, yet water kills me. What am I?"
**Answer:** Fire.

The riddles are fair — solvable through reasoning, appropriate for the fairy-tale tone, and not reliant on obscure knowledge. If the party struggles, Thorn enjoys the process. He leans forward, his eyes bright, and offers cryptic hints that are themselves half-riddles. He is having fun. He has not had a conversation worth having in a very long time.

**Stumping Thorn:** If a PC poses a riddle, Thorn considers it with genuine concentration. The GM should adjudicate whether the riddle is truly clever — a riddle with a unique or unexpected answer that requires lateral thinking succeeds automatically. A riddle that is merely obscure (relying on specialized knowledge Thorn would not have) does not count. Thorn is ancient and widely read; obvious riddles will not stump him. If the PC's riddle is genuinely good, Thorn laughs — a real, delighted laugh — and concedes with pleasure.

- **Pass:** The party answers all three riddles correctly, or stumps Thorn with one of their own. Thorn claps his hands, the fire flares brighter, and he tells the party a fragment of the story: "The queen's father made something for her. A box that played music — the most beautiful tune in the Nine Worlds. It held the memory of who she was before the ice, before the witch. Khaga hid it, because she knew what it could do. But she did not destroy it. The fey cannot destroy a thing made with that much love. It is still in the tower where Sona grew up — the old tower on the far island. Khaga's sentinels guard it." He does not yet reveal the tower's exact location or his own identity.
- **Fail:** The party cannot answer all three riddles and cannot stump Thorn. He shrugs, not unkindly. "Perhaps the next test will suit you better." He names his favor: the party must carry a sealed acorn (which he produces from his cloak) and plant it in the first unfrozen soil they find. The acorn is a seed of a Diaspolian oak — one of the ancient trees that once grew in the grove around the Mother of Rivers. The favor is strange but harmless, and planting the acorn later is a small moment of hope that the GM can use as a narrative grace note.

**Test 3: The Test of Courage**

The final test requires no disguise and no subtlety. As the party moves deeper into the woods, the trees thin and the undergrowth grows denser, choked with frozen bramble and the splintered remains of trees that have been knocked down by something very large. Claw marks score the trunks of the surviving pines — deep gouges, higher than a man can reach, spaced apart in a pattern that suggests a creature with a stride longer than a horse. The forest is silent in the way that forests are silent when everything in them is hiding.

This is the territory of the creature the Halflings feared — the monster that drove them from the woods. It is a **winter-touched leucrotta** (see encounter below): a massive, cunning predator that Khaga placed here as a guardian to discourage anyone from seeking the Old Man. The leucrotta has claimed the deep woods as its hunting ground, and it has been killing anything that enters — deer, wolves, and at least two Halfling hunters who ventured too far from the tree line.

Pipkin does not appear for this test. There is no guise, no conversation, no riddle. The test is simply this: the creature is here, and it must be dealt with. The party can fight it, drive it off, or lure it out of the forest through clever planning. What matters is that they do not turn back.

> *The sound starts as a voice — a child's voice, high and frightened, calling for help from somewhere in the frozen undergrowth ahead. It is convincing. It would fool anyone who had not already learned, on this world or the one before it, that the things that sound like people in the wilderness are almost never people.*

**Encounter: The Winter Leucrotta**

The leucrotta is a natural predator warped by prolonged exposure to Diaspola's cursed cold and Khaga's fey magic. It is larger than a normal specimen, crusted with rime ice, and possesses the species' signature ability: perfect vocal mimicry. It uses this ability to lure prey — calling out in the voices of lost children, wounded animals, or crying Halflings. Unlike the phankhonvrani's imperfect sound mimicry, the leucrotta's imitation is flawless, including speech.

- **Enemy:** 1 **leucrotta** (Bestiary 2, CR 5) with the following winter template modifications: +2 natural armor (AC 18), cold resistance 10, vulnerability to fire, and a cold-infused bite that deals an additional 1d4 cold damage. Its lure ability (voice mimicry) functions as per the standard stat block — any creature that hears the lure must succeed on a Sense Motive check opposed by the leucrotta's Bluff +11 or believe it is hearing a real voice and is compelled to investigate.
- **Terrain:** Dense frozen forest. The trees provide cover but the undergrowth is difficult terrain (frozen bramble, 1 point piercing damage per round of movement). The leucrotta has knocked down several trees to create a killing ground — a 30-ft. clearing surrounded by dense cover where it can lure prey into the open while it attacks from concealment. A frozen stream cuts through the clearing, its surface slick (Acrobatics DC 12 to cross without falling prone).
- **Tactics:** The leucrotta is intelligent (Int 6 — animal cunning, not true intelligence) and fights with ambush discipline. It uses its lure to draw the party into the killing ground, then attacks from cover with a charge (+2 attack, 2d6+8 + 1d4 cold damage, powerful bite). If injured below half HP, it retreats into the dense forest and uses its lure again to reposition, attempting to isolate a single party member. It does not fight to the death — if reduced below 15 HP, it flees the forest entirely. Driving it off counts as passing the test.
- **Khaga's Pet:** The leucrotta wears a collar of woven silver wire — fey work, unmistakably Khaga's. The collar is not magical (Khaga does not waste enchantments on beasts) but it marks the creature as hers. Destroying or driving off the leucrotta will enrage Khaga when she learns of it — which she will, through her fey spy network. This has no immediate mechanical consequence, but it should be noted as a factor that may affect the party's approach to the ice palace: Khaga will be aware that someone has been in her woods and has killed her pet.

- **Pass:** The party defeats or drives off the leucrotta. They demonstrate the willingness to face a dangerous creature in hostile territory rather than turn back.
- **Fail:** The party retreats from the forest without confronting the leucrotta. This is the only test that cannot be failed gracefully — retreat is retreat, and Pipkin notes it.

**Pipkin Revealed**

If the party passes at least two of the three tests, Pipkin reveals himself. He steps out from behind a tree that the party is certain was empty a moment ago — a small figure, barely three feet tall, with nut-brown skin, sharp dark eyes, and a face that is simultaneously ancient and ageless. He wears a cloak of woven pine needles that shifts color with the light, and he carries a gnarled staff topped with an acorn carved from amber. He is Pipkin. He has been all three figures the party encountered.

> *"You'll do," he says. The voice is the same voice — or rather, it is the voice that was underneath all three voices, stripped of disguise. It is warm and dry and carries the weight of someone who has been paying attention for a very long time. "I have been waiting for heroes. I was not sure you were them. I am still not entirely sure." He looks at the party with an expression that is evaluating and hopeful in equal measure. "But you stopped for the woman. You matched wits with the hermit. You did not run from the beast. That is more than most manage."*

Pipkin then tells the party the full story — Sona's father, the music box, Khaga's manipulation, the tower on the far island — and provides precise directions to the tower's location across the frozen sea. He also reveals his own history: he was a member of the goodly fey king's court, a companion to Sona's father, and he watched Khaga's corruption unfold from the beginning. He tried to warn Sona. He failed. He has been living in these woods ever since, too ashamed to leave and too stubborn to die.

If the party mentions the music box, Pipkin's reaction is immediate and emotional. He remembers the tune. He will hum a few bars — badly, his voice cracking — and then stop. "If that box still plays, it will remind her. She needs to remember who she was. The music was always the center of that family. It was the last honest thing in her life before Khaga took everything else."

Pipkin provides the party with the following:
- The exact location of Sona's tower on the outer islands, and directions across the frozen sea.
- A warning about the fey sentinels Khaga stationed at the tower.
- A **ring of warmth** (functions as *endure elements* [cold only], continuous) — a gift freely given, which the party should accept graciously. "The sea is colder than the land. You will need this."
- His true name, given voluntarily — an extraordinary gesture of trust from a fey creature. "Pipkin. That is what her father called me. Use it if you need me. I will hear it." The party may invoke Pipkin's name once during the chapter for a minor intervention — he appears, provides a piece of critical information or a brief magical assist, and then vanishes. This is a narrative resource, not a combat tool.

**If the party fails two or more tests:** Pipkin does not reveal himself. The party leaves the forest without the full story, without directions to the tower, and without Pipkin's gifts. They can still locate the tower through the Halflings' oral tradition (Scene 2) and a longer sea crossing, but they will lack the precise navigation and the ring of warmth. The chapter is still completable — the music box path is harder, not closed. Alternatively, the party may pursue the Ember Circle approach (Scene 7) or attempt to reach Sona through one of the other methods described in Scene 6.

*GM note: Pipkin's tests are designed to reward good play without punishing failure catastrophically. A party that passes all three tests enters the sea crossing well-equipped and well-informed. A party that passes two is adequately prepared. A party that fails entirely has a harder road but is not locked out of the chapter's conclusion. The tests also serve a narrative function: they establish that the non-aligned fey are watching, that trust must be earned, and that the qualities that matter on Diaspola are compassion, wit, and courage — not raw power. These are Sona's qualities, the ones Khaga buried under centuries of fear, and the party demonstrates them in miniature before helping Sona reclaim them.*

---

### Scene 4: The Frozen Sea

The Ivory Music Box is hidden in Sona's old tower on one of the far islands of the frozen sea. If the party completed Pipkin's tests (Scene 3), they know the tower's location and the story of the music box. If they did not — or if they failed Pipkin's tests — they must find another way to locate the tower: the Halflings' oral tradition places it "on one of the outer islands," which is enough to begin a search but not enough to navigate directly. Without Pipkin's directions, the sea crossing takes an additional day as the party searches island to island (see Navigation below).

Reaching the tower requires crossing the frozen sea — a vast expanse of ice that stretches from the shore of the land side to the scattered islands that were once the retreats of heroes and adventurers from other worlds. The journey is dangerous: the ice is not uniform (pressure ridges, thin patches over thermal vents, crevasses hidden by blown snow), the cold is relentless, and the corrupted Fey patrol the sea as their domain.

**The Sea Crossing**

The crossing takes two days on foot across the frozen sea. The GM should run this as a journey with three elements: environmental hazard, navigation, and fey interference.

- **Environmental Hazard:** The ice is treacherous. Each day of travel, the lead navigator must succeed on a Survival check (DC 14) to identify safe routes. Failure means the party encounters one hazard: a thin ice patch (Reflex DC 15 or fall through into frigid water — 1d6 cold damage per round, Swim DC 15 to escape), a pressure ridge that adds hours to the journey, or a crevasse (Perception DC 16 to spot, 2d6 falling damage if missed). The cold continues to require Fortitude saves as described above.

- **Navigation:** If the party has Pipkin's directions, his instructions are good but rely on landmarks partially obscured by centuries of ice accumulation. A Knowledge (nature) or Survival check (DC 12) each day keeps the party on course. Failure adds half a day to the journey but does not cause the party to become lost. Without Pipkin's directions, the crossing takes three days instead of two as the party must search the outer islands. Each island requires a Perception or Survival check (DC 14) to determine whether it holds the tower; failure means the party moves on to the next island and loses several hours.

- **Fey Interference:** The corrupted Fey know the sea is their territory. On the first day, the party encounters a fey patrol — a group of ice mephits and a corrupted sprite acting as scouts. They are not immediately hostile; they approach the party with mockery and threats, attempting to provoke a response. If the party engages them with fey etiquette (demanding passage under the rules of honest travel, or offering a gift), the fey are bound to respond in kind. If the party attacks or ignores them, the fey harass the party for the remainder of the day, imposing disadvantage on Survival checks and attacking from range before retreating.

  On the second day, as the party nears the island, Khaga's patrols become more aggressive. A larger group — corrupted Fey backed by a **phankhonvrani** pack bound to fey service through a bargain (asuras despise the arrangement but are compelled to honor it) — attempts to turn the party back. This encounter should be a genuine fight. The Fey use the ice terrain to their advantage: luring the party onto thin ice, creating obscuring snow with minor magic, and sending the phankhonvrani in fast to strike and withdraw.

### Scene 5: The Tower and the Sentinels

Sona's old tower stands on a small island near the outer edge of the frozen sea, half-buried in accumulated ice. The island itself is perhaps a quarter mile across — rocky, windswept, and bare except for the tower and the stumps of trees that once grew around it. The tower is three stories tall, its upper floors exposed to the wind, its lower floor sealed behind a door of frozen wood that has not been opened in centuries.

The fey sentinels guarding the tower are Khaga's elite — four corrupted Fey of significant power, stationed here for the sole purpose of ensuring the Ivory Music Box is never recovered. They have been here for a very long time, and they are bored, spiteful, and dangerous.

> *The tower is beautiful in the way that all ruined things are beautiful when the ice gets hold of them — its stone walls sheathed in a crystalline glaze that catches the weak light and fractures it into pale rainbows, its upper windows dark and empty, its door sealed behind a curtain of frozen water that hangs from the lintel like the teeth of a massive jaw. The island around it is flat and bare. There is nowhere to hide, and four figures are waiting for you on the approach — seated on ice formations as though they are thrones, their posture communicating a total absence of concern.*

**Encounter: The Fey Sentinels**

- **Enemies:** 4 corrupted Fey sentinels. Use stat blocks appropriate for a challenging encounter for level 4 characters (the party has not yet reached level 5) — suggested: 2 advanced **atomies** (Bestiary 3, reskinned as ice fey with cold damage replacing electricity), 1 corrupted **dryad** (ice-adapted, Cha-based spell-like abilities focused on *hold person*, *charm person*, *sleet storm*), and 1 **aakarshaki** (CR 4; see Stat Block Appendix) that has been bound to serve the Fey through a bargain — it despises its situation and fights the party because it must, not because it wants to. The aakarshaki's Soothing Call is flavored as a lullaby, fitting the fairy-tale tone.
- **Terrain:** The approach to the tower is open ground across flat ice. The sentinels have prepared the area: patches of *grease* (pre-cast, covering two 10-ft. squares on the approach), hanging icicle formations rigged to fall (Perception DC 14 to spot, Reflex DC 14 to dodge, 2d6 piercing + 1d6 cold), and an illusory second door on the tower that leads to a 10-ft. pit concealed by snow (Perception DC 16 or Will DC 15 to disbelieve).
- **Tactics:** The sentinels do not fight as a unit — they are fey, not soldiers, and their approach is individualistic and cruel. The dryad focuses on disabling one PC at a time with enchantment effects. The atomies dart in and out of melee range, targeting spellcasters. The aakarshaki hangs back and uses its Soothing Call to fascinate PCs, then moves in with stingers on fascinated targets. If two sentinels are killed, the survivors attempt to destroy the music box (they know where it is inside the tower) rather than continue fighting. This should create urgency — the party must stop them from reaching the upper floor.
- **Fey Etiquette Opportunity:** The sentinels can be challenged through fey etiquette before combat begins. If a PC demands passage under the fey threshold rule (the tower is a dwelling, and the sentinels are outside it — they were never invited in by its owner), the sentinels are bound to acknowledge the claim. This does not prevent the fight — they will argue that the tower has been abandoned and therefore has no threshold — but it creates a delay during which the party can position itself, and it gives a PC who wins the argument (Diplomacy DC 18 or knowledge of fey law) a surprise round as the sentinels are momentarily bound to inaction.

**The Ivory Music Box**

Inside the tower, the upper floor is Sona's old study — preserved in ice as though flash-frozen, its contents visible through layers of frost. A desk, a chair, shelves of frozen books. And on the desk, a small wooden box inlaid with ivory, its lid slightly ajar, frost crystals growing from its hinges.

> *The music box is smaller than you expected. It fits in one hand — a wooden box of dark grain, inlaid with ivory in a pattern of snowflakes that are individually distinct, no two alike. The lid is slightly open, and inside, a figure: a dancer in a pale dress, her arms raised above her head, her face turned upward, poised at the beginning of a turn she has not completed in five hundred years. The box is cold. Everything here is cold. But when your fingers brush the mechanism beneath the figure, something resists — not ice, not rust, but the tension of a spring that is still wound, still waiting. The box works. It has been waiting to play.*

The party should not open the box here. Its purpose is to be played for Sona, and the GM should communicate — through instinct, through Pipkin's earlier warning, through a Knowledge (arcana) check (DC 12) — that the music box is a precision instrument of mythic power and should be used at the right moment. If the party opens it regardless, the melody plays — achingly beautiful, a simple tune that sounds like warmth — and nothing happens. The box needs to be played in Sona's presence to break Khaga's spell. Playing it elsewhere wastes nothing (the spring rewinds itself), but the party has telegraphed their possession of the lever to Khaga's spy network, and the fey will attempt to intercept them before they reach the Snow Queen.

**Milestone:** Level 5 is awarded after recovering the Ivory Music Box.

### Scene 6: The Snow Queen

Reaching Sona requires traveling to the ice palace on the far side of the land. The journey from the coast back to the interior can be narrated or played as the GM prefers — the threats are environmental and fey harassment, similar to the sea crossing but over frozen tundra rather than open ice. The ice palace itself is visible from miles away: a structure of impossible beauty, its walls and towers carved from solid ice that refracts the pale light into shifting prismatic patterns. It is enormous, cold beyond endurance, and surrounded by a retinue of corrupted Fey who serve as Sona's court.

The party cannot fight their way into the palace. The fey retinue is too numerous, and Khaga is present, and a direct assault on the Snow Queen's court would trigger a defensive response from Sona herself — she would interpret it as an attack and respond with mythic-tier ice magic that the party cannot survive at level 5. The party must find a way in through guile, fey etiquette, or opportunity.

**Four approaches the party might take (the GM should be prepared for others):**

1. **The Wild Hunt.** If the timing aligns, the party can join the procession of the Wild Hunt — Sona's annual ride through the frozen land — and use it to gain access to the palace. The Hunt is open to all; the Fey cannot refuse observers without violating their own traditions. Once inside the palace as "guests" of the Hunt, the party has access to Sona, though they are surrounded by corrupted Fey. Playing the music box during the Hunt's culminating feast is dramatic and effective — it interrupts the proceedings with a truth that Khaga cannot suppress.

2. **Fey Bargain.** A non-aligned Fey with access to the palace (Pipkin may know one, or the party may have encountered one during their travels) can arrange entry in exchange for a service or gift. The terms must be fair — fey bargains are binding on both parties — and the GM should ensure the cost is meaningful but not campaign-derailing.

3. **Direct Audience.** The party requests a formal audience with the Snow Queen. Khaga will attempt to prevent this, but if the party invokes the fey rule of honest parley (a guest who asks for audience with a ruler under their own name cannot be refused), Khaga is bound to arrange it — though she will stack the audience with conditions designed to prevent the party from reaching Sona with the music box. Weapons confiscated. Gifts inspected. Time limited.

4. **The Ember Circle (Trap).** If the party contacts the Halfling rebels in Frostmere, they will be offered a route into the palace through the old service tunnels. This is a trap — see Scene 7: The Ember Circle Trap. A party that survives the trap emerges inside the palace's lower levels and can fight their way up to Sona, though they will be battered and resource-depleted. This is the hardest approach and yields the least favorable positioning for the turning scene, but it is available as a path of last resort — particularly for a party that failed Pipkin's tests, could not recover the music box, or simply chose to approach the problem through force.

Regardless of the approach, the climactic moment is the same: the party plays the Ivory Music Box in Sona's presence. If the party does not have the music box, they must find another way to reach Sona — the Ember Circle path or a direct audience may bring them face to face with the Snow Queen, but without the lever, they cannot turn her. See "If the party fights Sona instead" below.

**The Turning**
