> *The first note is small. A single chime, clear and warm, as improbable in this palace of ice as a flower growing through a crack in stone. Then the melody begins — simple, unhurried, a tune that a father might play for a daughter on a winter evening, the kind of song that exists in the space between sleeping and waking. It fills the room. It fills the palace. And it fills the woman on the throne, who has gone very still.*
>
> *Sona's hands, which have been clenched in her lap for as long as anyone can remember, slowly open. Her eyes, which have been darting between Khaga and the shadows for five hundred years, fix on the music box and do not move. The ice in the room — the walls, the floor, the frozen chandelier above the throne — begins to weep. Not melt. Weep. Water runs down the surfaces in thin streams, and the air grows warmer by degrees, and the Snow Queen's lips part.*
>
> *"I know that song," she whispers. And then, louder, with a voice that has not carried conviction in centuries: "I know that song."*
>
> *She turns to look at Khaga. The Nixie witch is already moving — already gesturing to her retinue, already summoning the cold — but she is too late. The thing she kept asleep is awake, and it remembers everything. Sona Brightspring rises from her throne, and the ice palace groans. The walls crack. The ceiling shudders. And the Snow Queen, her eyes burning with a light that has nothing to do with ice and everything to do with the mythic power she has carried since she was young and brave and unbroken, speaks a single word.*
>
> *Khaga does not survive it.*

The GM should not roll dice for Sona's destruction of Khaga. This is a narrative event — the moment when a mythic-tier Warlock's full power is unleashed against the creature that imprisoned her. While Sona and Khaga's confrontation shakes the palace apart overhead, Khaga's retinue turns on the party in a desperate attempt to silence the music box before the spell fully breaks.

**Encounter: Khaga's Retinue**

- **Enemies:** 1 **hatyararata** (CR 3; see Stat Block Appendix) serving as Khaga's personal enforcer — the only non-fey creature in the retinue, bound to Khaga's service through a bargain that predates the freeze. 2 **quicklings** (Bestiary 2, CR 3 — reskinned as ice fey, blur effect from sheer speed, cold-infused short swords dealing an additional 1d4 cold damage). 4 **ice mephits** (Bestiary, CR 3) that peel off the walls and ceiling as the palace fractures, using breath weapons and *chill metal* to create chaos.
- **Objective:** The fey are trying to reach and destroy the music box — not to kill the party. Every enemy prioritizes the PC holding the box. If a fey reaches the box, it spends a full-round action attempting to destroy it (hardness 10, 15 hp — the box is imbued with Sona's residual mythic power and is tougher than it looks). The party must protect the box holder for 5 rounds while Sona finishes Khaga.
- **Tactics:** The hatyararata is the most dangerous element. It uses *invisibility* (pre-cast) and Shadow Double to bypass the party's front line and strike directly at the PC holding the music box, delivering sneak attack damage on the opening hit. The quicklings exploit their supernatural speed to dart past defenders and slash at the box holder from multiple angles — their blur effect makes them difficult to pin down. The ice mephits serve as interference, using breath weapons (15-ft. cone of cold shards, 1d4 cold + sickened 3 rounds, Reflex DC 12) and *chill metal* on armored PCs to impose penalties and slow movement. If a quickling reaches the box, it attempts to snatch it (CMB vs. CMD) and flee rather than destroy it in place.
- **Terrain:** The ice palace is collapsing. At initiative count 20 each round, a section of ceiling falls — the GM selects a random 10-ft. square within the throne room. Any creature in that square takes 3d6 bludgeoning + 1d6 cold damage (Reflex DC 15 half). The floor is slick ice (Acrobatics DC 10 to move at full speed, DC 15 to charge or run). Exits are blocked by fallen debris — the party cannot flee until the encounter ends. The shuddering of the palace as Sona and Khaga's mythic powers clash provides a visceral backdrop: walls cracking, frozen chandeliers swinging loose, the sound of ice splitting under forces the party can feel but not see.
- **Resolution:** After 5 rounds, Sona's power surges through the palace. Every corrupted fey in the building takes 8d6 damage (no save) as the mythic force that froze a world is redirected at the creatures who engineered the curse. Any surviving fey are destroyed. The hatyararata, not being fey, may survive this blast (Fortitude DC 20 or destroyed) — if it survives, it flees through the collapsing palace and is not encountered again. Its bargain with Khaga is broken by her death, and it has no further interest in the fight.

After Khaga's destruction, the ice begins to recede — not immediately, not all at once, but visibly. The temperature rises. The palace walls thin. Water runs in channels that have been dry for centuries. Sona stands in the ruin of her throne room, shaking, weeping, and free. She does not thank the party immediately. She is too overwhelmed. But she looks at them, and she looks at the music box, and the gratitude in her expression is profound enough to carry without words.

*GM note: Sona's turning is the campaign's template. Every element is present: the lever, the emotional breakthrough, the Warlock's power redirected against the source of corruption, the world beginning to heal. On subsequent worlds, these elements will be harder to achieve, more morally ambiguous, or outright denied by circumstance. Diaspola shows the party what turning looks like when everything goes right, so that they understand what they are fighting for when it goes wrong.*

**If the party fights Sona instead:** If the party arrives at the palace without the music box — or if they choose to fight the Snow Queen rather than free her — Sona is a mythic encounter scaled for level 5 characters. She fights with ice magic, fey-granted abilities, and the desperate ferocity of a woman who believes she is defending herself against invaders sent by the evil spirits. Killing her costs +1 corruption and deprives the party of a powerful ally for the Battle of Nexus. The ice on Diaspola persists, though it will slowly recede over decades without Sona's power sustaining it. This outcome should feel like a tragedy, not a victory.

### Scene 7: The Ember Circle Trap

This scene occurs only if the party travels to Frostmere and makes contact with the so-called Ember Circle — the group of young Halfling rebels that Bramble warned them about. The trap is Khaga's work: a carefully constructed lure designed to capture or kill anyone foolish enough to attempt a direct assault on the ice palace through its foundations.

**Frostmere**

Frostmere is a Halfling village two days' travel from Hearthhollow, closer to the ice palace and visibly more oppressed. The buildings are in worse repair, the population thinner and more fearful, and the fey presence is overt — corrupted sprites patrol the streets and a pair of ice mephits roost openly on the village's central well, demanding tribute from anyone who draws water. The Ember Circle operates out of a cellar beneath a disused granary on the village's eastern edge. Their leader calls himself **Kindle** — a young Halfling man with a fighter's build and a firebrand's rhetoric.

Kindle is not a Halfling. He is a **tripurasura** using Change Shape to assume Halfling form — the same infiltration tactic the party encountered on Nexus, deployed here with far greater sophistication. Two of Kindle's inner circle are also disguised tripurasuras. The remaining three members of the Ember Circle are genuine Halflings — young, angry, and completely unaware that they have been manipulated into serving as bait. Khaga created the Ember Circle specifically to attract and neutralize threats: anyone with the skill and motivation to challenge her regime is funneled into this group, where they can be monitored and, when the time is right, eliminated.

*GM note: The party has been warned. Bramble's suspicion in Scene 2 is an explicit signal that the Ember Circle is not what it appears to be. If the party ignores this warning, the consequences are earned. However, the trap should not be a death sentence — it is designed to capture, not kill, because Khaga prefers living prisoners she can interrogate and use as leverage.*

**The Pitch**

Kindle's pitch is compelling. He claims the Ember Circle has discovered a network of service tunnels beneath the ice palace — original construction passages that predate the freeze, used by the palace's builders and forgotten when the ice entombed the lower levels. He has a map (fabricated by the tripurasuras, though it looks old and authentic — Forgery check DC 18 to detect). He has a plan: the party enters the tunnels at night, navigates to the palace's lower levels, and fights their way up to the throne room while the Ember Circle creates a diversion at the gates. Quick, decisive, bold.

If the party examines the plan critically:
- A Knowledge (local) or Knowledge (engineering) check (DC 14) notes that the tunnel map shows passages that would require magical construction — natural tunnels of this configuration do not form beneath ice palaces.
- A Sense Motive check (DC 16) detects that Kindle's confidence is performative. He says the right things, but his body language does not carry the tension of someone planning a genuine assault on a fortified position. He is too relaxed. The genuine Halfling members of the Circle, by contrast, are visibly terrified — which is the appropriate response and should itself be a red flag.
- A Perception check (DC 20) or *detect magic* reveals faint transmutation auras on Kindle and two of his lieutenants — the residual signature of their Change Shape ability.

If the party confronts Kindle directly or detects the disguise, the three tripurasuras revert to their true forms and attack while attempting to flee. The genuine Halflings are horrified and do not fight — they drop their weapons and surrender immediately, begging the party to believe that they did not know. This encounter is brief and serves primarily to confirm the trap's nature. The party can then decide whether to proceed into the tunnels (now aware it is a trap) or abandon this approach.

**The Tunnels**

If the party enters the tunnels — whether deceived or aware of the trap — they find that the passages are real. Khaga did not fabricate the tunnels from nothing; she repurposed existing maintenance passages and extended them with fey magic, creating a route that leads directly into a prepared killing ground beneath the palace. The tunnels are narrow (5 ft. wide, single file), dark, and progressively colder. The walls are smooth ice, and the footing is treacherous (Acrobatics DC 10 to avoid slipping, DC 15 if moving faster than half speed).

After approximately thirty minutes of travel, the tunnels open into a large circular chamber — a natural ice cavern that Khaga has converted into a prison. The chamber is fifty feet in diameter, thirty feet high, and lit by bioluminescent ice formations that cast a pale blue-green light. The floor is smooth and slightly concave, sloping toward a drain at the center. The walls are lined with alcoves, some of which contain the frozen remains of previous victims — adventurers, Halfling rebels, and at least one fey dissident, all entombed in ice. Khaga has been using this room for a long time.

**Encounter: The Ice Chamber Ambush**

The moment the party enters the chamber, the tunnel behind them seals — an ice wall grows across the entrance in seconds (hardness 10, 60 hp, or bypassed with sustained fire damage). Simultaneously, concealed passages in the chamber walls open, and Khaga's ambush force emerges.

- **Enemies:** 2 **aakarshaki** (CR 4 each; see Stat Block Appendix) — their Soothing Call echoes in the enclosed chamber with devastating effect. 4 **tripurasuras** (CR 2 each) in their true forms, fighting from the alcoves and using the frozen remains as cover. 1 corrupted **dryad** (ice-adapted, CR 4 — the same type encountered at the tower sentinels) who coordinates the ambush and focuses on enchantment effects. The dryad carries a message from Khaga, delivered in the witch's voice through the dryad's mouth: "I knew you would come. Everyone comes. No one leaves."
- **Terrain:** The chamber is a prepared killing ground. The concave floor funnels movement toward the center drain, which is a 10-ft.-deep pit covered by a thin ice sheet (Perception DC 14 to spot, Reflex DC 14 to avoid falling in). The alcoves provide cover for the tripurasuras. The bioluminescent ice provides dim light throughout. The sealed entrance can be broken through (hardness 10, 60 hp) but doing so takes time the party may not have.
- **Tactics:** The aakarshaki open with Soothing Call simultaneously — two overlapping fascinate effects in an enclosed space. Any PC who fails is immediately targeted by the tripurasuras' poison stingers while helpless. The dryad uses *hold person* and *charm person* to disable the strongest-looking combatant. The ambush force fights to subdue, not kill — Khaga wants prisoners. Enemies that reduce a PC to 0 HP will stabilize them rather than deliver a coup de grace. If the party proves too strong for the ambush force, the dryad retreats through the concealed passages and seals them behind her, leaving the remaining asuras to fight to the death.

**Escape and Aftermath**

If the party defeats the ambush force, they can escape the chamber through the concealed passages the enemies used — these lead upward through the palace's foundations and emerge in the lower levels of the ice palace itself. The party is inside the palace, battered and resource-depleted, but alive and undetected (the ambush was meant to be quiet). From here they can navigate upward to the throne room and attempt to reach Sona — see Scene 6.

If the party is captured (all PCs reduced to 0 HP and stabilized), they awaken in ice cells in the palace's lower levels, stripped of weapons and equipment. Khaga visits them — not to gloat, but to assess. She is curious about who they are and why they came. The party must then escape the cells (a separate challenge involving fey etiquette, physical skill, or creative magic use) and recover their equipment before reaching Sona. This extends the chapter but does not end it — capture is a setback, not a conclusion.

*GM note: The Ember Circle trap is designed as a consequence for ignoring warnings, but it should not feel punitive. The party chose a risky approach, and the risk was real. The ambush is hard but survivable, and the reward — emerging inside the palace — is genuine. A party that walks into the trap knowingly (aware it is a trap but choosing to spring it on their own terms) should be rewarded for audacity: give them a surprise round against the ambush force if they prepared before entering the chamber. The trap is Khaga's best work, but it was designed for naive victims, not experienced adventurers who know what they are walking into.*

### Scene 8: The Mother of Rivers

With Khaga destroyed and Sona restored, the party can access the Mother of Rivers — the mythic place of power at the center of the land side. Sona accompanies them on the journey, which passes through territory that is already beginning to thaw — the ice thinner, the air warmer, the first sounds of running water audible beneath the frozen surface. But Sona's strength has its limits. The breaking of Khaga's spell and the exertion of destroying the Nixie witch have left her diminished, and as the grove comes into view, she stops.

The grove surrounding the Mother of Rivers is still cursed. Khaga's death has begun the slow process of its unraveling, but the curse was built to outlast its creator — a deliberate cruelty, designed to persist even if Khaga fell. The trees are frozen crystals — beautiful, deadly, and enchanted to sap the strength of anyone who enters. The curse intensifies deeper in.

> *The grove is visible from a distance — a ring of trees that catch the light and throw it back in shattered rainbows, as though someone took a forest and replaced every trunk and branch and leaf with glass. It is beautiful in the way that a trap is beautiful. The air around it is colder than the surrounding landscape, colder even than the worst of the frozen sea, and carries a sound that is not wind: voices, faint and distorted, layered on top of each other in a chorus that sounds like weeping and screaming and begging all compressed into a single sustained note.*

**Sona at the Threshold**

Sona cannot enter the grove. In her weakened state, the curse would drain her remaining strength in minutes — and without her maintaining the suppression from outside, the curse would reassert itself at full power on the party within. She stations herself at the grove's edge and channels her magic inward, holding back the worst of the curse's effects for as long as the party is inside.

> *Sona stops at the tree line. Her hands are shaking — from exhaustion, from cold, from the effort of holding herself together after centuries of imprisonment — but her voice is steady. "I can hold it back from here. Not all of it — the wardstones are too deep, and the curse is anchored to something older than Khaga's spite — but enough that you will not collapse before you reach the center." She presses her palms together, and a faint warmth radiates outward from her body, pushing against the grove's malice like a hand holding back a door. "Go. Quickly. What strength I have will not last forever."*

*GM note: Sona's suppression reduces the grove's Strength damage from 1d2 per round to 1 point per minute — survivable, but the party should feel the pressure. If the party dawdles, the damage accumulates. If the party takes longer than 30 minutes inside the grove, Sona's suppression begins to falter: the damage increases to 1d2 per round again, and Sona calls out a warning from the threshold. This creates a soft time limit without a hard timer — the party should move with purpose but is not racing a clock. If Sona was killed rather than turned, no suppression is available. The curse operates at full strength: 1d2 Strength damage per round, Fortitude DC 16 halves. The grove is still navigable — the wardstone puzzle provides safe zones — but the journey is considerably more punishing.*

**The Imprisoned Fey**

The crystal trees are not empty. The Fey who opposed Khaga — those who refused to participate in Sona's manipulation, who tried to warn the Halflings, who resisted the corrupted court — were imprisoned here as punishment and warning. Their spirits are bound within the crystalline trunks, visible as shadows and suggestions of form beneath the ice: a face pressed against the interior of a frozen trunk, fingers splayed beneath the surface of a branch, a mouth open in a scream that has lasted centuries.

The imprisoned Fey are aware. They cannot speak coherently — the curse distorts their voices into the moaning and whispering the party has heard since entering the grove — but their suffering is unmistakable. As the party moves deeper, the imprisonment becomes more horrific. Closer to the grove's center, the trees are larger and the Fey within them more visible: whole bodies suspended in crystal, their expressions frozen between agony and despair, their eyes tracking the party's movement. Some manage fragments of speech that break through the distortion — a word here, a name there, enough to communicate that these are people, not decorations.

> *The tree before you is larger than the others — an ancient thing, its trunk six feet across, its surface so clear that the figure inside is visible in terrible detail. A woman. Fey, unmistakably — the pointed ears, the elongated features, the skin that should be the color of spring leaves but is instead the blue-white of deep ice. Her hands are pressed flat against the interior of the crystal as though she died trying to push her way out. Her mouth is open. The sound that comes from the tree is not a voice anymore — it is a vibration, a frequency that your bones interpret as grief before your ears register it as sound.*

*GM note: The imprisoned Fey should make the party angry. Not frightened — angry. This is cruelty on a scale that goes beyond tactical evil. Khaga did not kill her enemies. She made them watch their world freeze from inside glass coffins, unable to move, unable to die, unable to do anything except suffer and serve as a reminder of what happens to those who resist. By the time the party reaches the first wardstone, they should want to break this curse as a matter of principle, not just necessity. The emotional investment in freeing the Fey makes the place of power activation — and the breaking of the curse — feel earned.*

**The Frozen Keys — Puzzle**

Khaga's curse is anchored by three frozen wardstones placed at intervals along the path to the center of the grove. Each wardstone is a pillar of dark ice roughly four feet tall, carved with Sylvan script that glows faintly with a cold blue light. The wardstones are the structural supports of the curse — they channel and direct its power. Each wardstone can be deactivated by solving a riddle carved into its surface, which suppresses the curse in a radius around the stone and creates a safe zone where the Strength drain ceases. The party moves from wardstone to wardstone, expanding the safe path toward the spring at the center.

**Wardstone 1: The Outer Ring**

The first wardstone stands at the grove's outer edge, roughly fifty feet from the tree line. The Sylvan inscription reads:

> *I am born in the mountains and die in the sea. I carry life but cannot breathe. I carve the stone but hold no blade. I am patient beyond all measure. What am I?*

**Answer:** Water (or river, stream, or any equivalent). A Linguistics check (DC 12) translates the Sylvan if no party member speaks it; Sona can translate from outside the grove if asked. The answer is thematically tied to the Mother of Rivers — the curse mocks the very thing it has frozen.

*If answered correctly:* The wardstone cracks and dims. The curse lifts in a 30-ft. radius around the stone. The nearest crystal trees in this radius begin to weep — not melting, but leaking thin streams of water from hairline fractures. The imprisoned Fey within them stir. Their expressions do not change, but the vibration of their suffering shifts — something in it eases, the way a held breath eases when the pressure behind it lessens. The safe zone persists for as long as the party remains on this level of the grove.

*If answered incorrectly:* The wardstone discharges a burst of curse energy. All creatures within 15 ft. take 2d6 cold damage (Fortitude DC 16 half) and 1d4 Strength damage (same save). The wardstone resets and can be attempted again after 1 minute. Only one incorrect answer per wardstone triggers the discharge — subsequent wrong answers produce no effect, giving the party time to think.

**Wardstone 2: The Middle Ring**

Deeper in the grove, the crystal trees grow denser and the imprisoned Fey more numerous. The second wardstone is flanked by two massive crystal trunks containing Fey warriors — their weapons still in their hands, their armor fused to the ice, their faces set in expressions of defiant rage. They were fighting when Khaga froze them. The inscription reads:

> *I am strongest when I am given, weakest when I am taken. Kings and beggars carry me in equal measure. I cannot be bought, though many have tried. What am I?*

**Answer:** Trust (or faith, loyalty, or similar). This riddle speaks to the core of Sona's story — her trust was exploited by Khaga, and these warriors' trust in Sona was rewarded with imprisonment when she could not protect them.

*If answered correctly:* The wardstone deactivates. The safe zone expands. In this ring, the crystal trees crack more deeply. Through the fractures, the imprisoned Fey manage coherent speech for the first time — brief, strained, overlapping:

> *"The spring... still flows beneath..."*
> *"...she put something in the water... a guardian..."*
> *"...break the last stone and we are free..."*

The fragments warn the party that something guards the center of the grove — a detail that should put them on alert before they reach the final wardstone.

*If answered incorrectly:* Same discharge effect as Wardstone 1.

**Wardstone 3: The Inner Ring**

The final wardstone stands at the edge of the central clearing where the spring lies frozen. The crystal trees here are the oldest and the largest, and the Fey within them are not warriors but elders — leaders of the resistance, the most powerful of those who defied Khaga. Their imprisonment is the most complete: they are fully encased, their forms barely distinguishable from the crystal that holds them, as though the curse has been slowly absorbing them over centuries. The inscription reads:

> *I am the wound that heals the body. I am the fire that clears the field. I am the word that ends the silence. I am cruel, and I am kind, and I am necessary. What am I?*

**Answer:** Truth. The answer that encompasses the chapter's theme — the truth of what Khaga did to Sona, the truth that the music box restored, the truth that the party has carried through the grove and now brings to the heart of the curse.

*If answered correctly:* The final wardstone shatters. The safe zone reaches the center of the grove. The crystal trees throughout the grove fracture simultaneously — a sound like a thousand windows breaking at once — and the imprisoned Fey within them gasp as the curse's hold loosens. They are not yet free — the curse will not fully break until the place of power activates — but they can feel it weakening, and the chorus of distorted voices changes to something recognizable as hope.

*If answered incorrectly:* Same discharge effect. Additionally, the discharge at this depth triggers the grove's final defense (see the encounter below), beginning the combat while the wardstone resets.

*GM note: The riddles should be solvable through reasoning, not obscure knowledge checks. If the party struggles, the imprisoned Fey can provide hints through their fragmentary speech — they have been listening to the wardstones for centuries and know the answers, though the curse prevents them from speaking clearly enough to simply state them. A PC who presses an ear to a crystal tree and listens (Perception DC 14) catches a whispered syllable or two that points toward the answer. Sona, from outside the grove, may also sense the riddles' themes through her suppression link and call out a guiding phrase — "It is about what Khaga took from me" for the second riddle, for example. The puzzle should challenge the party, not block them.*

**Encounter: The Crystal Guardians**

When the party solves the third wardstone — or triggers its discharge through an incorrect answer — the grove's final defense activates. The curse, sensing that its structure is failing, animates its most terrible weapons: the imprisoned Fey themselves.

Three of the largest crystal trees in the inner ring shatter outward, and from within them step figures of translucent ice in the shape of the Fey they once held. These are not the freed spirits — they are constructs animated by the curse, wearing the forms of the imprisoned like armor. The real Fey remain trapped within, their spirits powering the constructs involuntarily. The party is fighting the very beings they are trying to save, and the constructs' movements carry echoes of the warriors they were — a sword stance, a dodge, a feint that speaks of muscle memory the curse has stolen and repurposed.

> *The tree splits open with a sound like a bone breaking, and the figure that steps out is beautiful and wrong. It has the shape of a Fey warrior — tall, armored, a crystalline blade forming in its hand as it moves — but its eyes are empty and its movements are those of a puppet, precise without intention, graceful without thought. The face is a Fey woman's face, frozen in an expression of horror, and as the construct raises its sword, the mouth moves. The word it speaks is not a battle cry. It is "please."*

- **Enemies:** 3 **crystal guardians**. Use the stat block for a Medium ice elemental (Bestiary, CR 4) with the following modifications: +2 natural armor (AC 19), cold aura (5 ft., 1d4 cold damage to adjacent creatures at the start of the guardian's turn), and vulnerability to fire. The guardians wield crystalline weapons that deal 1d8+4 slashing + 1d4 cold damage. They have DR 5/bludgeoning (the crystal is hard but brittle — blunt weapons crack it faster than blades).
- **Terrain:** The inner ring of the grove. The deactivated wardstones provide solid cover. The ground is slick with ice melt from the cracking trees. The frozen spring is 20 ft. behind the guardians; the rune circle around it is visible through the thinning ice dome. Crystal shards from the shattered trees litter the ground (difficult terrain in a 10-ft. radius around each tree stump).
- **Tactics:** The guardians fight defensively, positioning themselves between the party and the spring. They do not pursue retreating PCs beyond the inner ring — their purpose is to guard the center, not to hunt. They focus attacks on the nearest threats and do not coordinate tactically (the curse is a blunt instrument, not a strategist). If two guardians are destroyed, the third fights with renewed ferocity — the curse pours its remaining power into the last vessel, granting it +2 to attack rolls and damage and fast healing 2.
- **The Imprisoned Spirits:** When a guardian is destroyed, the Fey spirit within it is released — not yet fully freed (the curse still holds them to the grove until the place of power breaks it), but separated from the construct. The spirit manifests briefly as a translucent figure that looks at the party with gratitude and grief before fading back into the nearest crystal tree to wait. A PC who delivers the killing blow on a guardian hears the Fey spirit's voice clearly for the first time — a single sentence, spoken without distortion: *"Thank you. Finish it."*
- **GM note:** This encounter should feel bittersweet. The guardians are not villains — they are victims being used as weapons. Players who find ways to destroy the constructs without harming the spirits within (fire damage to crack the crystal shell, *dispel magic* to sever the curse's animation, or simply overwhelming the construct with force) should be rewarded narratively with clearer expressions of gratitude from the freed spirits. The emotional texture of fighting someone's prison while they beg you to keep going is the point. Do not soften it.

**The Spring**

With the guardians defeated and the wardstones broken, the party reaches the center of the grove. The frozen spring lies beneath a dome of ice that is already cracking — thin fractures spreading across its surface, water beading along the fissures, the blue glow of the spring beneath growing brighter as the curse loses its grip.

> *The spring is a miracle even frozen. The dome of ice above it is translucent, and through it you can see the water — impossibly blue, impossibly clear, moving in slow currents beneath the surface as though the spring never stopped flowing, as though it has been waiting, patient and quiet, for someone to let it out.*

At the spring, the place of power activates. The mechanism is similar to the Ancillary — a rune circle, older than the one the party used on Nexus, carved into the stone around the spring's basin. When the party steps into the circle, the mythic power of the world responds. The ice dome above the spring cracks and shatters. The water surges upward. And the curse breaks.

The effect is immediate and total. Every crystal tree in the grove fractures and dissolves, the ice running off in streams of clear water that flow toward the spring as though drawn by a force older than gravity. The imprisoned Fey — dozens of them, held for centuries — emerge from the ruins of their crystal prisons. They are weakened, disoriented, and profoundly changed by their ordeal, but they are alive. Some weep. Some stand in silence, looking at the sky as though they have forgotten what it looks like. Some turn toward the party and simply bow — a gesture that crosses every cultural line and needs no translation.

The party receives: mythic tier 2 and the second fragment of the Song of Heaven.

> *The Song comes to you the way the first fragment did — not learned, but remembered, as though it was always there and you simply did not know how to hear it. The new fragment fits against the first like a key turning in a lock. Two notes of a melody that is not yet complete, but already more beautiful than either piece alone. The water of the Mother of Rivers rises around you, warm and clean, and the freed Fey stand among the ruins of their prisons, their voices — their real voices, clear and unbroken for the first time in five hundred years — rising in a sound that is not quite a song and not quite a prayer but is, unmistakably, joy.*

### Chapter Conclusion

**World State (Turned):** The ice is receding. It will take years to fully thaw, but the process has begun and is irreversible. Sona takes responsibility for the rebuilding, supported by the freed non-aligned Fey and the Halfling communities. She is shaken but determined. The corrupted Fey are scattered — those that Sona and the PCs did not destroy have fled to the edges of the world, where they will diminish without Khaga's leadership. Sona is available as an ally in Phase 3 and will bring a contingent of Fey warriors and Halfling scouts to the Battle of Nexus. She can also provide information about the other Warlocks — she knew them all as companions, and while her knowledge is five centuries out of date, she can point the party toward levers and vulnerabilities on subsequent worlds.

**World State (Killed):** The ice persists. Without Sona's power to sustain the freeze, it will slowly recede over decades, but the Halfling communities face years of continued hardship. The non-aligned Fey attempt to rebuild, but without Sona's authority, factions among the corrupted Fey regroup and contest control. Diaspola's contribution to the Battle of Nexus is minimal — a small group of Halfling scouts, no Fey support. The world is not destroyed, but it is damaged in a way that will take generations to heal.

**Transition:** The party returns to Nexus via the Rainbow Road. The gate town is different when they arrive — the residents have noticed the light in the terminus, and word has spread. Maranthe meets them at the citadel. She is relieved to see them, asks for a full report, and provides resupply for the next world. If Sona was turned, the news from Diaspola reaches Nexus within days and produces a visible lift in morale — the first good news the city has received in living memory. The party rests, prepares, and departs for the Tanis gate town.

**Milestone:** Level 6 is awarded at the conclusion of this chapter. Mythic Tier 2 was granted earlier at the Mother of Rivers (Scene 8).

---

## Chapter 5: Tanis — The Wound That Will Not Heal *(Levels 7–8, Mythic Tier 3)*

### Chapter Summary

Tanis is the second world the party visits and the darkest they have encountered. Where Diaspola was a fairy tale twisted by cruelty, Tanis is a tragedy in progress — a world dying by inches from a wound that cannot be healed. The Contamination, a spreading zone of mutated flora, corrupted fauna, and infectious fungal spores, covers the majority of the cavern floor and originates from the body of the Warlock Garl the Even-Handed, a Forest Gnome druid whose unhealable wound has been poisoning his homeland for five centuries. The Dwarven population has retreated to desperate wall-cities carved into the cavern's stone, and the Forest Gnome druids who fight to hold the Contamination back are losing ground every year.

The turning mechanic on Tanis is unique among the eight worlds. Garl's lever is a wondrous figurine token containing the spirit of Rerox, his grizzly bear animal companion — hidden in his childhood home deep within the Contamination zone. Presenting the figurine does not heal Garl or free him from corruption. It pierces the fog of madness long enough for him to understand what every healer who has tried to save him could not bring themselves to say: his wound is incurable. He renounces his mythic power and asks the party to kill him. Turning Garl means granting him the mercy of death. The Contamination begins to wither when he dies.

This chapter should feel like a slow descent into suffering followed by a moment of terrible clarity. The tone is somber, the stakes are personal rather than political, and the emotional weight comes not from a villain's cruelty but from the helplessness of watching a good person destroyed by something no one can fix. Garl is not an antagonist. He is a victim. The party's task is to end his suffering — if they can find the means to make him understand that ending it is the right thing to do.

### GM Guidance

**Tone:** Tanis is the campaign's first sustained encounter with tragedy that has no clean resolution. Diaspola offered liberation — Sona freed, the ice receding, a fairy-tale ending earned through cleverness and courage. Tanis offers mercy at best and violence at worst. The GM should lean into this tonal shift. The Dwarves are not oppressed subjects awaiting rescue; they are survivors of a slow apocalypse who have adapted to misery and expect nothing better. The Gnome druids are not rebels with a plan; they are healers fighting a disease they cannot cure. No one on Tanis is waiting for heroes — they have had heroesm and the heroes died.

**The Contamination as environment:** The Contamination should be an oppressive, constant presence from the moment the party arrives. It is not a monster to be fought — it is a condition of the world. Use environmental descriptions to convey its scope: the smell of rot beneath the fungal sweetness, the way bioluminescent growths pulse in rhythms that almost resemble breathing, the distant sound of the Howler's screams echoing through the cavern. The mechanical hazards (spore saves, difficult terrain, visibility) reinforce the narrative weight, but the real threat is psychological — the Contamination is everywhere, it is getting worse, and nothing the party does short of reaching Garl will slow it.

**Garl's screams:** The Howler's screams are audible throughout the cavern — not constantly, but at irregular intervals that make them impossible to anticipate or ignore. The first time the party hears one, give it a full read-aloud. After that, mention the screams in passing during scene transitions and travel sequences, as ambient texture. The sound should become a drumbeat through the chapter — always present, always painful, always reminding the party of what waits at the ziggurat.

**Demons on Tanis:** Tornsoul's demons are present but operating differently than on Diaspola. The Contamination is already doing Tornsoul's work — the world is destroying itself without external intervention. A small number of demons have embedded themselves in the Contamination zone, feeding on the suffering and ensuring that anyone who gets close to understanding Garl's condition is killed or driven away before they can act on that understanding. They are saboteurs, not conquerors.

**Leveling:** The party enters at level 6, mythic tier 2. Level 7 is awarded after recovering the Rerox figurine from Garl's childhood home (Scene 5). Level 8 and mythic tier 3 are awarded at the chapter's conclusion after activating Gaia's Navel (Scene 8).

---

### Scene 1: The Gate Town and the Dark Threshold

The Tanis gate town on Nexus is the most visibly distressed of the eight. Its buildings are reinforced with heavy stone — Dwarven construction, practical and graceless, built to withstand siege rather than to welcome visitors. The terminus arch is dark. The residents who remain here are Dwarven refugees who fled Tanis generations ago and have no desire to return. They regard the party with a mixture of pity and grim respect.

> *The gate town is quiet in the way that places of mourning are quiet — not empty, but subdued, as though the buildings themselves have agreed to keep their voices down. The Dwarves who live here are stocky, stone-faced people who watch you pass with expressions that contain no curiosity and no hope. One of them, an old woman sitting on a bench outside the provisioner's shop, catches your eye and shakes her head slowly, as though you have already made a mistake she cannot correct.*

The party performs the Song of Heaven at the terminus — two fragments now, the melody longer and more complex than the single note that opened the road to Diaspola. The arch activates. The Rainbow Road unfurls into darkness.

The transit to Tanis is markedly different from the journey to Diaspola. Where the road to the frozen world was bright and cold, the road to Tanis descends. The colors dim. The light takes on a greenish cast. The air grows heavy and warm and carries a sweetness that is not pleasant — organic, fungal, the smell of things growing where they should not. The road deposits the party at the base of the cavern wall, on a narrow ledge overlooking a vast darkness punctuated by distant points of bioluminescent light.

> *The road ends and the world opens below you — or tries to. You are standing on a stone ledge, wide enough for comfort, set into a wall that curves away in both directions and upward into shadow. Far above, a narrow opening admits a sliver of grey sky, but the sun is not there now and may not be for hours. Below you, the cavern floor stretches outward in every direction, and what you can see of it is wrong. The darkness is not empty — it pulses with a faint green-gold light, organic and rhythmic, like the glow of something alive. The air is warm, humid, and sweet in a way that coats the back of your throat. And somewhere, far away, echoing off the cavern walls until the sound comes from everywhere at once, something is screaming.*

*GM note: The scream is the Howler — Garl, in one of his episodes of agony. The party cannot identify the source or nature of the sound at this point. It lasts for roughly thirty seconds before cutting off, leaving a silence that is worse.*

The ledge connects to a carved stairway descending the cavern wall toward the nearest wall-city, Ironvault, visible as a cluster of lantern-light and stonework approximately two hundred feet below. The descent takes roughly an hour and is not dangerous — the stairway is well-maintained, with iron railings and periodic rest platforms, clearly the work of Dwarven engineers who expected heavy traffic.

---

### Scene 2: Ironvault

Ironvault is a wall-city — a settlement carved into the cavern wall itself, constructed in horizontal tiers connected by ramps, ladders, and counterweighted lifts. It is home to approximately three thousand Dwarves and a few hundred Forest Gnomes. The city is functional, orderly, and profoundly cheerless. Every surface is stone or iron. Mining tunnels bore deep into the rock in every direction — some still active, the rhythmic clang of picks and the groan of ore carts audible through the walls at all hours, others sealed and repurposed as ventilation shafts that cycle air through magitek filtration engines. The filtration is imperfect; the faint sweetness of the Contamination seeps through despite the engineers' best efforts, and the outermost tiers are fitted with heavy iron shutters that can be sealed against spore surges — and are, regularly. Forgeworks occupy the lower tiers, their heat and noise channeled downward through exhaust shafts to avoid fouling the residential levels above. The forges produce weapons, tools, structural iron for the endless expansion of the tunnels, and replacement parts for the magitek machinery that keeps the city alive — water purifiers, air scrubbers, the great lift mechanisms that move ore and people between tiers. Ironvault is a city that builds itself deeper every year, because every year the world outside gives it one more reason to retreat.

Ironvault is also the oldest of the wall-cities, and it was not built by accident. Its position on the cavern wall, directly below the Rainbow Road's arrival point, is deliberate — it was founded in the earliest years after the war as the designated destination for inter-world travelers, a waystation and supply point for those who came to Tanis via Nexus. The city's records preserve this history, and its people retain an institutional memory of a time when the Rainbow Road was active and visitors from other worlds were, if not common, at least expected. That memory has been dormant for five centuries. The party's arrival reawakens it.

The reaction in Ironvault is complex. There is genuine excitement — the elders recognize what the party's arrival means, and word spreads through the tiers within hours. Dwarves who have spent their entire lives hearing stories about the Road and the heroes who once walked it crowd the ramps and lift platforms to see the newcomers. But there is suspicion beneath the excitement, deep-rooted and earned. Ironvault has survived by expecting nothing from the outside world. Every generation has produced someone who claimed to have a solution to the Contamination, and every generation has buried those claims along with the people who made them. The city's leaders convene to meet the party, and the welcome they offer is real — but it is the welcome of people who have learned to keep their hope at arm's length.

**Key NPCs:**

**Forge-Elder Dagna Deepdelve** — The leader of Ironvault's governing council. A Dwarven woman of advanced age, heavyset, with iron-grey hair cropped close and hands scarred from decades of forge work. She is practical, blunt, and deeply skeptical of outsiders promising solutions — she has heard too many promises from too many would-be healers who walked the Healer's Road and never returned. She will cooperate with the party if they demonstrate competence, but she will not invest hope. She provides the party with information about the Contamination's spread, the locations of other surviving communities, and the general layout of the cavern floor. She knows the Healer's Road and can describe its route, but she will not send any of her people to escort the party along it.

> *"Every generation, someone comes along who thinks they can fix it. They walk the Road. They don't come back. The world gets a little smaller. We drill a little deeper. That's how it works here. You're welcome to try. I'd rather you didn't — I'd rather have a few more capable fighters on the wall than a few more corpses on the Road — but I can see you've already decided. So I'll tell you what I know, and you'll tell me when you're ready, and I'll make sure the gate is open when you leave."*

**Root-Keeper Fennel** — The leader of the Forest Gnome druid circle that operates out of Ironvault. A small, wiry Gnome woman with moss-green hair streaked grey and skin weathered to the texture of bark. She is quieter than Dagna, more patient, and far more willing to believe the party might succeed — not because she is naive, but because the druidic tradition on Tanis remembers Garl as he was, and Fennel has never fully surrendered the belief that the person he was still exists somewhere inside the Howler. She can tell the party about Garl's history: his gentleness, his bond with Rerox, his friendship with the Gnomish druids, and the slow horror of watching the wound consume him. She is the party's best source of information about the lever, though she does not know the figurine's location — only that Garl once had a bear companion he loved, that the bear was gravely wounded in the war, and that "someone brought something of the bear's to the old places, but no one remembers who or where."

> *"He was the best of them, you know. The ten who walked the Road. The others were brave, or clever, or strong, but Garl was kind. That was his gift and it was the thing the wound took first. The kindness went, and then the clarity, and then everything else. What's left is pain and the memory of pain, and he can't tell the difference between the two anymore."*

**The Situation:** The party learns the following from Dagna, Fennel, and the general population of Ironvault:

- The Contamination covers roughly seventy percent of the cavern floor and is advancing at a rate of approximately one mile per year. At this rate, the surviving wall-cities have perhaps a century before they are engulfed — less if the rate accelerates, which it has been doing.
- The Warlock — known as the Howler — resides at the central ziggurat. He is in constant agony from an unhealable wound and has been for five centuries. His screams echo through the cavern at irregular intervals.
- The Healer's Road is the only maintained path through the Contamination to the ziggurat. It is kept clear by the Warlock's will — a remnant of his desire to be healed, manifested through his corrupted connection to the land. The Road is navigable but not safe; the journey takes approximately six days on foot.
- Twisted agents emerge from the Contamination periodically and travel to the wall-cities, requesting healers. If a healer volunteers, they are escorted to the ziggurat. If no one volunteers, the agent attacks in a berserk rage until killed. The communities have learned to keep a rotation of volunteer healers ready — sacrificial lambs sent to buy time. None have returned.
- The Forest Gnome druids maintain wards around the wall-cities that slow the Contamination's encroachment, but these wards require constant maintenance and the druids are aging faster than they can train replacements.
- Garl's childhood home is deep in the Contamination zone — not on the Healer's Road but in the opposite direction, in a region that was once a Forest Gnome village called Mosshollow. No one has visited Mosshollow in over two centuries. Fennel can provide rough directions but warns that the Contamination is thickest in the oldest settled areas.
- A druid expedition — three Gnomish druids led by a young druid named Thistledown — ventured into the Contamination two weeks ago to attempt a purification ritual at an old grove site. They have not returned. Fennel is increasingly worried but cannot spare anyone to search for them.

*GM note: The missing druid expedition is the chapter's side quest. The party can pursue it before or after recovering the figurine — the druids are alive but trapped, and rescuing them earns the party additional ally points and provides Fennel's circle with critical intelligence about the Contamination's inner regions. The quest is detailed in Scene 4.*

---

### Scene 3: The Contamination

The Contamination is both the dominant feature of Tanis and the primary environmental hazard of this chapter. Any journey across the cavern floor outside the wall-cities and the Healer's Road passes through it.

**Environmental Rules:**

**Visibility:** The Contamination reduces visibility to 60 feet in lighter areas (the outer fringe, within a mile of the wall-cities) and 30 feet in heavier areas (the deep interior, anywhere within five miles of the ziggurat). Bioluminescent growths provide dim light throughout, casting everything in a shifting green-gold pallor. Darkvision functions normally but does not penetrate the dense spore clouds that drift through the deeper zones.

**Spore Exposure:** Any creature that breathes the air in the Contamination zone must make a Fortitude save every hour (DC 14 in the outer fringe, DC 17 in the deep interior). Failure results in 1 point of Constitution damage and the *sickened* condition for 1 hour. A creature that accumulates 4 or more points of Constitution damage from spore exposure begins to exhibit fungal growths on its skin — cosmetic at first, but a clear warning. A creature reduced to 0 Constitution by spore exposure dies and rises 1d4 hours later as a fungal zombie (use the zombie template with the addition of the spore cloud ability described below). *Delay poison* suppresses the saves for its duration. A cloth mask soaked in alcohol or vinegar grants a +2 circumstance bonus. The Gnomish druids provide the party with herbal poultices that grant a +4 alchemical bonus to these saves — enough for six days of travel.

**Terrain:** The Contamination zone is difficult terrain. The ground is covered in a mat of fungal growth — spongy, uneven, and occasionally concealing sinkholes where the substrate has rotted away beneath. A creature that moves at more than half speed must make a Reflex save (DC 12) each round or fall prone. The fungal mat also muffles sound — Perception checks based on hearing take a −4 penalty within the Contamination.

**Fungal Zombies:** The Contamination's most dangerous products. These are former inhabitants of Tanis — Dwarves, Gnomes, and animals — whose bodies have been consumed and reanimated by the spore infection. Use the standard zombie template with the following addition: *Spore Burst (Ex)* — When a fungal zombie is destroyed, it releases a cloud of spores in a 10-foot radius. All creatures in the area must make a Fortitude save (DC 14) or suffer the effects of one failed spore exposure save (1 Constitution damage + sickened 1 hour). Fungal zombies wander the Contamination zone in groups of 2–6 and are drawn to sound and movement. They are not controlled by Garl — they are a byproduct of his condition, mindless and hostile to all living things.

**Mutated Creatures:** The Contamination has warped the native fauna of Tanis into aggressive, misshapen forms. These creatures retain their basic instincts but are larger, more aggressive, and often display fungal growths that grant them additional abilities. For random encounters in the Contamination zone, use the following table (roll 1d6 once per four hours of travel):

| d6 | Encounter |
|---|---|
| 1–2 | No encounter |
| 3 | 1d4+1 fungal zombies (mixed Dwarf and animal) |
| 4 | 1 mutated cave bear (use dire bear with the advanced template and spore burst on death) |
| 5 | Spore surge — dense cloud, all saves at +2 DC for the next hour, visibility halved |
| 6 | 1 janavariseng lurking in the fungal canopy (see Demons on Tanis, below) |

**Demons on Tanis:** Tornsoul has embedded a small force of demons in the deep Contamination zone. Their purpose is not conquest — the Contamination does that work already — but sabotage. They target anyone who enters the interior with apparent purpose: healers on the Road, druids attempting rituals, and now the party. The primary types present are **janavariseng** (CR 6), whose brute strength and ambush tactics are well suited to the cluttered, low-visibility terrain, and a single **mautchidaya** (CR 8) that serves as the cell's coordinator, using its flight and diving strikes to harry targets and its pinning gaze to isolate individuals for the janavariseng to finish. The party will encounter these demons in Scenes 4, 5, and 6.

---

### Scene 4: The Missing Druids *(Side Quest)*

The party can pursue the missing druid expedition at any point after learning of their disappearance in Scene 2. The druids ventured to an old grove site called the Verdant Ring, located approximately fifteen miles from Ironvault in the outer Contamination zone. Fennel can provide directions. The journey takes roughly eight hours through the Contamination (use the environmental rules and random encounter table from Scene 3).

**The Verdant Ring**

The grove was once a sacred site for the Forest Gnome druids — a ring of ancient trees surrounding a natural spring. The trees are dead now, reduced to fungal-encrusted pillars, and the spring is choked with grey-green growth. But the druidic wards carved into the trees' root systems still hold a faint charge, and it was this residual energy that drew Thistledown's expedition here — the theory was that recharging the wards could create an expanded zone of purity around the grove, proving the concept for a larger network of purified zones.

The expedition partially succeeded. The three druids — **Thistledown** (female Forest Gnome, young, determined, slightly reckless), **Old Bracken** (male Forest Gnome, elderly, the circle's best ward-carver), and **Sorrel** (female Forest Gnome, middle-aged, the circle's strongest combatant) — managed to reactivate two of the six ward-trees before they were attacked. A demon and a pack of fungal zombies, drawn by the flare of druidic energy, struck the grove and scattered the team. Old Bracken was injured and is being sheltered by Sorrel in a hollow beneath one of the dead trees. Thistledown was separated and is hiding in the fungal canopy on the grove's eastern edge, unable to reach the others without crossing open ground patrolled by the janavariseng.

**The Encounter**

When the party reaches the Verdant Ring, they find:

- The grove is roughly 200 feet in diameter, ringed by six massive dead trees. Two of the trees glow faintly with green light — the reactivated wards. The other four are dark.
- A janavariseng (CR 6) is pacing the grove's interior, occasionally stopping to sniff the air or kick at the fungal mat. It is aware that prey is hiding nearby but has not located either group of druids. It is patient — it has been waiting for two days.
- 4 fungal zombies stand motionless at the grove's edges, positioned where the janavariseng placed them as sentinels. They will animate and attack anything that enters their 15-foot detection radius.

The party has several options:
- **Direct assault:** Engage the janavariseng and zombies openly. The janavariseng fights aggressively, using Power Attack and its Brutal Charge ability in the open ground. The zombies converge on the sound of combat within 2 rounds. This is a straightforward fight for a level 6–7 party.
- **Distraction and rescue:** Draw the janavariseng away from the grove to allow the druids to escape. The janavariseng is not clever — a loud distraction on one side of the grove will pull it and the nearest zombies out of position, creating a window to reach the druids. Thistledown, if the party can reach her first (Perception DC 18 to spot her in the canopy, or she signals them with a bird call if they approach within 60 feet), can guide them to Bracken and Sorrel's hiding spot.
- **Stealth:** The fungal mat muffles sound (−4 to hearing-based Perception), and the spore haze reduces visibility. A party that moves carefully can potentially reach both groups of druids without engaging the janavariseng at all (Stealth checks opposed by the janavariseng's Perception, with the −4 auditory penalty working in the party's favor). The janavariseng, however, has scent, which is not penalized by the terrain.

**Rescue and Aftermath**

Old Bracken has a broken leg and 3 points of Constitution damage from spore exposure. Sorrel is uninjured but exhausted. Thistledown is unharmed and furious — at the demon, at herself for underestimating the danger, and at the Contamination in general. If the party heals Bracken and escorts all three druids back to Ironvault, the rescue is complete.

The rescued druids provide the party with two valuable pieces of intelligence:

1. **The ward network works.** Thistledown's theory is sound — the reactivated wards did push back the Contamination in the grove's immediate vicinity. With more druids and more time, the technique could be expanded. This information is the basis for Tanis's allied contribution in Phase 3: if the party completes this side quest and turns Garl, the druid circle has both the proof of concept and the restored primal energy to build a network of purified zones, dramatically accelerating the world's recovery and providing a staging ground for the Battle of Nexus.
2. **The demon was waiting for them.** It did not stumble onto the grove — it was there before them, positioned and ready. Someone — something — knew they were coming and sent a hunter to stop them. This confirms that Tornsoul's demons are actively sabotaging recovery efforts on Tanis, not merely feeding on suffering. Fennel finds this information deeply alarming and shares it with Dagna, which shifts the Forge-Elder's attitude from skepticism to guarded cooperation — if something is deliberately preventing the druids from doing their work, then the party's mission to confront the Warlock is not just hopeful idealism but a strategic necessity.

**Ally Points:** Completing this side quest adds 1 ally point to Tanis's contribution if Garl is also turned (for a maximum of 3). If Garl is killed, the side quest does not add a point — without the restored primal energy from Garl's renunciation, the ward network cannot be scaled beyond a local effect.

---

### Scene 5: Mosshollow

The journey to Garl's childhood home takes the party deep into the Contamination — roughly forty miles from Ironvault, through the densest and oldest part of the infected zone. The trip takes approximately two to three days on foot, depending on the party's speed and the number of encounters along the way. Use the environmental rules from Scene 3 with deep-interior DCs (Fortitude DC 17, visibility 30 feet). The Gnomish poultices are essential — without them, Constitution damage from spore exposure becomes a serious attrition threat over a multi-day journey.

**The Journey**

The deeper the party travels, the worse the Contamination becomes. The outer fringe is ugly but navigable — fungal growth coating the ground, the occasional dead tree, the distant glow of bioluminescent clusters. The interior is something else entirely. Here the fungal mat is several feet thick, the air is so dense with spores that it takes on a visible haze, and the native flora has been replaced entirely by Contamination growths — towering fungal stalks, pulsing bioluminescent sacs, and webs of mycelium that stretch between structures like the rigging of a ship. The remains of Dwarven cities are visible beneath and within the growth — stone walls half-consumed by fungus, iron doors rusted shut, the outlines of streets and plazas swallowed by the advancing mat.

> *The cities are the worst part. You pass through three of them on the second day — or through what is left of them. The buildings are still standing, mostly, but the Contamination has eaten into the stone the way rot eats into wood, softening the edges, filling the windows, draping the rooftops in curtains of grey-green growth that stir without wind. Some of the buildings are occupied. You can see the shapes inside — motionless, fungal, roughly person-shaped. They do not move as you pass. You do not stop to check whether they can.*

**Planned Encounter: The Mautchidaya**

On the second day of travel, the party is ambushed by the mautchidaya (CR 8) that coordinates Tornsoul's demon cell on Tanis. It has been tracking the party since they entered the deep interior — it recognized them as purposeful travelers, not lost healers, and has been shadowing them from above, using the spore haze and its flight to remain undetected.

The mautchidaya attacks from above at a moment of the party's vulnerability — during a rest, while crossing a difficult obstacle, or immediately after a random encounter when the party's resources are partially depleted. It opens with its **Pinning Gaze** on the party's most mobile combatant (typically a melee fighter or someone with flight), then executes a **Diving Strike** on the most isolated target.

- **Enemies:** 1 mautchidaya (CR 8). It is accompanied by 2 janavariseng (CR 6) that emerge from concealment in the fungal canopy when the mautchidaya attacks, flanking the party from ground level.
- **Terrain:** Deep Contamination zone. Visibility 30 feet. The fungal mat is thick and uneven (difficult terrain). Towering fungal stalks (15–20 feet tall) provide the mautchidaya with perching points between dives and the janavariseng with cover for approach. The ruins of a Dwarven building nearby offer hard cover if the party retreats to it, but the interior contains 1d4 dormant fungal zombies that animate if disturbed.
- **Tactics:** The mautchidaya fights as a skirmisher — dive, strike, ascend, reposition. It uses its Pinning Gaze to lock down threats and its poison to weaken targets over multiple rounds. The janavariseng are blunt instruments that charge the nearest enemy and use Power Attack. If the mautchidaya is reduced below half hit points, it retreats into the spore haze (Fly speed + reduced visibility makes pursuit difficult) and does not return. The janavariseng fight to the death.
- **GM note:** This encounter is the chapter's most dangerous combat. A CR 8 creature with two CR 6 flankers is a serious threat for a level 6–7 party, even with mythic tier 2. The terrain advantages (low visibility, difficult ground, fungal zombie traps) favor the demons. If the party is already weakened from travel attrition, consider having the mautchidaya attack with only one janavariseng, with the second arriving 3 rounds later to give the party time to deal with threats sequentially. The goal is a hard fight, not a TPK.

**Mosshollow**

Garl's childhood home is in the ruins of Mosshollow, a Forest Gnome village that was one of the first settlements consumed by the Contamination. The village is barely recognizable — the tree-homes that Gnomes favored have been reduced to fungal pillars, and the paths between them have vanished under centuries of growth. Fennel's directions, supplemented by any information from Sona, if turned, who knew Garl well and can describe Mosshollow's layout from his stories, can lead the party to the general area. Finding the specific home requires a Search check (DC 20) or tracking the faint residual aura of the wondrous figurine with *detect magic* (the aura is faint but present, detectable within 30 feet through the ambient magical noise of the Contamination).

**The Home**

Garl's childhood home is a hollow inside what was once a great oak tree — now a dead, fungus-sheathed pillar thirty feet tall. The interior is small, Gnome-scaled, and remarkably well preserved compared to the surrounding ruin. The Contamination has penetrated the walls but has not consumed the space — something about the residual druidic energy in the wood has slowed it. The air inside is cleaner than outside, the spore concentration noticeably lower.

Inside, the party finds:

- **The remains of the old friend.** A Forest Gnome skeleton, seated against the far wall, still wearing the remnants of traveling clothes and a leather satchel. The skeleton is undisturbed — the Contamination grew around it but did not animate it, perhaps because of the figurine's proximity. The satchel contains mundane supplies (rotted rations, a corroded water flask, a journal too damaged to read) and the figurine.

- **The Rerox figurine.** A small stone figurine of a grizzly bear, carved with obvious skill and great affection. It is warm to the touch — not with heat, but with the impression of warmth the way a living thing feels warm. A character who holds it can sense, faintly, the presence within: not consciousness exactly, but something patient and trusting, an animal's faith that the person who put it to sleep will come back to wake it up.

> *The figurine fits in your palm. It is grey stone, smooth from handling, carved in the shape of a great bear — not a stylized representation but a portrait, specific and individual, with a notch in one ear and a scar across the muzzle. When you close your fingers around it, the stone is warm. Not like something heated — like something alive. And for a moment, just a moment, you feel something press against your awareness from the inside. Not a thought. Not a voice. Just the patient, trusting certainty of something that has been waiting a very long time.*

*GM note: The figurine is not a combat item and has no mechanical function beyond its role as the lever. It radiates moderate conjuration magic (the original spell that bound Rerox to the figurine). A character who casts* identify *or* analyze dweomer *on it learns that it contains a living creature in a state of suspended animation — a grizzly bear, severely wounded, preserved at the moment before death. The figurine can only be activated by Garl — it is keyed to the bond between druid and companion.*

**Milestone:** Level 7 is awarded upon recovering the Rerox figurine.

---

### Scene 6: The Healer's Road

The Healer's Road connects the surviving wall-cities to the central ziggurat. It is the only path through the Contamination that is maintained and relatively safe — kept clear by Garl's will, a remnant of his desperate desire for healing that manifests through his corrupted primal magic even when the rest of his mind is lost to pain. The Road is a strip of bare stone approximately twenty feet wide, free of fungal growth, with the Contamination pressing in from both sides like walls of diseased vegetation. Walking it feels like passing through a corridor — the growth on either side is head-height or taller, blocking sight lines and creating an oppressive sense of enclosure.

The full Healer's Road from the wall-cities to the ziggurat takes approximately six days on foot — a distance that reflects the true scale of the cavern. The party need not walk the entire length; if coming from the deep interior after visiting Mosshollow, they can intercept the Road at a midpoint, reducing the remaining journey to approximately two days. The Road is free of the normal environmental hazards — no spore saves, no difficult terrain — but it is not safe. The Contamination that Garl's will holds back from the Road's surface still presses in from both sides, and the things that live within it — fungal zombies, mutated creatures, and Tornsoul's demons — are drawn to the Road precisely because it is a corridor of movement through otherwise impassable terrain. The party faces a series of escalating encounters along the Road, each more dangerous than the last.

**Encounter 1: The Dead Caravan**

On the first day, the party comes upon the remains of a healer's escort — a group of fungal zombies that were once Dwarven volunteers and their patient, a Gnomish healer, all killed on the Road and reanimated by the spores. The zombies are clustered around a collapsed handcart in the center of the Road, motionless until the party approaches within 30 feet.

- **Enemies:** 6 fungal zombies (mixed Dwarf and Gnome, standard zombie template with Spore Burst). One of the zombies — the former healer — is larger and more intact than the others, wearing the remnants of a druid's vestments. Use the fast zombie variant for this one (Ref +2, move 40 ft., no staggered condition).
- **Terrain:** The Road is 20 feet wide. The handcart provides partial cover in the center. The Contamination walls on either side are dense and opaque — stepping off the Road means entering full Contamination conditions (DC 17 spore saves, difficult terrain, zero visibility beyond 10 feet).
- **Tactics:** The zombies converge on the nearest living creature. They do not coordinate. The fast zombie (the former healer) moves first and attempts to close with the party's back line. When destroyed, each zombie releases its Spore Burst — in the confined space of the Road, multiple bursts in close succession can force several saves in a single round.
- **Escape hatch:** The party can simply retreat along the Road. The zombies do not pursue beyond 100 feet from the cart — they are bound to the site of their death, not to a hunting instinct. A party that retreats and waits an hour can circle back and find the zombies have returned to their motionless cluster, allowing the party to bypass them by moving along the Road's edge at a careful distance (Stealth DC 12 — the zombies' Perception is poor).
- **GM note:** This encounter is a warm-up. Its purpose is to establish that the Road is not safe and to demonstrate, viscerally, what happens to the healers who walk it. The handcart still contains supplies — a healer's kit, a few potions of *lesser restoration* (useful against spore damage), and a leather-bound journal whose final entry reads: *"Day four. Can hear him screaming. Marta says we should turn back. I told her we cannot turn back. We promised."*

**Encounter 2: The Lure**

On the second day (or later on the first, if the party is making good time), the party hears a voice calling from the Contamination — a Dwarven woman's voice, hoarse and desperate, crying for help. The voice comes from approximately forty feet into the growth on the Road's eastern side, and it is convincing. A character who listens carefully can hear specifics: the woman claims she is trapped beneath a collapsed wall, that her leg is broken, that she has been calling for days. She knows the Road is nearby. She begs anyone who can hear her to come.

The voice is a trap. A pair of **hatyararata** (CR 3) have learned to mimic the sounds of the Road's traffic — the pleas of healers, the groans of the dying, the calls for help that are common in the Contamination zone. They are lurking in the growth on either side of the Road, flanking the point where the voice originates. Their plan is simple: lure one or more party members off the Road and into the Contamination, then strike from invisibility while the targets are disoriented by the spore haze and difficult terrain.

- **Detection:** A Sense Motive check (DC 18) reveals that the voice, while emotionally convincing, repeats certain phrases in a pattern — it is mimicry, not speech. A Perception check (DC 20) from the Road's edge detects faint movement in the growth on both sides of the voice's apparent location — the hatyararata repositioning. A character with *detect evil* or *detect magic* can sense the demons' presence within 60 feet if they probe into the growth.
- **If the party enters the Contamination:** Full environmental rules apply immediately — DC 17 spore saves every hour, difficult terrain, visibility 30 feet. The hatyararata attack from invisibility using their Shadow Double ability to create flanking positions. They target the character furthest from the Road, attempting to isolate them in the growth where the rest of the party cannot easily reach.
- **Enemies:** 2 hatyararata (CR 3). In the open, they are not a serious threat to a level 7 party, but the Contamination terrain dramatically favors them — they are immune to the spores, unhindered by the difficult terrain, and their invisibility is far more effective when visibility is already reduced to 30 feet.
- **Escape hatch:** The Road itself. Any party member who retreats to the Road is effectively safe — the hatyararata will not follow onto the cleared stone, where their terrain advantages vanish and they are badly outmatched. The voice continues for several minutes after the party disengages, then falls silent. A party that refuses to leave the Road entirely can bypass the encounter with no consequence beyond the knowledge that something in the Contamination is hunting them.
- **GM note:** The purpose of this encounter is not the combat — the hatyararata are outclassed in a straight fight. The purpose is the decision: does the party leave the safety of the Road to help someone who might be real? The voice is good enough to fool most parties, and the moral weight of ignoring a cry for help in this place — where every community has lost people to the Road, where the entire culture of Tanis is built around the question of who volunteers and who refuses — should make the choice genuinely difficult. If the party sees through the deception, reward their caution. If they fall for it, make the ambush sharp but survivable, and let the realization that they were fooled by demons sting more than the wounds.

**Encounter 3: The Blockade**

On the final stretch of the Road before the ziggurat — when the screams are loudest and the Contamination walls on either side are at their tallest and thickest — the party encounters a deliberate obstruction. A section of the Road approximately sixty feet long has been partially overgrown. The Contamination has pushed inward from both sides, narrowing the clear path from twenty feet to barely five — a single-file passage choked with reaching fungal tendrils and patches of exposed spore growth. This is not natural encroachment. The growth has been cultivated and directed by a **vayuphak** (CR 5) and a **janavariseng** (CR 6) working in concert — the vayuphak using its spell-like abilities to accelerate the fungal growth into a chokepoint, the janavariseng waiting in concealment on the far side to strike the party as they emerge in single file.

- **Enemies:** 1 vayuphak (CR 5) concealed within the overgrown section, positioned on top of the Contamination wall 15 feet above the Road. 1 janavariseng (CR 6) crouching in the growth on the far side of the blockade, ready to charge the first creature that emerges. 4 fungal zombies embedded in the narrowed walls of the passage, dormant until a creature passes within 5 feet — then they lunge outward, grappling.
- **Terrain:** The narrowed passage is the critical feature. The five-foot width means the party cannot fight side by side — only one Medium creature can occupy the passage's width at a time. The fungal tendrils reaching inward from both sides provide concealment (20% miss chance) and threaten to entangle — any creature that ends its turn in the passage must make a Reflex save (DC 14) or be entangled until the start of its next turn. The overgrown sections on either side of the narrowed passage are full Contamination conditions.
- **Tactics:** The vayuphak opens with its spell-like abilities from above, targeting the party while they are strung out in the narrow passage. The embedded zombies activate when PCs are adjacent, grappling to hold them in place for the vayuphak's attacks. The janavariseng waits until at least two party members have emerged from the far side of the blockade, then charges with Brutal Charge and Power Attack. The intent is a coordinated pinch — the party caught between enemies in front and entangling growth behind, with spell-like abilities raining down from above.
- **Escape hatch:** The party can retreat back through the passage to the open Road behind them. The janavariseng does not pursue into the narrow section (it is too large to maneuver effectively), and the vayuphak breaks off if the party withdraws beyond the blockade's edge. Alternatively, a party with area-of-effect fire spells or alchemist's fire can burn through the overgrown section — the accelerated fungal growth is dry and flammable (hardness 2, 30 HP per 10-foot section), and fire clears the passage in 2–3 rounds, restoring the Road to its full width and denying the enemies their chokepoint advantage. The vayuphak flees via greater teleport if the blockade is destroyed and the janavariseng is killed.
- **GM note:** This is the hardest of the Road encounters and should feel like a deliberate attempt to prevent the party from reaching the ziggurat. The demons know the party is coming — the mautchidaya, if it survived the ambush in Scene 5, has communicated their presence and direction. This blockade is a prepared defensive position, not a chance encounter. The party should recognize the tactical sophistication — this is not mindless Contamination, this is an intelligent enemy using the environment as a weapon.
