The narrow passage is designed to neutralize the party's numerical and power advantage by forcing them into a killbox. Smart parties will find ways to break the chokepoint before entering it. Parties that charge through will take significant damage but can still prevail — the enemies are dangerous but not overwhelming for a level 7 party with mythic tier 2.

**The Twisted Agent**

Midway along the Road, the party encounters one of Garl's twisted agents returning from a mission to the wall-cities. The agent is a former Dwarf healer — long dead, reanimated and sustained by the Contamination, its body warped into something barely recognizable as the person it was. It is roughly humanoid, seven feet tall, its frame stretched and distorted by fungal growth that has replaced muscle and bone. It moves with a lurching, uneven gait and speaks in a voice that sounds like it is being produced by something that has forgotten how mouths work.

The agent stops when it sees the party. It does not attack. It asks, with that terrible, broken courtesy, whether any of them can heal its master.

> *The thing on the road was a Dwarf once. You can see it in the proportions — the breadth of shoulder, the shortness of limb — but everything else has been taken and replaced. Fungal growth has eaten the skin and pushed outward, cracking the surface into plates of grey-green chitin. The eyes are gone, replaced by clusters of bioluminescent nodules that pulse in the same rhythm as the Contamination around you. It stands in the center of the road and looks at you — or aims its face at you, since looking implies eyes — and when it speaks, the voice is soft and polite and completely wrong.*
>
> *"Forgive the intrusion. My master suffers. He has suffered for a long time. Is there anyone among you who can heal? The road is safe. My master will see you. He is in great pain. Please. Is there anyone among you who can heal?"*

The agent is not hostile unless refused. If the party says yes — or says anything that the agent interprets as willingness to try — it turns and leads them toward the ziggurat at a walking pace, making no further conversation. If the party says no, the agent's demeanor shifts. The courtesy drains from its voice. Its body tenses. It attacks.

- **If the agent attacks:** Use the stat block for an advanced fungal zombie (dire zombie template with the spore burst ability, CR 5). The agent is not a serious threat to a level 7 party, but the encounter serves a narrative purpose — it demonstrates what happens to those who refuse, and it shows the depth of Garl's madness. The agent is not choosing to attack. It is compelled by the same corrupted instinct that keeps the Road clear. Garl's need is absolute, and anything that denies it triggers a violent response the agent cannot control.
- **If the agent leads them:** The agent escorts the party the remaining distance to the ziggurat without incident. It does not speak again. It does not respond to questions. It walks, and they follow, and the Contamination presses in from both sides, and the Howler's screams grow louder with every mile.

*GM note: The party may attempt to communicate with the agent beyond the initial exchange. It has no independent will — it is an extension of Garl's need, not a person. It cannot provide information, express opinions, or deviate from its two-state behavior (escort or attack). Attempts to use magic to restore its mind fail — there is no mind left to restore. The healer it was died centuries ago. What remains is a function, not a being.*

---

### Scene 7: The Ziggurat

The ziggurat rises from the center of the Contamination zone — a stepped pyramid of dark stone, ancient Dwarven construction, older than the cavern cities and built for a purpose no one now remembers. It is the heart of the Contamination. The fungal growth is thickest here, climbing the ziggurat's flanks in sheets and cascading from its tiers in curtains of grey-green biomass. The air is almost unbreathable — even the Healer's Road's protection falters within a quarter mile of the structure, and the party must resume spore saves (DC 17) for the final approach.

The ziggurat has a single entrance: a wide doorway at the base, flanked by stone columns that have been nearly consumed by the Contamination. The interior is a single great chamber, stepped downward toward a central platform. The walls are covered in Dwarven carvings — histories, genealogies, prayers — all but obscured by fungal growth. The air inside is hot, humid, and thick with the smell of infection.

Garl is on the central platform.

> *He is so small. That is the first thing you notice — after everything you have heard about the Howler, after days of hearing his screams echo through the cavern, after passing through miles of the Contamination that pours from his body, the creature on the platform is a Forest Gnome barely three feet tall. He is sitting on the stone with his legs drawn up and his arms wrapped around his knees, and he is rocking. The wound is in his side — you can see it, a gash below the ribs that glows with a sickly amber light, and from it, slowly, continuously, a dark ichor drips onto the stone and spreads outward in tendrils that merge with the fungal mat covering the floor. He is not screaming now. He is in one of his lucid intervals, and his face — old, lined, gentle beneath the pain — turns toward you with an expression of such desperate hope that it is worse than any scream could be.*
>
> *"You're here," he says. His voice is small and rough and shaking. "You've come to help me. Please. Please, I know it can be fixed. Everyone says it can't, but I know — I* know *— there has to be a way. Please. I've been waiting so long."*

**The Confrontation**

Garl is lucid when the party arrives — one of his clear intervals, which last anywhere from minutes to hours before the pain overwhelms him again. He is coherent, desperate, and pathetically grateful for the party's presence. He will talk to them, answer questions (to the extent his fractured memory allows), and submit willingly to any attempt to heal him. He begs them to try. He has been begging everyone who comes to try for five hundred years.

**If the party presents the Rerox figurine:**

Garl's reaction is immediate and total. The hope drains from his face and is replaced by something older and quieter — recognition. Not of the figurine itself, but of what it means. The bond between druid and companion is a living thing, and when the figurine comes within Garl's reach, that bond reawakens. He does not need to activate the figurine. He simply knows that Rerox is inside it — waiting, trusting, as patient as the day Garl put him there — and in that moment of clarity, the fog of corruption and madness and five centuries of pain lifts just far enough for Garl to understand.

The wound cannot be healed. It was never going to be healed. Every healer who walked the Road, every druid who tried, every spell and potion and prayer — all of it was for nothing, and somewhere beneath the madness he has known this for a very long time. The figurine does not tell him this. It tells him something else: that there was a time when he was the kind of person who would put his best friend to sleep gently rather than let him die in pain. And that he can still be that person, one last time, if he chooses.

> *He takes the figurine from you with both hands, and he holds it the way you hold something that can break, and for a long time he does not speak. The rocking stops. The ichor still drips from his wound, but slower now, as though even the Contamination is holding its breath. When he looks up, his eyes are clear — not the feverish clarity of hope, but the quiet clarity of a man who has finally stopped fighting something he cannot beat.*
>
> *"Rerox," he says. Just the name. He presses the figurine to his chest and closes his eyes, and when he opens them again, there are tears on his face, but his voice is steady.*
>
> *"I always knew. I think I always knew. I just — I couldn't stop hoping. You understand? I couldn't stop, because if I stopped hoping, then all of it — everyone who came, everyone who tried, everyone I —" He stops. He breathes. "It can't be fixed."*
>
> *It is not a question.*
>
> *"I would like to let Rerox out first. Is that all right? He's been in there so long. He should see me. He should know I'm here." He looks at you, and the expression on his face is the one you see on the faces of people who have made a decision that costs them everything and are at peace with it. "And then I would like you to help me. The way I helped him. Gently. Please. I am so tired, and it hurts so much, and I would like it to stop."*

Garl activates the figurine. Rerox emerges — an enormous grizzly bear, scarred and grey-muzzled, blinking in confusion at the centuries that have passed in what felt like a moment. The bear sees Garl and crosses to him immediately, pressing its great head against the Gnome's chest. Garl wraps his arms around Rerox's neck and holds on for a long moment, whispering words too quiet for the party to hear. Then he releases the bear, looks at the party, and nods.

The party must kill Garl. This is the turning — not a liberation, not a redemption, but a mercy. Garl has renounced his mythic power (the party can feel it leave him — a withdrawal of pressure, like a held breath released), and he is no longer a Warlock. He is a dying Forest Gnome druid asking for the same kindness he once showed his companion. His hit points are irrelevant. Any lethal attack will do. He does not resist.

> *The Contamination begins to die the moment he does. It starts at the ziggurat — the tendrils of ichor that have been spreading from his wound for five centuries slow, darken, and begin to crumble. The fungal mat on the floor turns grey and brittle. The bioluminescent glow fades. It will take weeks for the full extent of the Contamination to wither, but the process is immediate and irreversible, spreading outward from the ziggurat like a wave. On the cavern walls, in the distant wall-cities, the Dwarves will notice the change within hours — the air clearing, the sweet smell fading, the Contamination's advance halting for the first time in living memory. They will not know what happened. They will only know that something has changed.*
>
> *Rerox lies down beside Garl's body and does not move. The bear understands what has happened in the way that animals understand — not with words, but with the absolute certainty of a bond that has not broken, only changed. Rerox will not leave. If the party allows it, the bear remains at the ziggurat, guarding the body of the druid who loved him enough to put him to sleep rather than let him suffer, and who, in the end, asked for the same kindness in return.*

**If the party does not have the figurine:**

Without the lever, Garl cannot be turned. He is a Warlock — diminished, agonized, barely coherent, but still a mythic-tier threat. He submits to the party's healing attempts with desperate gratitude — holding still, whispering encouragement, his whole body trembling with the effort of hope. The healing fails. It always fails. The corruption devours every spell, every potion, every laying of hands. The magic touches the wound and is swallowed, twisted, spat back out as a pulse of amber light that scorches the stone. Garl feels it fail. He feels it the way he has felt it fail hundreds of times before. And this time, something breaks.

> *The change is instantaneous. One moment he is sitting there, small and shaking and grateful, his hands clasped around yours as though you are the last thing keeping him above water. The next moment the hands tighten — and you realize, with a shock that goes through you like ice water, that you cannot pull free. His grip is immovable. The bones in your hand grind together and you hear yourself gasp, and when you look at his face the gentleness is gone. The eyes are the same eyes — brown, old, full of pain — but behind them something vast and mindless has woken up, something that has been coiling in the wound for five centuries, and it is finished waiting.*
>
> *He stands. He is three feet tall and he lifts you off the ground one-handed, your full weight held at arm's length as though you weigh nothing, and he screams. The scream is not the distant sound you have been hearing for days — it is the source, unfiltered, close enough to feel in your teeth and your ribs and the marrow of your bones. The fungal mat on the floor surges outward from his feet. The walls of the ziggurat crack. And then he throws you — not a push, not a shove, but a throw, the full rotational force of a body that should not be capable of this, and you are in the air, crossing the chamber, hitting the far wall hard enough to see white, and sliding to the floor thirty feet from where you started.*
>
> *When your vision clears, he is already changing. The small body is twisting, expanding, the skin splitting along seams of amber light as something inside forces its way out. The sound he makes is no longer a scream. It is something older and worse — the sound of a living thing being unmade from the inside, and the thing that is unmaking him does not care that he is in agony. It never has.*

The party must fight him.

**Encounter: The Howler**

- **Enemy:** Garl the Even-Handed, the Howler. *Stat block: Stat Block Appendix.*
- **Terrain:** The ziggurat interior. The central platform is 40 feet in diameter, surrounded by stepped stone tiers rising to the walls. The floor is covered in a thick layer of Contamination growth (difficult terrain). The entrance is 60 feet from the platform. The ceiling is 30 feet high, thick with hanging fungal growth that drops in clumps when disturbed (creatures moving beneath the ceiling must make Reflex saves DC 14 or be struck for 1d6 bludgeoning and exposed to a spore burst).
- **Garl's abilities:** Garl has lost all druidic spellcasting. His wild shape produces only mutated, tortured forms — each a nightmare version of a natural animal, warped by the Contamination. He shifts forms frequently during combat, each form granting different physical attributes (the stat block provides three forms: bear, serpent, and swarm). His primary assets are his enormously enhanced Strength, his damage reduction (the wound has made his body resistant to physical harm — the corruption absorbs damage), and the Contamination itself, which he can direct within the ziggurat as a weapon (fungal tendrils, spore bursts, animated zombie minions rising from the floor).
- **Tactics:** Garl does not fight tactically. He is in too much pain to strategize. He attacks the nearest threat with whatever form he is wearing, shifting to a new form when the current one takes significant damage. Between attacks, he screams — and in the screams, between the wordless agony, the party can hear fragments of coherent speech: pleas for help, apologies, half-remembered names. He is not choosing to fight. The corruption is choosing for him.
- **GM note:** Fighting Garl should feel terrible. He is not a villain reveling in power or a tyrant defending his throne. He is a good person in unimaginable pain whose body has been turned into a weapon against his will. The party should know, by this point, that he was the gentlest of the ten heroes, the moral center of the group, a healer and a peacemaker. Killing him without the figurine is not a mercy — it is a last resort. The corruption takes everything it can from the moment: the screams, the pleading, the mutated forms that were once a druid's sacred connection to nature. Make the party feel the weight of this. Corruption score: +1.*

---

### Scene 8: Gaia's Navel

Whether Garl was turned or killed, the party must descend beneath the ziggurat to reach Gaia's Navel. A stairway at the back of the ziggurat chamber leads down — carved stone, Dwarven work, older than the ziggurat itself. The stairway descends for approximately two hundred feet before opening into the chamber below.

> *The air changes on the stairs. The heat, the humidity, the choking sweetness of the Contamination — all of it falls away as you descend, replaced by something cooler, cleaner, older. The fungal growth on the walls thins and stops. The stone beneath your feet is smooth and dry and warm in a way that has nothing to do with temperature — it is the warmth of something that has been alive for a very long time and has no intention of dying. The stairway opens into a chamber, and the chamber is full of light.*
>
> *The pool is in the center — a circle of worked stone, thirty feet across, filled with water that glows with a warm golden radiance. The light it casts has no shadows. The chamber around the pool is a garden — dense, green, thriving, impossibly healthy in this place where nothing healthy should be able to survive. Flowers bloom in colors that the cavern above has not seen in five hundred years. The air smells of clean earth and growing things and rain. The silence here is not the silence of absence. It is the silence of a place that has been waiting, patiently and without doubt, for someone worthy to arrive.*

**If Garl was turned:** The party descends knowing that the man who spent five centuries denied the pool's healing was finally at peace when he died. The garden feels like a memorial — beautiful and sad in equal measure. Any PC who steps into the pool is healed of all wounds, all diseases, all Constitution damage from spore exposure, and all conditions. The healing is total, immediate, and accompanied by a sensation of profound warmth that is not physical but emotional — the primal magic of the earth, expressed as comfort.

**If Garl was killed:** The garden is the same, the pool is the same, but the experience carries a different weight. Garl spent centuries at the edge of this pool, unable to enter, unable to leave, the only place in the world where his pain lessened. The beauty of the garden is an accusation — all this healing, and none of it for him. The pool heals the party as described above, but the GM should describe the sensation differently: the warmth is still there, but it feels older, sadder, the comfort of a parent who has lost a child and is offering what it has to whoever remains.

**The Place of Power**

The rune circle is carved into the floor of the pool itself — visible beneath the golden water, ancient Dwarven script inlaid with a metal that glows faintly in the same warm light. When the party enters the pool and stands within the circle, the place of power activates.

The activation on Tanis is quieter than on Diaspola. There is no shattering ice, no dramatic release. The golden light intensifies slowly, the water rises around the party's legs, and the garden responds — every flower opens, every leaf turns toward the light, the vegetation pressing outward as though the garden is trying to grow beyond the chamber's walls for the first time in centuries. The primal magic of the earth surges through the pool and up through the ziggurat above, and the party feels it: the Contamination is not just dying but being replaced. Clean growth is pushing through the fungal mat, the air is clearing, the cavern floor is beginning a process of restoration that will take years to complete but has become, in this moment, inevitable.

The party receives: mythic tier 3 and the third fragment of the Song of Heaven.

> *The third fragment of the Song comes to you the way the others did — remembered, not learned, a piece of knowledge that was always present and only now makes itself heard. It is deeper than the first two fragments, lower in register, with a resonance that you feel in your chest rather than hear with your ears. It is the sound of stone and soil and root, of things that grow in darkness, of patience measured in millennia. When it joins the melody you already carry, the Song gains a foundation — the bright, clear notes of the Ancillary and the flowing beauty of the Mother of Rivers now rest on something solid and enduring, and the whole is stronger for it. Three fragments of nine. The Song is not yet complete, but it is beginning to sound like something that could, given time and sacrifice, heal the world.*

---

### Chapter Conclusion

**World State (Turned):** The Contamination is dying. Garl's death — willing, chosen, a renunciation rather than a defeat — severed the source, and the primal magic released by the activation of Gaia's Navel is accelerating the recovery. The fungal growth is withering across the cavern floor, receding at a rate visible to the naked eye. The process will take months to complete, but the wall-cities are already sending scouts to the cavern floor for the first time in generations. Root-Keeper Fennel and the druid circle are leading the restoration effort, using the ward network techniques proven at the Verdant Ring (if the side quest was completed) to establish purified zones that serve as anchors for regrowth. Forge-Elder Dagna has committed Ironvault's engineering resources to rebuilding the floor-level cities once the Contamination has receded sufficiently. Rerox remains at the ziggurat, guarding Garl's body, and the Gnomish druids have begun treating the site as a shrine. Tanis's contribution to the Battle of Nexus includes a contingent of Dwarven heavy infantry from the wall-cities and a druid support corps led by Fennel — disciplined, well-equipped, and motivated by the first genuine hope their people have known in five centuries.

**World State (Killed):** The Contamination persists. Without Garl's willing renunciation, his death does not sever the source cleanly — the corruption lingers, drawing on the residual mythic energy embedded in the land. The Contamination stops advancing but does not recede. The wall-cities survive but remain under siege from a static threat that will take decades or centuries to dissipate naturally. Fennel and the druid circle continue their holding action, but without the surge of primal energy from a clean turning, the ward network cannot scale. Dagna sends a token force to the Battle of Nexus — a small contingent of Dwarven fighters, brave but unsupported by druidic magic. Tanis's contribution is minimal.

**Transition:** The party returns to Nexus via the Rainbow Road. The journey back to the gate town requires traversing the Contamination zone in reverse, but the conditions have changed — if Garl was turned, the Contamination is visibly receding, the air is cleaner, and the party encounters no resistance. If Garl was killed, the journey is identical to the inbound trip, minus the demon ambush (the mautchidaya, if it survived, has withdrawn). On Nexus, Maranthe meets them at the citadel. She can see the change in them — the weight of what they have done on Tanis is visible. She does not ask for details immediately. She provides resupply, rest, and time before debriefing. If Garl was turned, the news from Tanis reaches the Nexian population and produces a complex reaction — relief that another world has been freed, grief for the manner of it. The gate town residents, many of them Dwarven refugees, receive the news with a silence that is not indifference but its opposite. The party rests, prepares, and departs for the Auzra gate town.

**Milestone:** Level 8 is awarded at the conclusion of this chapter. Mythic Tier 3 was granted earlier at Gaia's Navel (Scene 8).

---

## Chapter 6: Auzra — The Gray Eminence *(Levels 9–10, Mythic Tier 4)*

### Chapter Summary

Auzra is the campaign's first chapter built around political complexity rather than environmental threat. Where Diaspola was a fairy tale and Tanis a tragedy, Auzra is a thriller — a police state sustained by paranoia, propaganda, and magical compulsion, where the party must navigate a three-tiered society, build a coalition from the ground up, and ultimately decide whether to spark a popular uprising to cover their assault on the Warlock's fortress.

The world is a vast sky — dozens of floating islands arranged in three tiers, connected by mist, chain, and regulated magical transport. The lowest tier is a toxic labor colony. The middle tier is a surveillance state of chained islands, where universities that once celebrated free inquiry now serve as instruments of ideological control. The upper tier holds Castle Perilous, the Warlock's fortress-palace, and the academy where the realm's most talented arcanists are imprisoned. The Gray Eminence — Tana White-Lightning, a nonbinary Aasimar wizard — rules from the upper tier through the Directorate, a bureaucracy whose senior agents are bound by magical compulsion they do not know they carry.

The lever is Tana's original spellbook, hidden in a ruin on the lower tier by a resistance leader who died before revealing its location. Recovering it requires descending into the toxic atmosphere of the lowest tier. Presenting it to Tana forces a confrontation with the person they were before the corruption — a great teacher who shared knowledge freely — and opens the possibility of turning.

This chapter demands more from the party as planners and diplomats than any before it. The Hawkmen of the mist layer are their primary allies, but the population of the middle tier — Grey Elves who have been terrorized for generations — must also be moved to action if the party hopes to create a distraction large enough to breach Castle Perilous. Three side quests, each building toward the possibility of an uprising, define the chapter's middle act. The party can attempt the assault without the uprising, but the path is significantly harder.

### GM Guidance

**Tone:** Auzra operates at a different register than the first two worlds. The threat here is not a spreading blight or an eternal winter — it is a system of control so thorough that most of its subjects do not recognize it as tyranny. The Grey Elves of the middle tier are educated, articulate, and deeply frightened. They know the Directorate is oppressive, but they also remember — or have been told — that the alternative is chaos, that the Hawkmen are savages, that the Gray Eminence's control is the only thing preventing the realm from falling apart. Five centuries of propaganda have done their work. The party must contend not only with the Directorate's enforcers but with a population that has been conditioned to fear freedom more than servitude.

**The three-tiered structure:** Auzra's physical layout mirrors its social hierarchy. The lower tier is a prison disguised as a colony. The middle tier is a cage disguised as a civilization. The upper tier is a throne disguised as a school. Each tier has its own atmosphere, hazards, and social dynamics, and the party will visit all three. The GM should make the transitions between tiers feel significant — each ascent or descent changes the rules.

**The Hawkmen:** The Hawkmen are the party's lifeline on Auzra. They are fierce, honorable, and deeply lonely — a people who remember a time when they shared the sky with the Grey Elves as equals and who have spent five centuries mourning the loss of that partnership. They are not an organized military force; they are scattered bands of loners and small family groups who come together only when something truly important compels them. The party's arrival is that something. The Hawkmen should feel like kindred spirits to the party — proud, capable people who have been fighting a losing battle for longer than anyone should have to, and who see in the party the first real hope they have had in generations.

**Demons on Auzra:** Tornsoul's demons operate on Auzra through the Directorate itself. A small cell of demons has embedded within the bureaucracy's upper ranks, using the existing apparatus of control to advance Tornsoul's interests. The Directorate's compulsion magic makes this trivially easy — agents who are already magically bound to obey orders do not question orders that happen to serve demonic purposes. The demons' primary objective is to ensure that Tana remains isolated, paranoid, and in power, because the Gray Eminence's tyranny keeps Auzra's population divided, fearful, and incapable of organizing resistance. The party will encounter these demons directly during the infiltration of Castle Perilous.

**Leveling:** The party enters at level 8, mythic tier 3. Level 9 is awarded after recovering the spellbook from the lower tier (Scene 5). Level 10 and mythic tier 4 are awarded at the chapter's conclusion after activating the Overlook (Scene 9).

---

### Scene 1: The Gate Town and the Sky Threshold

The Auzra gate town on Nexus sits on elevated, windswept terrain — rocky outcroppings and sparse grassland that rises toward a ridge where the terminus arch stands exposed to a constant, driving wind. The architecture here is lighter than the Dwarven stonework of the Tanis gate town: open-framed structures of wood and canvas, built to flex rather than resist, many of them long abandoned. The few residents are Grey Elves and a handful of Hawkmen — refugees from Auzra who could not or would not return. The Hawkmen keep to themselves, nesting on the ridge above the town, and watch the party approach with sharp, unblinking eyes.

> *The wind here has not stopped since you arrived. It pushes at you from the west, constant and cold, carrying the smell of open sky and distant rain. The gate town is built for it — the buildings lean into the gusts, their frames creaking in a rhythm that sounds almost like speech, and the few people you see move with the low, braced posture of those who have lived their entire lives at an angle. The terminus arch is at the top of the ridge, exposed on all sides, and it hums. Not with magic — with wind. The arch is a pipe, and the air moving through it produces a low, resonant tone that you can feel in your chest. When you perform the Song, the tone shifts to match.*

The party performs the Song of Heaven — three fragments now, the melody gaining depth and complexity. The arch activates. The Rainbow Road extends outward and upward into open sky.

The transit to Auzra is unlike any before it. The road does not descend into darkness or traverse a frozen landscape — it rises. The colors intensify. The air thins and cools. Banks of cloud rush past on either side, and the road itself seems to narrow, the rainbow tiles barely wide enough for the party to walk abreast. Then the clouds break, and the world opens.

> *The sky goes on forever, that is the only honest description to encompass your initial impressions of this place. You emerge from the cloud bank and the world below you is not a world — it is a void, a depthless expanse of air and light and distant haze, punctuated by islands. Dozens of them. Hundreds, maybe, at different heights and distances, some close enough to see the trees on their surfaces, others nothing more than dark shapes hanging in the blue. Below, far below, a layer of yellowish cloud obscures whatever lies at the bottom — if there is a bottom. Above, the sky darkens toward indigo, and the perpetual twilight of the upper reaches glows faintly, like a bruise. The road deposits you on a small island — perhaps a quarter mile across, covered in wind-bent scrub and mossy stone, with a sheer drop on every side. The air is damp, cool, and thin. You are standing in the mist layer, and the mist is already closing in.*

The party arrives on a small island in the middle tier's mist layer — one of the countless minor islands that the Hawkmen use as waypoints and gathering places. This particular island is close to one of the few semi-permanent Hawkmen camps, a fact that is not coincidental — the Rainbow Road's terminus was placed here deliberately, in a location accessible to the people most likely to remember what it meant.

---

### Scene 2: The Wind-Speakers

Within an hour of the party's arrival, they are found. A patrol of three Hawkmen descends from above and lands on the island's edge. They are tall and lean — taller than humans, with severe features, slightly pointed ears, large hooked noses, and great hawk-like wings protruding from their backs, bound to their shoulder blades by heavy muscle. Their wings display plumage ranging from tawny to dark brown, matched by their hair, and fold up behind them at rest, standing a foot above their heads. They are armed with longbows and curved blades, and their posture is wary but not hostile. They have seen the Rainbow Road activate. They know what it means.

> *They land without sound — three figures dropping from the mist like stones, then spreading their wings at the last moment and touching down on the island's rim with a precision that speaks of a lifetime spent in the air. They are tall and thin, medium-brown skin, light eyes, with great folded wings rising above their heads like furled sails. Their features are severe — hooked noses, sharp cheekbones, the look of people shaped by wind and altitude. The largest of the three — a male with rust-red wing plumage streaked grey at the temples — studies you for a long moment, then speaks. His voice is deep, roughened by wind, and his Common carries a clipped cadence that turns every sentence into something halfway between a statement and a challenge.*
>
> *"The Road opened. We saw it from the thermals. Three generations of my family have watched that arch, and it has never opened — not once, not for anyone, until today." He looks at each of you in turn, measuring. "My grandmother told me stories. She said heroes would come from the Road when the worlds needed them most. I told her she was dreaming." A pause. "She would have enjoyed being right."*

**Key NPC:**

**Kerrax Stormborne** — A Hawkman elder and the closest thing the scattered bands of the mist layer have to a leader. He is not a chief — the Hawkmen do not organize that way — but his word carries weight because he has earned it over decades of fighting the Directorate's patrols and keeping the mist-layer bands connected. He is in his late middle years, scarred from combat, and possesses a dry, understated manner that conceals a fierce intelligence. He does not trust easily, but he trusts what he sees, and what he sees in the party is the fulfillment of a story his people have kept alive for five centuries when no one else believed it.

Kerrax provides the party with a comprehensive briefing on Auzra's current state:

- **The three tiers:** Lower (toxic, domed settlements, labor and exile), middle (five chained islands, Directorate police state, universities), upper (Castle Perilous, the academy, the Warlock). Each tier is increasingly difficult to access and increasingly dangerous.
- **The Directorate:** The Gray Eminence's bureaucracy. Grey Elf administrators who enforce Tana's will across the middle tier. Mid-level agents and above are magically compelled, though they believe themselves to be acting freely. The Directorate controls inter-island transport, regulates all arcane practice, suppresses independent research, and maintains a network of informants throughout the middle tier's population.
- **The Hawkmen's situation:** Scattered bands in the mist layer, surviving on hunting and raiding. They can move freely through the mist — the avian predators that make the layer dangerous to others are navigable hazards for a people born in the air. The Directorate sends patrols into the mist periodically, but the Hawkmen know the currents and the hiding places, and the patrols rarely find what they are looking for. The Hawkmen's great sorrow is not the fighting — it is the broken relationship with the Grey Elves, who were their partners for millennia before the Warlock's propaganda turned them against each other.
- **The upper tier:** Accessible only by flight or regulated magical transport (teleportation circles controlled by the Directorate). The Hawkmen cannot approach it in force — Castle Perilous is defended by magical wards, arcane artillery, and elite guards. Individual Hawkmen have attempted reconnaissance flights and been driven back or killed. The upper tier is, for practical purposes, a fortress.
- **The Gray Eminence:** Kerrax knows Tana only by reputation and rumor. The Warlock has not been seen publicly in over a century. Everything is done through the Directorate. The Hawkmen's oral tradition preserves a different image of Tana — a great teacher, a sharer of knowledge, a friend to the Hawkmen in the early years — but Kerrax is pragmatic enough to know that person may no longer exist.

Kerrax offers the party the Hawkmen's assistance. He cannot command anyone — each Hawkman decides for themselves — but he will spread the word, and he expects that many will come. The heroes of the Rainbow Road are not a myth to the Hawkmen. They are a promise.

> *"We are not an army. We are not even a tribe, not anymore — not the way we were before. We are people who remember, and that is what keeps us in the air when everything else says we should have given up and crawled into a dome on the low tier like everyone else. You are what we remember. I will fly to the bands and tell them you have come, and those who believe will come to you. The rest will watch from a distance and make up their minds later. That is how my people work. We are slow to trust and impossible to shake once we do."*

There is, however, a problem. The Hawkmen cannot carry the party over long distances. A Hawkman can bear another person for short flights — between nearby mist-layer islands, across a gap, up or down a cliff face — but the distances between the mist-layer islands and the chained islands of the middle tier, let alone the upper tier, are too great for sustained carrying flight. The party needs another way to travel.

Kerrax acknowledges this openly. He will send word to the other bands and gather his companions — a process that will take roughly a week, as the Hawkmen are scattered across the mist layer and travel between bands is measured in days, not hours. When they arrive, they will work together to find a solution. In the meantime, Kerrax suggests the party may want to make use of their time. There is a ruin on a nearby island — perhaps two hours' flight from the gathering place — that Hawkmen legend associates with Tana from before the war. The Hawkmen have never explored it. Wizardly magic is anathema to their culture (they practice sorcery and other innate magical traditions, but the learned arcane arts carry a deep taboo), and the ruin is plainly a wizard's work. If the party is willing, the Hawkmen can drop them at the island and return for them when signaled.

> *"There is a place," Kerrax says, and something in his voice shifts — not quite discomfort, not quite reverence, but the tone of a man acknowledging something he does not fully understand. "A small island, east of here. There is a tower on it — old, partially ruined. Our stories say it belonged to the Gray Eminence, from before. From when they were still —" He pauses. "Still the person they were supposed to be. We do not go there. The magic in that place is not our kind. But you are not us, and you may find something useful. I can have three of my people carry you there. When you are ready to return, light a fire on the mountaintop at night. We will see it."*

Within a week, a gathering forms on the island — not a great host, but a meaningful one. Roughly forty Hawkmen from bands across the mist layer, drawn by Kerrax's word and their own belief in the old stories. They are warriors, scouts, and fliers of extraordinary skill, and they are ready to help the party in whatever way they can.

*GM note: The Hawkmen are the party's safe harbor on Auzra. Their mist-layer islands are beyond the Directorate's effective reach, and Hawkmen scouts can provide intelligence, short-range transportation, and tactical support throughout the chapter. The party should feel supported but not carried — the Hawkmen can help them where they need to go, but the decisions about what to do when they arrive are the party's alone. The gliders from Tana's Refuge (Scene 3) are the intended solution to long-distance travel between tiers. If the party does not find the gliders, the Hawkmen construct a large basket carried by six fliers — functional but uncomfortable, conspicuous, and limited to one trip at a time, making the chapter's logistics significantly harder.*

---

### Scene 3: Tana's Refuge *(Optional)*

The ruin Kerrax described sits on a small island roughly a quarter mile across — a barren, wind-scoured rock with a single low mountain at its center and a ruined tower on the mountain's western slope. Three Hawkmen carry the party to the island in short relay flights (two hours total, with rest stops on smaller islands along the way) and depart with instructions: light a bonfire at the mountain's summit when ready for pickup. They leave the party with supplies — dried meat, water, blankets, and flint — sufficient for several days.

The tower is immediately recognizable as a wizard's work. The construction is Grey Elf stonecraft, precise and elegant, but the design incorporates magitek elements — conduit channels in the walls, mounting points for devices long since removed, a lightning rod at the apex that still hums faintly in high winds. The upper floors have partially collapsed, exposing the interior to the elements, but the ground floor and basement are structurally sound. The tower is four stories tall (three above ground, one below), and the damage appears to be the result of age and weather rather than violence.

**The Upper Floors**

The second and third floors are partially open to the sky, their outer walls cracked and their roofing gone. The second floor was a workspace of some kind — a broad table, now rotted to splinters, and mounting brackets for equipment that has been removed. The third floor was an observation platform, with wide windows on all four sides providing a panoramic view of the surrounding sky and islands. Both floors are structurally unsafe in places (Perception DC 14 to identify weak sections, Reflex DC 16 to avoid falling through if a weak section is stepped on, 2d6 falling damage to the floor below).

Among the carved stonework on the upper floors, observant PCs can find fragments of a runic pattern worked into the decorative borders — two distinct sections of what is clearly a larger design, incorporated so seamlessly into the ornamentation that they could easily be mistaken for purely aesthetic elements. A Knowledge (Arcana) DC 20 check identifies them as components of a locking rune. Finding both fragments reduces the DC of the basement door check (see below) by 10.

**The Basement**

The ground floor contains a stairway descending to the basement — a single large room, dry and well-preserved, used for storage. Crates and barrels line the walls, their contents long since rotted or corroded to uselessness. A careful search of the basement reveals a concealed door in the north wall (Perception DC 25). The door is fitted flush with the stonework and sealed with a runic lock — a magitek mechanism that requires the correct rune sequence to open.

**The Runic Lock:** The lock requires a Knowledge (Arcana) check to decipher. The base DC is 35 — an extraordinary difficulty, reflecting the skill of the wizard who designed it. If the party found both rune fragments on the upper floors, the DC is reduced to 25. If they found only one fragment, the DC is reduced to 30. The party may attempt the check up to three times. On the third failure, the lock's automated defenses activate.

**The Guardian:** The lock is protected by a construct — a magitek sentinel, dormant in an alcove beside the concealed door, activated either by three failed attempts at the lock or by any attempt to force the door (Strength check, *knock* spell, physical damage to the door or wall). The construct is a **magitek guardian** — use the stat block for a Large animated object (Bestiary, CR 5) with the following modifications: +4 natural armor (AC 20), hardness 10 (magitek construction), slam attack 2d6+7, and a built-in *shocking grasp* discharge (3d6 electricity damage, delivered on a successful slam, usable every 1d4 rounds). The guardian fights until destroyed and does not pursue beyond the basement.

*GM note: The guardian is a speed bump, not a chapter-defining fight. A level 8 party with mythic tier 3 should handle it without significant difficulty, though the hardness 10 and electricity discharge can be unpleasant for melee-heavy groups. The real challenge of this scene is the runic lock — the DC 35 check without the rune fragments is deliberately punishing. The scene rewards thorough exploration of the upper floors before attempting the basement. If the party skips the upper floors entirely and tries to brute-force the lock, they will almost certainly fight the guardian — which is a viable path, just a more resource-intensive one.*

**The Laboratory**

Beyond the concealed door, a short corridor leads to Tana's pre-war laboratory — a complex of four rooms, sealed and preserved by the same magitek environmental controls that kept the concealed door hidden. The air inside is stale but breathable. Faint magitek lighting activates when the door opens, casting the rooms in a warm amber glow.

**Room 1 — The Entryway:** A small antechamber with hooks for cloaks, a boot rack (empty), and a shelf holding a dusty but intact set of alchemist's goggles (non-magical) and a pair of magitek work gloves (provide a +2 circumstance bonus to Craft [Alchemy] checks).

**Room 2 — The Living Quarters:** A combination sleeping area, sitting room, and kitchen. The bed is a narrow cot with a wool blanket that crumbles at a touch. The sitting area contains a reading chair, a side table, and a small shelf of personal effects — a wooden comb, a hand mirror (cracked), and a clay cup with the faded image of a bird painted on it. The kitchen area has a pantry (contents long since spoiled and desiccated) and a magitek heating plate that still functions, though it takes several minutes to warm up. The room feels lived-in rather than merely occupied — Tana spent real time here, not just working hours.

A sealed chest beneath the cot (Disable Device DC 28) contains 800 gp in mixed coinage (old Auzran mintage, still valuable), a pouch of preserved diamond dust (worth 500 gp as a spell component), and two randomly determined magic items appropriate for the party's level (roll on the appropriate table or select items useful to the party's composition).

**Room 3 — The Alchemical and Magitek Laboratory:** A large workshop dominated by two long benches, one fitted for alchemical work (burners, retorts, distillation apparatus — all magitek-powered and still functional) and the other for magitek fabrication (precision tools, calibration instruments, a magnification device). The reagent cabinets are empty — anything unsealed has degraded to dust over five centuries. The sealed cabinets contain a few intact components: three vials of universal solvent (worth 50 gp each), a block of mithral the size of a fist (worth 1,000 gp), and a magitek power cell (fully charged, can power a Medium magitek device for approximately one month).

**Room 4 — The Arcane Laboratory and Library:** The largest room, and the one that matters most. A broad desk faces a wall of shelving filled with books, scrolls, and bound journals — Tana's personal research library, preserved by the sealed environment. The collection is substantial but not vast — perhaps two hundred volumes, carefully curated, covering arcane theory, planar mechanics, the history of the Nine Worlds, and Tana's own research notes. A Knowledge (Arcana) DC 15 check confirms that this is a working scholar's library, not a display collection — every book shows signs of use, with marginalia, bookmarks, and cross-references throughout.

On the desk sits a **magitek recording device** — a flat metal plate the size of a dinner plate, with a slot on one side sized to accept thin metal cards. Three metal cards are stacked beside the device. The device and cards are in perfect condition. A Knowledge (Engineering) or Knowledge (Arcana) DC 15 check identifies the device as a magitek voice recorder — the cards are slotted into the device, which plays back inscribed audio recordings. The operation is intuitive; even without the Knowledge check, a character who experiments with the device for a few minutes will discover how it works.

**The Recordings**

The three cards contain audio diaries recorded by Tana in the years immediately following their return to Auzra after the war. The recordings should be read aloud in full — they are the party's first direct encounter with the person Tana was before the corruption, and the contrast between this voice and the paranoid tyrant of Castle Perilous is essential to the chapter's emotional arc.

**Recording One:**

> *The voice is clear, precise, and tired — not the exhaustion of a single sleepless night but the deep weariness of someone who has been carrying something too heavy for too long. It speaks in the cadence of a scholar accustomed to dictating notes, but the content is not academic.*
>
> *"Day ninety-three. The eastern chain array is failing. I inspected it personally — three of the five anchor pylons have stress fractures in the magitek housing, and the resonance pattern in the fourth suggests it will follow within the year. If that chain breaks, the Second and Third Islands drift apart, and the atmospheric dynamics of the gap will make reconnection impossible without resources we do not have. I have spent the last two days recalculating options. I believe I can convert the array to a purely arcane binding — rune-etched chain links replacing the magitek components. It will be weaker, less elegant, and it requires me to spend three weeks on-site carving runes instead of addressing the seventeen other critical failures on my list. But it will hold. The magitek approach is better engineering. The arcane approach is what I can actually do with the materials available."*
>
> *A pause. The sound of something being set down — a cup, perhaps, or a book.*
>
> *"I keep thinking I should convert everything. All of it. Strip out the magitek and rebuild on traditional arcane foundations. The infrastructure was brilliant — I am not diminishing what it was — but it was built by a civilization with resources, expertise, and manufacturing capacity that no longer exist. What remains is failing faster than I can repair it, and every repair I make is a stopgap, not a solution. If I start over with arcane methods, I lose elegance and efficiency but I gain sustainability. I can teach others to maintain arcane systems. I cannot teach them to maintain magitek they do not have the facilities to produce. My spellbook has seven different binding configurations that would work for the chains alone. I keep coming back to it. Everything I need is in that book."*

**Recording Two:**

> *"Day one hundred and forty-one. Used the gliders today to survey the lower tier damage. The atmospheric contamination is worse than my initial readings suggested — the toxic layer has settled and thickened. The dome over Settlement Four is holding, but Five and Six are showing seal degradation. I have ordered the remaining population consolidated into the four best-maintained domes. There are objections. There are always objections. I am too tired to be diplomatic about it.*
>
> *"The gliders are the only reliable transportation left. The teleportation circles are down — three of the five, at least, and I suspect the remaining two are drawing more power than they can sustain. The Hawkmen carry messages between the tiers, bless them, but they cannot move materiel. The gliders are old, they need maintenance I barely have time to perform, but they work. I have four here, and I have been teaching two of the younger scholars to fly them. It is not difficult — the craft are remarkably intuitive. Landing is the tricky part. One of my students put his through the wall of a storehouse yesterday. He was not injured. The storehouse was."*
>
> *A sound that might be laughter, brief and rough, as though the speaker has forgotten how.*
>
> *"I consulted my spellbook again this evening. The atmospheric purification ritual on page two hundred and seven. It would work — I am certain of it — but the material components are beyond what I can acquire. Everything comes back to resources. Everything comes back to what we lost. I keep the book beside me at all times now. Not because I need to reference it constantly — I have most of it committed to memory — but because having it close reminds me that solutions exist. That I have solved problems before. That the person who wrote those solutions is still in here somewhere, under all of this."*

**Recording Three:**

> *"Day two hundred and nine. The chains are holding. The arcane conversion was successful — it will last decades, perhaps centuries, with routine maintenance that any competent arcanist can perform. I should feel something about this. Relief, perhaps. I feel nothing. The next crisis is already waiting.*
>
> *"I have been rereading Veraleth's* Paradoxes of Persistent Enchantment *— the original edition, the one on the third shelf. I keep finding things in it I missed before. Or perhaps I did not miss them — perhaps I understood them differently then, before the war, before I knew what it felt like to watch a world break and try to hold the pieces together with theory and willpower. Veraleth's third chapter — the one on sympathetic resonance in long-duration enchantments — has implications for the dome seals that I had not previously considered. I have marked the relevant passages. I intend to test the application next week."*
>
> *Another pause. Longer this time. When the voice returns, it is quieter.*
>
> *"I am losing time. Not in the practical sense — I know what day it is, I know what needs to be done, I keep my lists and I work through them. I am losing time in the other sense. The sense of it passing. Days blur together. I eat when I remember. I sleep when I cannot stand. I work and I work and I work, and the world does not get better — it gets marginally less worse, which is not the same thing, and the difference is eroding something in me that I do not know how to name. I opened my spellbook this morning to look up a ward configuration and I found a note I had written in the margin years ago — a silly note, something about how a particular spell interaction 'cannot possibly work this way and yet it does, which is the best kind of magic.' I do not remember writing it. I do not remember being the kind of person who would write it. But the handwriting is mine."*
>
> *"I need to sleep. I will review* Paradoxes of Persistent Enchantment *again in the morning. Third shelf. The green binding. I keep forgetting where I put things."*

*GM note: The recordings establish four things the party will need. First, the spellbook's profound importance to Tana — it is not merely a tool but a lifeline, a connection to the person they were. Second, the existence of the gliders and their basic operation. Third, Tana's growing exhaustion and the early signs of the paranoia that will eventually consume them — the isolation, the loss of identity, the inability to trust anything they have not verified personally. Fourth, the title and location of a specific book:* Paradoxes of Persistent Enchantment *by Veraleth, on the third shelf, green binding. The party should be allowed to notice that Tana mentions this volume in both the first and third recordings. If they do not notice, a Perception DC 12 check while reviewing the recordings catches the repetition.*

**The Secret Passage**

The book Tana references — *Paradoxes of Persistent Enchantment* — is on the third shelf of the library, exactly where the recordings describe. It has a distinctive green leather binding, worn but intact. When the book is pulled from the shelf, a mechanism activates: the shelf section swings inward, revealing a narrow stone passage descending at a steep angle into the rock beneath the laboratory.

The passage is approximately eighty feet long and protected by three traps, spaced at intervals designed to stop unauthorized access to whatever lies below.

**Trap 1 — The Stunning Ward:** A pressure plate on the passage floor (Perception DC 24 to spot, Disable Device DC 24 to disarm). When triggered, a burst of electrical energy fills a 15-foot section of the corridor. All creatures in the area must make a Fortitude save (DC 20) or be stunned for 1d4 rounds. The trap resets after 1 minute.

**Trap 2 — The Fire Seal:** A tripwire stretched across the passage at shin height (Perception DC 26 to spot, Disable Device DC 26 to disarm). When triggered, gouts of alchemical fire erupt from nozzles concealed in the walls, dealing 6d6 fire damage to all creatures in a 20-foot section of the corridor (Reflex DC 22 for half). The fire also ignites a residual alchemical coating on the walls that burns for 3 rounds, dealing 2d6 fire damage per round to any creature in the area. The trap does not reset.

**Trap 3 — The Seal:** A rune carved into the floor at the passage's final turn (Perception DC 28 to spot, Disable Device DC 28 to disarm). When triggered, a *wall of force* materializes across the passage, sealing it completely. The wall is permanent until dispelled (CL 15th). This trap is designed not to harm but to deny access — if the first two traps fail to stop an intruder, the seal ensures they cannot reach the hangar. *Disintegrate* or *dispel magic* (CL check DC 26) can remove it.

*GM note: These traps are high-DC for a level 8 party but not impossible, especially with mythic abilities. A party with a dedicated trapfinder should be able to handle them with careful play and some luck. A party without a trapfinder will likely trigger one or more — the stunning ward is the most forgiving (non-lethal, temporary), the fire seal is the most painful, and the wall of force is the most frustrating. Allow creative solutions — a character who has studied the runic lock on the concealed door may recognize similarities in the trap runes (Knowledge [Arcana] DC 22 grants a +2 insight bonus to Perception and Disable Device checks against the traps).*

**The Hangar**

The passage opens into a natural cavern beneath the island's rock — a space roughly sixty feet wide and forty feet deep, with a broad opening on the western face that looks out over open sky. The opening is concealed from outside by an overhang of rock and a subtle illusion (*permanent image*, CL 15th) that makes it appear to be solid cliff face from a distance.

Inside the cavern, secured to the floor by magitek clamps, are four gliders.

> *They are beautiful. Not in the way that weapons are beautiful, or fortresses, or anything designed to intimidate — beautiful in the way that a well-made tool is beautiful, every line serving a purpose, every surface shaped by someone who understood that function and elegance are not opposites. Each glider is approximately fifteen feet long, with a wingspan of twenty feet, constructed from a lightweight metal frame covered in a taut, silvery fabric that shimmers faintly with residual enchantment. The cockpit seats two — a pilot in front, a passenger behind — in a reclined position beneath a transparent canopy. The controls are minimal: a steering yoke, a pair of pedals, and a flat panel of magitek instruments, one of which — a gyroscopic compass set in a brass housing — is still active, its needle pointing steadily toward the nearest large landmass.*

Each glider is equipped with:

- **Magitek propulsion:** A power cell in the engine housing provides up to two hours of powered flight at a speed of approximately 60 miles per hour. After the power cell is depleted, the glider can continue as an unpowered glider for extended periods, riding thermals and wind currents. The power cells are partially charged — each contains roughly 90 minutes of powered flight remaining.
- **Gyroscopic compass:** Points toward the nearest significant landmass and emits a low pulse that increases in frequency as the landmass is approached. The compass functions indefinitely and requires no power.
- **Seating:** Pilot and one passenger. The passenger seat has a basic harness. The pilot seat has a full restraint system.

**Operating the Gliders:** A Knowledge (Engineering) or Knowledge (Arcana) DC 22 check reveals the principles of operation — the controls, the power management, the flight characteristics. Without this check, a character can still fly the glider through experimentation (the controls are intuitive), but they do so without understanding the power cell's limitations or the compass's function.

Basic flight — straight and level, gentle turns, climbing and descending — does not require a skill check. The gliders are designed for ease of use. Any maneuver beyond basic flight — sharp turns, evasive action, flying in formation, navigating through heavy mist or strong winds — requires a Fly check (DC varies by maneuver, typically 15–25). **Landing requires a Fly check (DC 18).** Failure by less than 5 results in a hard landing (1d6 damage to pilot and passenger, minor damage to the glider). Failure by 5 or more results in a crash (3d6 damage, glider is damaged and requires repair before further use). Failure by 10 or more is a catastrophic crash (6d6 damage, glider is destroyed).

*GM note: The gliders transform the party's mobility on Auzra. With four gliders, the party can move independently between tiers, bypass Directorate checkpoints entirely, and reach the upper tier without Hawkmen assistance. This is the intended reward for thorough exploration of the ruin. If the party finds the gliders, the rest of the chapter opens up significantly — they are no longer dependent on the Hawkmen for transportation and can operate on their own timetable. Kerrax, when he sees the gliders, will recognize them immediately — they were used extensively by the Grey Elves before the Directorate outlawed them. He will urge the party to keep them hidden and use them discreetly, as the sight of gliders in the air will draw Directorate attention immediately.*

**If the Party Does Not Explore the Ruin**

If the party chooses to wait for Kerrax's gathering without exploring the ruin, or explores it but cannot access the laboratory (fails the runic lock and defeats or is driven off by the guardian without entering), the Hawkmen provide an alternative solution when the gathering convenes. Six Hawkmen collaborate to construct a large basket — woven from mist-layer vine and reinforced with leather straps — suspended from a wooden frame carried by the six fliers in coordinated formation. The basket can hold the entire party but is uncomfortable, conspicuous, slow (half the speed of Hawkmen flight), and limited to one trip at a time (the carriers need hours of rest between flights). It works, but it makes every transit between tiers a logistical challenge and eliminates the possibility of independent movement.

**Oddfellow's Briefing**

When the gathering convenes, Kerrax introduces the party to one of his most valuable companions: **Oddfellow**, a Hawkman bard and oral historian who has spent decades studying Grey Elf culture from the outside. Oddfellow is unusual among the Hawkmen — slight of build, soft-spoken, with tawny plumage and an academic manner that sits oddly on a people who pride themselves on directness. He speaks fluent Elven with a university accent that he acquired through years of eavesdropping on middle-tier conversations from concealed perches, and he knows more about Grey Elf society than most Grey Elves.

Oddfellow provides the party with a detailed briefing on what they will face on the middle tier:

- **The five islands:** The chained islands are named Turana (outermost, the closest to the mist layer and the safest landing point), Velessi, Ondara, Kallandra (the central island — the seat of the Directorate, the main teleportation circle to the upper tier, and the most heavily surveilled location on the middle tier), and Merath (outermost on the far side, home to the largest of the five universities).
- **Grey Elf society:** Grey Elves are a long-lived people who place the highest value on learning. Academic credentials are the primary form of social currency — a Grey Elf's standing in society is determined largely by their scholarly reputation, institutional affiliation, and published work. This makes the Directorate's control of the universities particularly insidious: by controlling education, they control social mobility, and by controlling social mobility, they control everything.
- **Inter-island travel:** The Grey Elves rarely travel between islands. The chain-bridges are dangerous during quakes, the mist between islands harbors avian predators, and the Directorate requires documentation for all inter-island transit. Most Grey Elves are born, educated, and die on the same island. This insularity works in the party's favor — a stranger on Turana can claim to be from Merath, and no one present will have the personal knowledge to contradict them. However, the Directorate's documentation requirements mean that any formal interaction (entering a university, purchasing regulated goods, booking passage on the chain-bridge) will require identification papers.
- **Kallandra:** Oddfellow is emphatic about the central island. It is the Directorate's stronghold — more proctors per capita than any other island, a censor in permanent residence, checkpoints at every chain-bridge terminus, and a thorough inspection process for anyone arriving from the outer islands. The party should not set foot on Kallandra unless they have an ironclad cover story backed by forged documentation, appropriate disguises, and the nerve to hold up under direct scrutiny from a Directorate agent.

> *"Turana is where you want to start," Oddfellow says, tracing a claw along a rough map scratched into the stone. "It is the furthest from Kallandra, the least watched, the most likely to have residents who have never seen a proctor in person. You land there, you establish your story, you learn the rhythms of the place. From Turana you can reach the other outer islands by chain-bridge without passing through Kallandra. But the moment you need the central island — the teleportation circle, the Directorate archives, anything that matters — you are walking into the teeth of it, and you had better be ready."*

Oddfellow can also help the party prepare forged documents if given time and appropriate materials (Linguistics DC 22 or a successful *fabricate* spell, modified by a day of Oddfellow's guidance which grants a +2 circumstance bonus). He cannot accompany the party to the middle tier — a Hawkman on the chained islands would be arrested on sight — but he will be available for consultation if the party returns to the mist layer between forays.

*GM note: Oddfellow's briefing replaces the intelligence-gathering that the party would otherwise need to do in the field. The party should arrive on the middle tier with a clear picture of the social landscape, the island layout, and the dangers of Kallandra. What they do not know — and what the middle tier scenes are designed to reveal — is where the spellbook is hidden and how to reach it. The Hawkmen's oral tradition does not include this information. The party must discover it through investigation on the chained islands themselves.*

---

### Scene 4: The Chained Islands

The middle tier is where the majority of Auzra's Grey Elf population lives — five major islands chained together by enormous links of magitek-reinforced metal, suspended in the mist layer above the toxic lower tier and below the cold upper tier. The islands are large enough to support cities, farmland, and wilderness areas, and the chains that connect them are broad enough to walk across, though doing so during one of the periodic chain-quakes (when the islands pull against their restraints) is extremely dangerous.

If the party recovered the gliders from Tana's Refuge (Scene 3), they can fly themselves to the middle tier, landing on one of the outer islands under cover of mist (Fly DC 18 to land safely). The gliders should be concealed upon landing — Kerrax has warned that they are outlawed, and the sight of one in the air will bring Directorate patrols. If the party is relying on the Hawkmen basket, the transit is slower, more conspicuous, and requires coordination with the six carriers — the basket can only make one trip, and the Hawkmen need a concealed roost on the target island to rest before the return flight. Either way, once the party is on the middle tier, they must navigate the Directorate's territory on foot.

**The Directorate in Practice**

The middle tier operates under a system of control so pervasive that it has become invisible to most of its subjects. The universities — five major institutions, one on each island — are the social and intellectual heart of Grey Elf culture on Auzra. Academic achievement is the highest form of social capital. Scholarly reputation opens doors; ignorance closes them. The Directorate exploits this by controlling what can be studied, who can study it, and what conclusions are permitted. The curricula have been stripped of anything Tana considers dangerous — which, after five centuries of escalating paranoia, means anything of substance. History is sanitized. Arcane theory is truncated. Philosophy is reduced to approved axioms. The universities are magnificent in appearance and hollow in function.

> *The island is beautiful and deeply wrong. The city rises from manicured terraces — white stone, arched bridges, gardens maintained with obvious care — and the people who walk its streets are well-dressed, well-spoken, and terrified. You can see it in the way they move: briskly, with purpose, eyes forward, never lingering in a public space longer than necessary. Conversations happen in doorways, in lowered voices, in the gaps between buildings where the Directorate's sight lines do not reach. The university at the island's center is the largest structure you have seen since the citadel on Nexus — a sprawling complex of lecture halls, libraries, and dormitories, and every entrance is staffed by Grey Elves in the Directorate's grey-and-silver livery, checking credentials and noting names.*

**Directorate Enforcers**

The Directorate's enforcement arm consists of Grey Elf agents organized in three tiers:

- **Watchers:** Low-level informants embedded in the general population. Not magically compelled — they serve willingly, motivated by fear, ambition, or genuine belief in the Directorate's mission. They report suspicious behavior and monitor public gatherings. Unreliable as individuals, effective as a network.
- **Proctors:** Mid-level agents responsible for enforcement. Magically compelled via a *geas/quest* effect renewed annually at Castle Perilous. Proctors carry out arrests, conduct interrogations, and manage the transport of "talented" individuals to the upper tier academy. They believe their loyalty is genuine. They are wrong. A *break enchantment* (DC 24) or *remove curse* (CL 15th) can end the compulsion, but the subject must be willing or helpless, and the caster must succeed against the spell's high caster level.
- **Censors:** Senior agents who answer directly to Tana's inner circle. Magically compelled at a higher level (*geas/quest* at CL 20th, requiring *miracle* or *wish* to break, or *break enchantment* at DC 30). Censors oversee the universities, control inter-island transport, and have authority to order executions. There are fewer than twenty across all five islands.

**Blending In**

The party must establish a way to move within Grey Elf society. Unless the entire party consists of elves — unlikely — their appearances will make them instantly noticeable as outsiders. Grey Elves are insular by nature and the Directorate has spent centuries cultivating xenophobia; a human, dwarf, or halfling walking the streets of Turana will draw stares, questions, and eventually a proctor's attention.

The party has several options for managing this:

- **Magical disguise:** *Disguise self*, *alter self*, *veil*, or similar spells can conceal non-elf party members. The disguise must be maintained for the entire duration of the party's time on the middle tier — potentially days. Spells with short durations (minutes per level) require constant recasting, which creates its own risk. *Hat of disguise* or similar permanent items are ideal. Oddfellow can advise on appropriate Grey Elf dress, mannerisms, and speech patterns (his coaching grants a +2 circumstance bonus to Disguise checks made to pass as a Grey Elf).
- **Scholar's exemption:** Grey Elf society, for all its paranoia, retains a vestigial respect for learning. A charcter who can convincingly present themselves as a visiting scholar — a specialist in some obscure field that the local academics would find intriguing — can move through the university districts with minimal scrutiny. This requires forged academic credentials (Linguistics DC 22, modified by Oddfellow's assistance) and a Knowledge check in the claimed specialty (DC 20) to survive casual conversation with actual scholars. The cover story works best on the outer islands, where contact with non-elves is rare enough to be exotic rather than suspicious.

*GM note: The disguise requirement is not meant to be punitive — it is meant to make the party feel the weight of the Directorate's control. The specific approach matters less than the fact that the party must think about it, plan for it, and maintain it. If a player comes up with a creative solution not listed here, reward it. The point is engagement with the social environment, not a specific mechanical outcome.*

**The Party's Objectives on the Middle Tier**

The party arrives on the middle tier knowing that they need Tana's spellbook — but not where it is. The Hawkmen's oral tradition preserves the knowledge that the spellbook was hidden somewhere on the lower tier by a Grey Elf resistance group after Tana's corruption, but the specifics have been lost over five centuries. The party needs to find out where the spellbook is hidden before they can retrieve it. They also need allies for the eventual approach to the upper tier.

Their objectives are:

1. **Discover the spellbook's location:** The party must piece together clues from multiple sources on the middle tier to determine where on the lower tier the spellbook is hidden — and specifically, which dome or ruin contains it.
2. **Find a route to the lower tier:** Descent is controlled by the Directorate. The official route is a series of magitek lift platforms on Kallandra, accessible only with Directorate authorization. The party can also reach the lower tier by glider, by Hawkmen basket, or — as described below — by getting themselves exiled.
3. **Build alliances for the approach to the upper tier:** Whether the party chooses the uprising path or the stealth path later, they need contacts on the middle tier who can provide information, resources, or direct assistance.

**Finding the Spellbook: Three Sources**

The spellbook's location can be discovered through three sources on the middle tier, each providing a different level of detail. The party does not need all three, but each source narrows the search.

**Source 1 — The University Historians:** The universities on the outer islands contain fragments of uncensored history, buried in archives that the Directorate has neglected rather than purged. A successful Diplomacy check (DC 20) or Gather Information check (DC 18) in the university districts on Turana or Merath identifies academics who have quiet interests in pre-Directorate history. These scholars can confirm the general story — that a Grey Elf resistance group stole the spellbook and hid it on the lower tier — but they do not know the specific location. Their records indicate only that the resistance operated primarily in the southern domes and that the spellbook was moved "beyond the reach of the Directorate's eyes," which the historians interpret as meaning outside the domed settlements, in the toxic zone.

**Source 2 — The Velessi Memorial:** On the island of Velessi, in a neglected corner of the public gardens, there is a small memorial stone. It is not labeled — the Directorate removed the plaque decades ago — but the stone itself is carved with a list of names, and older residents of Velessi know that these are the names of the resistance members who were caught and executed after hiding the spellbook. A Gather Information check (DC 22) on Velessi, or a tip from Professor Aelindra Moonwhisper (if the party has already made contact with her through the reconciliation catalyst), leads the party to the memorial. The names on the stone are a historical record, and cross-referencing them with university archives (Knowledge [History] DC 20 or a day of research with academic access) reveals that the resistance cell was based in Dome 6 on the lower tier — a settlement that has since been depopulated and sealed due to structural failures in the dome's atmospheric systems. The spellbook is confirmed to be on the lower tier, and Dome 6 is identified as the resistance's base of operations, but the specific hiding place within or near the dome is not recorded.

**Source 3 — The Tavern Song:** If the party visits a working-class tavern on one of the outer islands — not the university districts, but the chain-bridge way stations and windward taverns where laborers, sky-farmers, and maintenance workers drink — they may hear a folk song. A Gather Information check (DC 15) or simply spending an evening in such a tavern leads to a performance by **Tinuvel**, a Grey Elf bard of no particular distinction who plays the outer-island tavern circuit for tips and beer money. Among Tinuvel's repertoire is a somewhat ribald folk song called *"They Couldn't Get It Back"* — a comic ballad about a wise old man who steals a spellbook from a stuffy wizard, hides it where the wizard's servants cannot find it, and laughs himself to death in his exile dome while the wizard's agents search everywhere but the right place.

> *Tinuvel's voice is pleasant enough, if unremarkable, and the song draws laughter from the regulars who clearly know it well — they join in on the chorus, which involves increasingly creative rhymes for "dome" and "home." The melody is simple, repetitive, the kind of thing that lodges in the skull and refuses to leave. The lyrics are not subtle. The wise old man is plainly the resistance leader. The stuffy wizard is plainly Tana. And in the fourth verse, the old man hides the book "beneath the broken crown, where even wizards won't look down" — a line the regulars deliver with particular relish, as though getting something past the Directorate's censors, even in a tavern song, is a victory worth savoring.*

In order to put the clues in the song together, the PCs must succeed in a DC24 Perform or Linguistics check. If deciphered, the song provides the specific detail the other sources lack: the spellbook is hidden beneath Dome 6. Combined with the historians' general confirmation and the Velessi memorial's identification of the resistance cell's base, the party now has a complete picture.

*GM note: The three sources are designed to reward different playstyles. A party that focuses on academic investigation will find the historians and the memorial. A party that prioritizes social engagement and street-level interaction will find the tavern song. A party that does both will have redundant confirmation and arrive on the lower tier with confidence.
