If the party finds none of the three — possible but unlikely, given the low DCs on Sources 1 and 3 — they arrive on the lower tier knowing only that the spellbook is "somewhere down there," which makes the lower-tier scenes significantly harder but not impossible (the residents of Dome 4 can point them toward Dome 6 once they arrive).*

**Reaching Kallandra**

If the party needs to reach the central island — for the teleportation circle, the Directorate archives, or any other reason — they must pass through the most heavily surveilled location on the middle tier. Kallandra's chain-bridge termini are staffed by Directorate checkpoints, and every person entering the island is subject to documentation review, identity verification, and — for anyone flagged as unusual — a brief interview with a proctor.

Passing the checkpoint requires forged documentation (already prepared with Oddfellow's assistance) and a Bluff check (DC 28) to withstand the proctor's scrutiny. The DC is deliberately high — this is not a routine checkpoint but a border crossing into the Directorate's stronghold, and the proctors assigned to Kallandra are the best the organization has. Failure does not necessarily mean combat; a failed check by less than 5 results in the party being turned away ("documentation irregularity — return to your island of origin and reapply"), while failure by 5 or more results in detention and interrogation (requiring escape or rescue). Failure by 10 or more means the proctor recognizes the documentation as forged and raises a general alarm.

The party can bypass the checkpoints entirely if they have gliders (approaching from the air and landing on an unmonitored section of the island, Fly DC 20 to land in the narrow space available) or if they have Hawkmen allies willing to guide them through a maintenance access point on the island's underside (Climb DC 18, or flight).

**The Exile Route**

The party has an additional route to the lower tier that bypasses the Directorate's lift platforms entirely: they can get caught on purpose and exiled.

Exile to the lower tier is the Directorate's standard punishment for offenses that do not warrant execution — public dissent, unlicensed arcane practice, possession of banned texts, or simply being inconvenient. The exiled are stripped of possessions (confiscated by the Directorate and warehoused on Kallandra), fitted with a tracking anklet (magitek device, easily removed with *dispel magic* or Disable Device DC 20 once on the lower tier), and transported to the lower tier via the magitek lift platforms. The exile process takes approximately one day from arrest to transport.

A party that deliberately provokes exile — by getting caught with banned texts, publicly challenging the Directorate's historical narrative, or simply allowing their cover to fail — will be arrested, processed, and sent down. The advantages: it is the easiest route to the lower tier, requires no skill checks, and deposits the party inside the dome system rather than in the toxic zone outside. The disadvantages: the party loses their equipment during processing (it must be recovered later or replaced), the tracking anklets must be dealt with, and the Directorate records the exile — meaning the party's presence on the lower tier is known, and any return to the middle tier will be met with heightened security.

*GM note: The exile route is a legitimate option that some parties will find appealing, particularly if they have struggled with the middle tier's social challenges. Do not punish them for choosing it — the equipment loss and tracking anklets are complications, not deal-breakers, and a resourceful party will solve both quickly. The real cost is strategic: the Directorate knows they are on the lower tier, which accelerates the timeline for the censor and the khalahathi guardians in Scene 5 to be on alert.*

**Encounter: Directorate Patrol**

The party's presence on the middle tier will eventually draw attention. A Directorate patrol — 1 proctor and 4 watchers — confronts them within the first day, typically at a checkpoint or transport hub where identification is required. The encounter can play out several ways:

- **Deception:** Forged credentials (Linguistics DC 22 or a successful *disguise self* / *alter self* with a Bluff check opposed by the proctor's Sense Motive) allow the party to pass. The proctor is compelled to follow procedure, and procedure says that valid credentials are sufficient.
- **Bribery/Persuasion:** Watchers can be bribed (Diplomacy DC 20 or 50 gp) to look the other way. Proctors cannot be bribed — the compulsion prevents it — but they can be misdirected (Bluff DC 24) into believing the party has legitimate business.
- **Confrontation:** If the party's cover is blown, the proctor calls for reinforcements (2 additional proctors arrive in 1d4+1 rounds). Proctors are 5th-level Grey Elf fighters with standardized equipment (longsword, chain shirt, *wand of hold person* with 10 charges). The watchers flee. The party can win this fight easily at level 9, but the noise and attention are the real cost — every fight on the middle tier tightens the Directorate's net.
- **Escape:** The Hawkmen do not provide the party with an overt escort on the middle tier — a visible Hawkman presence would compromise the party's cover immediately. Instead, Kerrax stations hidden warriors at strategic points on each island: concealed roosts on cliff faces, drainage tunnels, disused maintenance shafts. The party is told the locations of these fallback points before they depart for the middle tier. If the party needs to flee, they can reach a hidden roost within minutes and signal for extraction — but they must reach it on their own. The Hawkmen will intervene directly only if the party is in mortal danger, and even then, their intervention blows the party's cover on that island permanently.

*GM note: The middle tier should feel like a spy thriller. The party is behind enemy lines in a police state, and the tension comes not from combat power — they are more than capable of fighting through Directorate patrols — but from the consequences of being discovered. Every fight raises the alert level. Every alert makes the next phase harder. Encourage the party to use social skills, magic, and creative thinking rather than force. The Directorate is a system, and systems have vulnerabilities that a sword cannot reach.*

---

### Scene 5: The Lower Tier

The spellbook is hidden on the lower tier — a single great island shrouded in toxic gases, home to domed settlements that house a labor population and a prison colony. Descending to the lower tier is the chapter's most physically dangerous segment, and what the party finds when they arrive is the chapter's most emotionally difficult.

**The Descent**

There are three routes to the lower tier:

**Route 1 — The Hawkmen Passages:** The Hawkmen's unofficial route is a series of natural fissures and tunnels through the underside of the central middle-tier island. The passages are tight, unlit, and unstable — periodic chain-quakes from the islands pulling at their restraints send tremors through the rock, and sections of tunnel have collapsed or shifted since the Hawkmen last used them. The descent takes approximately four hours. At the base of the passages, the party emerges onto the underside of the middle tier — a rocky overhang hundreds of feet above the lower tier island, with nothing between them and the ground below but open air and toxic cloud. If the party has gliders, they can fly down to the lower tier's surface directly (Fly DC 18 to land, with the toxic haze reducing visibility on approach). If using the Hawkmen basket, the carriers can make the descent — it is a relatively short vertical drop compared to the horizontal distances between tiers. Without either, the party needs *fly*, *feather fall*, or similar magic to descend safely. This route deposits the party in the toxic zone outside the domes.

**Route 2 — Glider Descent:** If the party has gliders and prefers a direct approach, they can fly from the middle tier (or the mist layer) down through the toxic cloud to the lower tier's surface. The descent requires a Fly check (DC 18) to land safely, with the toxic haze reducing visibility on approach. This route also deposits the party outside the domes.

**Route 3 — Exile:** If the party chose the exile route (see Scene 4), they are transported via the Directorate's magitek lift platforms on Kallandra and deposited inside the dome system — specifically in Dome 4, the largest and most populated of the active settlements. This route is the easiest physically but carries the complications described in Scene 4 (equipment confiscation, tracking anklets, Directorate awareness).

**The Toxic Atmosphere**

The lower tier's atmosphere is fouled — a lingering effect of the apocalypse that has never healed. The toxic gases are heavier than normal air, settling into a permanent layer that extends from the lower tier's surface to roughly two hundred feet above it. Within this layer:

- Creatures must make a Fortitude save (DC 16) every 10 minutes or take 1d4 Constitution damage. This is a poison effect.
- Visibility is reduced to 60 feet. The gases have a yellowish tinge that worsens with depth.
- The domed settlements are pressurized with filtered air — the interiors are safe. Outside the domes, exposure is constant.
- *Delay poison* suppresses the saves. *Life bubble* creates a 10-foot safe zone. A wet cloth over the mouth provides no benefit — the toxin is absorbed through the skin as well as the lungs.

**The Domed Settlements**

The lower tier's population lives in a series of six domes — translucent magitek structures that filter the toxic atmosphere and maintain breathable air inside. Domes 1 through 4 are active, housing the lower tier's entire population after Tana's consolidation order centuries ago (referenced in Recording Two, Scene 3). Dome 5 is partially sealed — its atmospheric systems are failing, and the remaining residents have been relocated to Dome 4. Dome 6 is abandoned entirely — sealed, depressurized, its atmospheric systems offline, its interior exposed to the toxic gases. It is in Dome 6 that the spellbook is hidden.

The population is a mix of Grey Elves born here (the labor caste, who produce food and manufactured goods for the upper tiers), exiles sent down as punishment, and a small number of Hawkmen prisoners. There is no Directorate presence on the lower tier — the toxic atmosphere and the controlled supply chain are sufficient prison. The people here are not oppressed by enforcers; they are oppressed by geography.

> *The inside of the dome is worse than the outside, and the outside was poison. The air is breathable — the filtration systems see to that — but it carries the smell of too many people in too small a space with too little of everything. The streets, if they can be called streets, are narrow paths between structures that were never meant to be permanent and have become permanent by default — prefabricated shelters patched with scavenged metal, lean-tos built against the dome's inner curve, communal cooking pits venting smoke that has nowhere to go but up, where it gathers against the translucent ceiling in a brown haze that dims the already feeble light filtering through from outside. The people match their surroundings. They are thin. They are tired. Their clothing is worn past the point where mending helps and into the territory where fabric simply gives up. Children play in the drainage channels because there is nowhere else to play. The elderly sit in doorways and cough — a wet, persistent sound that comes from years of breathing recycled air through filtration systems that were designed for a fraction of the current population. There is no sanitation system beyond the channels themselves, which carry waste to a processing unit near the dome's edge that was built to serve a few hundred people and now serves several thousand. The smell is the first thing that hits you. The hopelessness is the second.*

The Hawkmen prisoners are the most visible sign of the Directorate's cruelty. There are perhaps two dozen of them across the active domes — Hawkmen captured in raids or border skirmishes, exiled rather than executed, and subjected to a particular punishment that the party will notice immediately: their wings are magically bound. A *dimensional anchor*-derived enchantment locks each wing in its folded position, tight against the back, preventing extension or flight. The binding does not cause pain — it is worse than pain. It is the removal of the thing that makes a Hawkman a Hawkman. The bound prisoners move through the dome with a hunched, diminished posture, instinctively trying to extend wings that will not open, their faces carrying the particular blank expression of people who have been robbed of something they cannot articulate and that no one around them understands.

If the party includes Hawkmen allies — and Kerrax will insist on sending at least a small escort for the lower-tier descent — the reaction to the bound prisoners is immediate and visceral. The escort leader, a scarred female warrior named **Seela**, stops mid-stride when she sees the first bound Hawkman. Her expression cycles through shock, fury, and a cold, locked-down rage that is more frightening than any outburst.

> *She does not speak. She walks to the bound Hawkman — a young male, thin and hollow-eyed, who flinches when she approaches because he has learned to flinch when anyone approaches — and she takes his face in her hands with a gentleness that is completely at odds with the killing fury in her eyes. She examines the binding. She traces the enchantment's rune-work with one claw, delicately, the way a surgeon traces an incision. Then she lets go, steps back, and turns to the party. When she speaks, her voice is perfectly level.*
>
> *"I will free them. All of them. Before we leave this place, I will free every one of them, or I will die here. If you want my help with your spellbook, you will help me with this first. That is not a request."*

*GM note: Seela's demand is non-negotiable and should be treated as such. The wing bindings can be removed by* dispel magic *(CL check DC 20),* break enchantment *(DC 22), or* remove curse *(CL 15th). A party with access to these spells can free the prisoners relatively quickly. A party without them will need to find an alternative — the lower tier's residents include a few Grey Elf exiles with minor arcane skill who can attempt* dispel magic *at a lower caster level, or the party can seek out the binding rune's anchor point (a magitek device in each dome's administrative center) and destroy it, which releases all bindings in that dome simultaneously but raises an alarm that the Directorate's censor will detect. Either way, freeing the Hawkmen is a scene that should carry weight — each freed prisoner's wings unfold slowly, painfully, like blood returning to a numbed limb, and the sound they make is not triumph. It is grief. They have been grounded for years. The sky is right there, visible through the dome, and they cannot reach it until they leave.*

**Arrival at Dome 4**

No matter which route the party takes to the lower tier, they enter the dome system at Dome 4 — the largest, most populated, and most functional of the active settlements. If they descended via the Hawkmen passages or gliders, the external airlocks on Dome 4's southern face are the closest safe entry point. If they arrived via exile, the Directorate's lift platform deposits them in Dome 4's central receiving area.

Their arrival causes an uproar. Outsiders have not set foot on the lower tier in living memory — the last visitors anyone can recall were a Directorate inspection team decades ago, and those were Grey Elves in uniforms, not a mixed-race party of adventurers carrying mythic power and accompanied by Hawkmen warriors. Within an hour of their arrival, the party has drawn a crowd. Within two hours, they are the most famous people on the lower tier.

> *Word travels fast in a dome. It has to — there is nothing else to do, nothing else to talk about, nothing that has happened here in decades that qualifies as news. You arrived thirty minutes ago and already the crowd is three deep around you, pressing in with a desperate curiosity that is not quite hope and not quite suspicion but something in between — the expression of people who have been told for so long that nothing will change that they have stopped believing change is possible, but who cannot look away from the possibility that they might be wrong. Children are pushed to the front. An elderly woman reaches out and touches the nearest PC's arm, then pulls her hand back as if burned. A young man with the hollow cheeks and oversized eyes of someone raised on insufficient nutrition asks, very quietly, if you are from the upper tier. When you say no — when you say you are from outside, from other worlds, from the Rainbow Road — the crowd goes silent in a way that is more unsettling than the noise.*

The party becomes near-instant celebrities. The residents of Dome 4 have been forgotten by the upper tiers for so long that the arrival of anyone — let alone heroes from outside the world — is an event of staggering significance. People bring them food (what little they have), offer them sleeping space, ask them questions about the outside that reveal how completely isolated the lower tier has become. Some of the younger residents do not fully believe that the upper tier exists — they have never seen it, and the exiles' stories sound like fairy tales.

**The Council**

Dome 4 is governed by a council of seven — elected by the residents, in theory, though in practice the positions rotate among the same families who have held them for generations. The council president is **Mirovon**, a Grey Elf of middle years with a bureaucrat's careful manner and a politician's instinct for self-preservation. Mirovon was installed in the presidency by the Directorate — not through compulsion magic (there is no censor permanently stationed in the domes) but through the simpler mechanism of implied threat. The Directorate controls the supply chain: food, medicine, replacement parts for the atmospheric systems. Mirovon's job is to keep the lower tier quiet, and in exchange, the supplies keep flowing. He is not evil. He is a man who has made a rational calculation about survival and has been making it for so long that he has forgotten there was ever another option.

The council convenes an emergency session within hours of the party's arrival. Mirovon is visibly nervous — the party's presence threatens the equilibrium he has maintained for years, and he does not know what the Directorate's response will be. The session is held in Dome 4's central hall — a converted cargo warehouse, the largest interior space available — and the entire population of Dome 4 packs in to watch.

The council's questions are practical: Who are you? Why are you here? What do you want? How long will you stay? What happens to us when you leave? Mirovon steers the conversation toward reassurance — he wants the party to do whatever they came to do and leave as quickly as possible, before the Directorate notices. The other council members are more divided. Some share Mirovon's caution. Others — particularly the exiles, who have nothing left to lose — see the party as the first real opportunity for change in their lifetimes and push for active cooperation.

*GM note: The council scene is a roleplaying encounter, not a skill challenge. Let the party make their case. Mirovon will agree to anything that does not openly defy the Directorate — he will provide directions, supplies, and labor support, but he will not authorize actions that the Directorate could interpret as rebellion. The exiles on the council are more willing to take risks but have less institutional authority. The party does not need the council's permission to act — no one on the lower tier has the power to stop them — but having the council's cooperation makes logistics easier and establishes the party's reputation for the resistance contact that follows.*

**The Resistance**

After the council session, as the crowd disperses and Mirovon retreats to his offices, the party is approached by a Grey Elf named **Manos**. He is middle-aged, quiet, with the calloused hands of a manual laborer and the watchful eyes of someone accustomed to not being noticed. He asks to speak with the party privately — not in the central hall, not in any public space, but in a maintenance alcove near the dome's atmospheric processing unit, where the noise of the machinery covers conversation.

Manos is an agent of the dome's small active resistance cell. He does not volunteer this immediately — he needs to assess whether the party can be trusted. The interaction is roleplay-driven: Manos asks careful, indirect questions about the party's intentions, their knowledge of the upper tier, their opinion of the Directorate. He is looking for sincerity, not correct answers. A character who is forthright about their mission (destroy the Warlock's power, free the worlds) earns Manos's trust faster than one who tries to be clever. Bluff checks actively work against the party here — Manos has spent his entire life among people who lie to survive, and he can smell deception. A failed Bluff check (opposed by Manos's Sense Motive +14) ends the conversation; Manos excuses himself and does not return. A successful Diplomacy check (DC 18) or genuine transparency about the party's mission and purpose earns his confidence.

If the party gains Manos's trust, he takes them to the resistance.

**Tirion and the Resistance Cell**

The resistance operates out of a concealed space beneath Dome 4's water reclamation facility — a series of connected maintenance tunnels that have been quietly expanded over decades into a livable, if cramped, meeting area. The cell consists of roughly a dozen members: exiles, second-generation lower-tier residents, and a few Hawkmen prisoners whose bindings have been partially loosened (not removed — the resistance lacks the magical skill, but they have found ways to reduce the enchantment's grip enough for limited wing movement in enclosed spaces).

The resistance leader is **Tirion**. He is extremely old — ancient by Grey Elf standards, which means he has seen things that most of the people alive on any tier have only read about. He was one of the first people exiled to the lower tier, centuries ago, sent down for the crime of questioning the Directorate's educational policies. Before his exile, he was a skilled engineer and a member of Tana's working group — one of the team of scholars and builders who maintained Auzra's infrastructure in the years immediately following the apocalypse. He knew Tana personally, before the corruption became total. He is the only person alive on the lower tier who can say that.

> *The old man is sitting in a chair that has been repaired so many times it is more patch than original, and he does not stand when you enter because standing takes him a while these days and he has learned to conserve his energy for things that matter. His face is a map of the lower tier's history — lines carved by decades of recycled air and insufficient light, eyes that are still sharp beneath brows that have gone entirely white. His hands, resting on the chair's arms, are steady. He studies you the way a scholar studies a primary source: carefully, methodically, looking for what is real beneath the surface.*
>
> *"Manos says you are from outside," he says. His voice is thin but precise, every word chosen. "He says you carry the old power. He says you want to reach the spellbook." A pause. He looks at each of you in turn. "I hid that book. Not personally — I was already down here by then — but I planned the operation. I chose the location. I recruited the team. Seven of them went up to do it. Three came back. The Directorate caught the other four and executed them on the middle tier as an example." Another pause. "Their names are on a stone on Velessi, if anyone still remembers to look."*

Tirion provides the party with three critical pieces of information:

1. **The spellbook's exact location:** It is in a sealed vault in the central tower of a ruined Grey Elf outpost approximately three miles from Dome 4, deep in the toxic zone. The outpost was chosen because it was already forgotten, and the vault was sealed with an *arcane lock* that Tirion's people cast at the highest level they could manage. He provides precise directions.

2. **The censor and the guardians:** Tirion knows that the Directorate assigned a censor to the lower tier specifically to guard the ruins — the order came down within years of the spellbook's hiding, which means someone on the upper tier knew or suspected. The censor is refreshed periodically (sent up and replaced), but the posting has always been filled. The censor's guardians — the khalahathi — arrived later, and Tirion suspects they are not what the Directorate thinks they are. "Bound outsiders," he says with contempt. "The Directorate does not bind outsiders. The Directorate can barely manage a *sending* spell. Those things serve something else."

3. **The tunnel network:** Tirion's resistance has spent decades excavating and mapping a secret tunnel network that passes beneath the domes, connecting them through the bedrock of the lower tier's island. The primary route runs from Dome 4 to Dome 6, emerging behind the main entrance — inside the sealed dome, bypassing the external airlocks that the censor monitors. This is the safest route to the spellbook: it avoids the toxic zone entirely until the party reaches Dome 6 itself, and it bypasses the censor's exterior patrols.

> *"The tunnels are not safe," Tirion says, and the way he says it — flat, factual, the tone of an engineer describing a structural deficiency — makes it clear that this is not a figure of speech. "We dug them over many years, and we did what we could to shore them up, but the rock down here is unstable. There are collapses. There are gas pockets. And there are things in the deeper sections — creatures that came up from below, or were already there when we started digging. We have lost people in those tunnels. But the route to Dome 6 is the best-mapped section, and if you follow the markers, you will reach it."*

Tirion asks one thing in return for his help: when the party goes to Dome 6, they bring a resistance escort to help evacuate any salvageable equipment or supplies from the abandoned settlement. The resistance has known about Dome 6's contents for years but has never had the combat capability to clear the tunnel of its inhabitants and hold it long enough for a salvage operation. The party provides that capability.

**The Tunnel Network**

The resistance tunnels are a rough-hewn network carved through the bedrock beneath the lower tier's island. The main route from Dome 4 to Dome 6 is approximately two miles long, running at a depth of roughly fifty feet below the surface. The tunnels are narrow — five feet wide, seven feet tall — with wooden shoring at intervals and phosphorescent fungus on the walls providing dim light. The resistance has marked the route with carved directional symbols at every intersection: a circle with an arrow indicating the correct path, and an X marking dead ends or dangerous sections.

Despite the markers, the tunnels are disorienting. Side passages branch off at irregular intervals, some leading to other domes, some to natural caverns, some to dead ends where the digging stopped due to unstable rock or gas pockets. The party must navigate the route using a series of Survival checks.

**Navigation:**

- The tunnel route to Dome 6 requires **four Survival checks** (DC 15), one at each major intersection where the correct path is not immediately obvious.
- Success means the party follows the correct branch. Failure means they take a wrong turn and lose 30 minutes before reaching a dead end or recognizable landmark and backtracking.
- Failure by 5 or more leads the party into an unstable section — a gas pocket (Fortitude DC 16, 1d4 Con damage, poison), a partial collapse (Reflex DC 16 or 2d6 bludgeoning and pinned, Strength DC 20 to free), or a flooded section (Swim DC 15, 30 feet of murky water).
- A character with Knowledge (Dungeoneering) DC 18 can identify the correct path at each intersection without a Survival check, recognizing the geological indicators that the resistance diggers followed.
- Tirion's escort (2 resistance members) provides a +4 circumstance bonus to all Survival checks in the tunnels. If the party insists on going alone, they lose this bonus.

**Random Encounters: The Tunnels**

The deeper sections of the tunnel network are infested with demons — creatures that have seeped up from below or been drawn to the lower tier by the same corrupted energies that maintain the toxic atmosphere. The resistance has learned to avoid the worst sections, but the route to Dome 6 passes through territory that cannot be entirely circumvented.

Roll on the following table once per navigation segment (four segments total). On a result of 1–3, no encounter. On a result of 4–6, an encounter occurs.

| d6 | Encounter |
|---|---|
| 4 | **1d4 phankhonvrani** (CR 1 each) — Small, skittering creatures that infest the side tunnels. They attack in swarms, targeting the last person in the marching order. More nuisance than threat at the party's level, but their shrieks echo through the tunnels and may attract worse things. |
| 5 | **1d2 aakarshaki** (CR 4 each) — Lurkers that cling to the tunnel ceiling, dropping onto their prey from above. Perception DC 20 to spot before they attack. Their preferred tactic is to grapple a single target and drag them into a side passage. |
| 6 | **1 mautchidaya** (CR 8) — A solitary predator that has claimed a section of tunnel as its territory. It blocks the route forward and must be fought or driven off. Its lair contains the remains of previous victims — resistance members who went missing in the tunnels over the years. Among the remains: 200 gp in personal effects, a *cloak of resistance +2*, and a hand-drawn map of the tunnel network that grants a +2 bonus to all remaining navigation checks. |

*GM note: The tunnel encounters are meant to create tension and drain resources, not to seriously threaten a level 9 party with mythic tier 3. The real danger of the tunnels is getting lost — each wrong turn costs time, and time on the lower tier is a limited resource. If the party has already freed the Hawkmen prisoners (Seela's demand), the Directorate's censor may be on alert, which adds urgency. The resistance escort makes the navigation significantly easier; a party that refuses the escort out of pride or paranoia will have a harder time and should feel the consequences. The mautchidaya encounter is the most significant — it is a real fight in a confined space, and the cloak and map in its lair are worthwhile rewards for a thorough search.*

**Dome 6**

The tunnels emerge in Dome 6's sub-level — a maintenance space beneath the settlement's central plaza, accessed through a concealed hatch that the resistance installed decades ago. The hatch opens into darkness. Dome 6's atmospheric systems are offline, and the interior is filled with the same toxic gases as the surface outside. The party must use the same protections here as they would in the open air (Fortitude DC 16 every 10 minutes, *delay poison*, *life bubble*, etc.).

Dome 6 is a ghost town. The structures are intact — the dome's shell protected them from weather and the worst of the atmospheric degradation — but everything organic has decayed. Furniture is dust. Fabric is threads. The streets are empty, the buildings dark, and the silence is absolute except for the faint hiss of toxic gas seeping through cracks in the dome's deteriorating seals. The resistance escort moves through the settlement with practiced efficiency, marking salvageable equipment and materials for later retrieval.

The central tower — the ruin where the spellbook is hidden — stands at the settlement's eastern edge, partially collapsed but structurally sound in its lower levels. It was a Grey Elf administrative building before the dome was abandoned, repurposed by the resistance as a hiding place because its vault was already built for secure storage.

**Encounter: The Censor's Hounds**

The ruin is not unguarded. A Directorate censor — the only one assigned to the lower tier, a Grey Elf named **Vaelith** — has been tasked with ensuring that no one accesses the ruins outside the active domes. Vaelith does not know why this particular location is important; the order came down centuries ago and has been renewed with each successive censor. They enforce it through a pair of bound creatures — **2 khalahathi** (CR 10) — demons that the Directorate believes are summoned constructs, maintained by the censor's magical authority.

If the party reached Dome 6 via the tunnels, they have bypassed Vaelith's exterior surveillance. The censor is stationed in a small magitek shelter outside Dome 6's main airlock, monitoring the external approaches. The khalahathi, however, patrol both outside and inside the dome — one circuits the exterior ruins, and one patrols the dome's interior on a regular route that brings it past the central tower every fifteen minutes.

- **Enemies:** 2 khalahathi (CR 10). One exterior (irrelevant if the party entered via tunnels), one interior. The interior khalahathi is alert and aggressive.
- **Vaelith:** A 7th-level Grey Elf wizard, compelled by *geas/quest* (CL 20th). If they detect the party (via the interior khalahathi raising an alarm, or via their own monitoring of the dome's remaining magitek sensors), they will attempt to flee to the active domes to raise a general alarm. They are not a serious combat threat at the party's level, but the alarm is a strategic concern.
- **Terrain:** Inside Dome 6. Toxic atmosphere (DC 16 Fort saves every 10 minutes). Abandoned buildings provide cover but visibility is limited to 60 feet by the toxic haze inside the dome. The streets are cluttered with debris (difficult terrain in most areas). The central tower's interior is structurally sound but dark — no light sources function.
- **Tactics:** The interior khalahathi fights in the open streets near the tower, activating *displacement* and *mirror image* to absorb initial attacks while closing to melee range. Once engaged, it uses whirlwind of steel to punish clustered opponents — its four-weapon fighting style and 10-foot reach make it devastating against parties that bunch up in the narrow dome streets. Its all-around vision means it cannot be flanked, so rogues and other flanking-dependent characters must find alternative advantages. It is intelligent enough to focus fire on spellcasters. If it detects the party before they reach the tower, it moves to block the entrance. If the party reaches the tower first, it pursues inside.
- **Stealth approach:** The party can time their approach to avoid the interior khalahathi's patrol route (Perception DC 18 to observe the pattern from concealment, requiring 15 minutes of observation). A successful group Stealth check (DC 22) allows the party to reach the tower and enter during the gap in the patrol. The khalahathi makes a Perception check each round against any sounds from inside the tower.
- **Escape hatch:** The party can draw the khalahathi away from the tower with a distraction (noise, illusion, or a visible target at range) while a smaller team enters the tower from the far side through a collapsed wall section. The spellbook is in a sealed vault on the upper level, accessible via a partially collapsed stairway. A fast, stealthy party can potentially retrieve the spellbook without fighting the khalahathi at all, though the risks of the toxic atmosphere and the structural instability create their own pressure.

**The Spellbook**

The vault is a small stone room at the top of the surviving stairway — the resistance leader chose it for its defensibility and its elevation above the worst of the toxic gases. The door is sealed with an *arcane lock* (CL 12th) that has persisted for five centuries. Inside, the room is dry, clean, and remarkably well-preserved — the vault's own construction has kept the toxic gases at bay. The spellbook rests on a stone shelf, wrapped in oilcloth.

> *The book is not what you expected. It is not a grimoire bound in exotic leather, not a tome crackling with visible power. It is a plain volume — soft brown leather, well-used, the edges worn smooth by handling, the spine cracked and re-stitched more than once. When you open it, the first pages are not spells at all. They are notes. A young person's handwriting, cramped and eager, filling the margins with observations, corrections, arguments with themselves. "This cannot be right — check Veraleth's Third Postulate." "Tested this morning. It works. It actually works. Showed Magister Elorn and he said I was wrong. Tested it again. Still works. Showed him the results. He said he was wrong. That was a good day." The spells come later, growing in complexity as the pages progress, but the notes never stop. The person who wrote this book loved magic the way some people love music — not as a tool or a weapon, but as the most interesting thing in the world.*

*GM note: The spellbook is the lever and should be treated with corresponding narrative weight. It is a window into who Tana was before the corruption — a brilliant, passionate, slightly reckless young scholar who delighted in learning and in sharing what they learned. The contrast between this person and the paranoid, hoarding tyrant of Castle Perilous is the emotional engine of the turning scene. Let the party read the notes. Let them form an opinion of the person who wrote them. It will matter when they meet Tana.*

**Milestone:** Level 9 is awarded upon recovering the spellbook.

---

### Scene 6: The Three Catalysts

After recovering the spellbook, the party must reach the upper tier and Castle Perilous. The direct approach — a Hawkmen or glider flight to the upper tier — is possible but extremely dangerous. Castle Perilous is defended by magical wards, arcane artillery emplacements, and elite guards, and an airborne approach will be detected long before the party reaches the walls. The party's chances improve dramatically if they can create a distraction on the middle tier large enough to draw the Directorate's attention and resources downward, away from the upper tier.

Three side quests, each self-contained but interconnected, build toward the possibility of a popular uprising on the middle tier. The party can pursue any or all of them in any order. Each completed quest contributes to the uprising's viability and to Auzra's allied strength in Phase 3.

**Catalyst 1: The Reconciliation**

The Hawkmen and the Grey Elves were partners for millennia before Tana's propaganda turned the Elves against their neighbors. The younger generations of Grey Elves have grown up on stories of Hawkmen savagery — raiders, thieves, primitives who threaten civilized society. The truth is the opposite, and the older Grey Elves — those with long enough memories, or access to uncensored historical records — know it. But knowing and acting are different things when the Directorate punishes dissent.

The party must broker a meeting between Hawkmen representatives and sympathetic Grey Elves on the middle tier. Kerrax can provide three Hawkmen willing to attend — **Velra**, a young female warrior who speaks fluent Elven; **Old Gust**, an elder who remembers the pre-propaganda era through his grandmother's stories; and **Piik**, a Hawkman scout who has secretly traded goods with Grey Elf sky-farmers on the outer island edges for years and has existing contacts.

The Grey Elf side is harder. The party must identify sympathetic individuals — academics who have noticed the gaps in the approved histories, merchants who have benefited from Piik's quiet trade, elderly citizens whose family histories don't match the official narrative. A successful Diplomacy check (DC 22) or Gather Information check (DC 20) in the university districts identifies a potential contact: **Professor Aelindra Moonwhisper**, a historian at the Second Island University who has been quietly archiving banned texts in a hidden room beneath her office. She is frightened, cautious, and aware that what she is doing carries a death sentence. She is also furious — a cold, academic fury that has been building for decades as she documented the systematic destruction of her people's history.

The meeting takes place on one of the small mist-layer islands near the middle tier's edge — neutral ground, away from Directorate surveillance. The party must get the Grey Elf attendees off their island (smuggling them past Directorate checkpoints) and ensure the meeting is not discovered.

**Success:** The meeting produces a fragile but real alliance. Aelindra agrees to quietly spread the truth among her trusted contacts — not propaganda, but history. The Hawkmen agree to suspend raiding operations to demonstrate good faith. The reconciliation does not produce an instant army, but it plants the seed. If the uprising occurs (Scene 6), the reconciliation determines whether Grey Elf civilians participate or simply stand aside — the difference between a popular revolt and a Hawkmen-only assault.

**Catalyst 2: The Broken Chain**

The Directorate's compulsion magic is its backbone. Without it, the proctors and censors would be ordinary Grey Elves — loyal to the institution, perhaps, but capable of doubt, hesitation, and conscience. The party has the opportunity to break that compulsion on a specific, strategically valuable target.

**Proctor-Captain Thessaly Greymantle** commands the Directorate's enforcement division on the Third Island — the central island, which houses the main teleportation circle to the upper tier. Thessaly is efficient, respected by subordinates, and deeply committed to the Directorate's mission. They are also magically compelled and have been for eleven years. Before the compulsion, they were already a loyal Directorate member — an idealist who genuinely believed that Tana's governance, however harsh, was preferable to the chaos they were told existed before.

The party can identify Thessaly as a target through intelligence gathered on the middle tier (Aelindra knows of them, Hawkmen scouts have observed their patrol patterns, lower-tier residents remember them from an inspection tour). Reaching Thessaly requires either luring them to a location away from their retinue or infiltrating their offices on the Third Island.

**Breaking the compulsion:** *Break enchantment* (DC 24) or *remove curse* (CL 15th check against CL 17th). The subject must be willing or helpless. Thessaly is not willing — the compulsion ensures that — so the party must either restrain them by force (risking an alarm) or find a way to make them momentarily receptive. Aelindra, if the reconciliation has occurred, can arrange a private meeting under the pretense of a historical consultation — Thessaly was a scholar before they joined the Directorate and still has intellectual interests that the compulsion does not suppress.

**After the compulsion breaks:** Thessaly does not immediately become an ally. The compulsion is gone, but the fear is not. They know what the Directorate does to traitors. They know that the Directorate's power is vast and the party's is uncertain. They also know, now that they can think clearly, that eleven years of their decisions were not entirely their own — and the realization is devastating. The party must make a Diplomacy check (DC 25) or a compelling argument (GM discretion — referencing specific Directorate actions that Thessaly now recognizes as unjust, or presenting evidence of the demon infiltration) to convince Thessaly to actively help rather than simply flee.

**Success:** Thessaly provides three critical assets: the schedule and access codes for the Third Island teleportation circle (enabling the party to reach the upper tier via the Directorate's own transport system), a list of compelled agents and their renewal dates (useful for future disruption), and their personal authority to order the Third Island garrison to stand down during the uprising. Thessaly is terrified but resolute — the compulsion kept them from seeing the truth, and now that they see it, they refuse to look away.

**Catalyst 3: The Academy**

Castle Perilous houses the academy — Tana's prison-school for the realm's most talented arcanists. Students are identified by the Directorate, taken from their families on the middle tier, and transported to the upper tier, where they receive genuine arcane education in exchange for a lifetime of captivity. The academy produces the scrolls and magic items that Tana hoards, the enchantments that bind Directorate agents, and the wards that protect Castle Perilous. It is the engine of Tana's power.

The party cannot assault the academy before reaching the upper tier — it is inside Castle Perilous. But they can lay the groundwork for its liberation from the middle tier by making contact with the families of academy captives.

Every Grey Elf family on the middle tier that has lost a child to the academy carries a grief that the Directorate's control cannot fully suppress. The party can reach these families through Aelindra's network (if the reconciliation has occurred) or through direct outreach in the university districts. The message is simple: the party intends to storm Castle Perilous, and when they do, the academy's students will be freed — but only if the Directorate's forces are too busy to defend both the middle tier and the upper tier simultaneously.

**Success:** The academy families become the emotional core of the uprising. Their motivation is not political — it is personal. They want their children back. A Diplomacy check (DC 20) to the families as a group, or a particularly compelling appeal to a prominent family (GM discretion), secures their commitment. When the uprising begins (Scene 6), the academy families lead the civilian component — ordinary Grey Elves, unarmed and unarmored, flooding the streets and the Directorate checkpoints, demanding the return of their children. The Directorate cannot ignore this — it must respond, and every proctor sent to manage the crowd is a proctor not defending Castle Perilous.

**Ally Points:** Each completed catalyst adds strategic value but the ally point award is holistic. All three catalysts completed + Tana turned = 3 ally points. Two catalysts + turned = 2. One catalyst or none + turned = 2. Killed = 1 (stable, the uprising provides a transitional government) or 0 (destabilized, the uprising collapses without Tana's cooperation and the Directorate reasserts control).

---

### Scene 7: The Spark

The party has the spellbook. They have allies — Hawkmen warriors, reconciled Grey Elves, a turned Directorate officer, grieving families ready to take to the streets. They need to reach the upper tier and Castle Perilous. This scene describes two paths: the uprising path (available if at least two catalysts were completed) and the stealth path (available regardless).

**The Uprising Path**

If the party has completed at least two of the three catalysts, the uprising is viable. The plan is straightforward: the civilian population of the middle tier, led by the academy families and supported by Hawkmen warriors, floods the streets in a coordinated action across all five islands. The Directorate must respond. Every proctor sent to the chained islands is one fewer defending the transport circles, the lift platforms, and the aerial approaches to the upper tier.

The party coordinates the timing with Kerrax (Hawkmen), Aelindra (Grey Elf civilians), and Thessaly (Directorate defection, if available). The uprising begins at dawn on a date of the party's choosing.

> *It starts with the families. They come out of their homes at first light — hundreds of them, then thousands, Grey Elves who have spent their entire lives obeying, who have swallowed their fear and their grief and their anger for so long that they have forgotten what those things taste like. They walk into the streets and they do not stop. They walk to the Directorate offices and they stand there, and the proctors who come to disperse them find that the crowd does not disperse, because the people in the crowd have remembered something that five centuries of control could not entirely erase: that they are angry, and that there are more of them than there are proctors, and that their children are in a tower on a rock in the sky and they would very much like them back.*

The uprising is not a battle — it is a distraction. The civilians are not armed and do not fight. The Hawkmen provide security, preventing the Directorate from using lethal force against the crowds (the presence of armed Hawkmen warriors at key intersections forces the proctors to divide their attention between crowd control and military threat). Thessaly, if turned, orders the Third Island garrison to stand down, creating a gap in the enforcement net.

During the chaos, the party reaches the upper tier. If Thessaly was turned, they use the Third Island teleportation circle — Thessaly provides the access codes and ensures the circle is unguarded during a specific window. If Thessaly was not turned, the party flies to the upper tier on gliders (or via the Hawkmen basket) under cover of the uprising's confusion — the arcane artillery emplacements on Castle Perilous are manned by Directorate forces who have been recalled to the middle tier, and the reduced defenses allow the party to reach the island's surface uncontested.

**The Stealth Path**

If the party has not completed sufficient catalysts for the uprising — or chooses not to risk civilian lives — they must reach the upper tier through subterfuge alone. This path is harder but does not require NPC support.

The party flies to the upper tier under cover of darkness and mist — using the gliders from Tana's Refuge if they found them, or the Hawkmen basket if they did not (the basket approach is significantly more difficult, imposing a −4 penalty on all Stealth checks due to the basket's size and the noise of six Hawkmen in coordinated flight). They approach from below to avoid the worst of Castle Perilous's detection wards. The approach requires a series of group Stealth checks (DC 22) as the party passes through the ward perimeter. Failure on any check triggers an alarm — the party has 1d4+1 rounds before a rapid-response team (4 proctors + 1 censor) arrives at their entry point, and the entire castle goes to heightened alert.

Even without an alarm, the upper tier is heavily patrolled. The party must navigate the grounds of Castle Perilous to reach the inner chambers where Tana resides. This is a dungeon-crawl sequence through a fortress — patrol routes to avoid, wards to disable or circumvent, locked doors and magical traps. The party's mythic abilities are essential here; without them, the defenses would be insurmountable.

**Encounter: The Gate Guardians**

Regardless of which path the party takes, the final approach to Tana's inner chambers is blocked by Castle Perilous's elite defenders — creatures the Directorate has guarded these halls with for centuries, believing them to be bound outsiders.

- **Enemies:** 1 khalahathi (CR 10) and 2 adhukaits (CR 7). These are demons, embedded in the castle's guard force since before anyone currently alive can remember. They answer to Tornsoul's interests, not Tana's, though the distinction has been irrelevant for centuries — keeping Tana isolated and in power serves both.
- **Terrain:** The approach corridor to Tana's inner sanctum. Forty feet wide, a hundred feet long, lined with pillars. The floor is polished stone, the walls hung with tapestries depicting Auzra's history (the sanitized version). The corridor is warded — *dimensional anchor* effect throughout, preventing teleportation. The pillars provide cover.
- **Tactics:** The khalahathi takes the center of the corridor, activating *displacement* to make itself harder to hit, then uses whirlwind of steel to strike every creature within its 10-foot reach — in a 40-foot-wide corridor with pillars, this forces the party to spread out or take devastating four-weapon attacks. Its all-around vision prevents flanking, denying rogues their most reliable damage source. The adhukaits use the space the khalahathi creates, flanking with their twin-body coordination against anyone who tries to circle around the khalahathi or engage at range. If the uprising is active, the defenders fight with increasing desperation — they know something has gone wrong outside and are buying time.
- **Escape hatch:** The party can retreat through the corridor to the castle's outer chambers, where the pursuit will break off (the defenders are bound to the inner sanctum and will not leave it). This allows the party to rest, heal, and try again, though the defenders will be alert and may have called for reinforcements (1d4 proctors arrive within 10 minutes).

---

### Scene 8: Castle Perilous

Tana's inner sanctum is at the apex of Castle Perilous — a vast, circular chamber lined floor to ceiling with shelves, every shelf packed with scrolls, books, wands, staves, rings, and magical artifacts of every description. The collection is staggering in its scope and heartbreaking in its purpose. Tana cannot use any of it. The corruption that destroyed their ability to memorize spells also prevents them from attuning to most magic items for longer than a few hours. The hoard is not a resource — it is a compulsion, the corrupted expression of a mind that once loved knowledge and now can only love possessing it.

Tana is at the center of the chamber, seated at a desk covered in scrolls, surrounded by a semicircle of open books they cannot read. They are an Aasimar — tall, angular, with silver-white hair and features that would be beautiful if they were not drawn tight with suspicion and exhaustion. Their eyes glow faintly — a celestial heritage that the corruption has dimmed but not extinguished. They look up when the party enters, and their first reaction is not fear or anger. It is interest. Someone new is here. Someone who might know something they do not.

> *The chamber smells of parchment and dust and the particular ozone tang of too much stored magic in too small a space. The figure at the desk is not what the title "Gray Eminence" suggests. There is no throne, no crown, no visible symbol of authority. There is a person sitting at a desk, surrounded by more knowledge than any single being could absorb in a dozen lifetimes, and the expression on their face as they look at you is the expression of someone who has been interrupted while doing something important and is deciding whether the interruption is worth their time.*
>
> *"You are not Directorate," they say. The voice is dry, precise, and carries the faint musical quality of the Aasimar — a resonance that the corruption has not been able to strip away entirely. "You are not from this world. You carry the old power — I can feel it from here. Old power, the kind the system produces." A pause. The eyes narrow. "Who sent you? What do you know? Tell me everything."*

**The Confrontation**

Tana is lucid, intelligent, and dangerous. Unlike Garl, whose madness consumed his reason, Tana's corruption has sharpened their intellect while twisting its purpose. They are capable of conversation, negotiation, and deception. They are also deeply paranoid and will assume, by default, that the party has come to steal something from them.

**If the party presents the spellbook:**

Tana's reaction begins as suspicion — they reach for the book the way one reaches for a weapon that might be trapped, cautiously, with one hand while the other prepares a defensive spell. But when they open it and see the handwriting, the suspicion drains away. The book is undeniably theirs. The handwriting is theirs. The notes — the excited, margin-filling observations of a young scholar who could not believe their luck at being alive in a world where magic existed — are theirs. And the person who wrote those notes is so far from the person holding the book that the distance between them is a kind of violence.

> *They do not read the spells. They read the notes. Their lips move as they follow the words — their own words, in their own hand, from a time so distant it might as well be another person's life. You watch the change happen in real time. The paranoia does not leave their face; it is overwritten, layer by layer, by something else. Recognition. Grief. Shame. Their hand, the one that was preparing a spell, drops to the desk. The glow in their eyes brightens — not with power, but with the celestial light that the corruption has been suppressing for five hundred years, forcing its way to the surface for the first time since the war.*
>
> *"I wrote this," they say. Not to you. To themselves. "I wrote 'that was a good day.' I remember the day. Magister Elorn's face when I showed him the results. I—" They stop. They close the book. They press their palms flat against the cover and breathe, and when they speak again the voice is different. Smaller. Younger. Afraid.*
>
> *"What have I done?"*

The turning is not instantaneous. Tana must be talked through the decision — the corruption fights to reassert control, whispering that knowledge is power, that power must be hoarded, that the party is here to take what is theirs. A Diplomacy check (DC 25) or a particularly compelling argument (referencing the students imprisoned in the academy, the Grey Elves living in fear, the Hawkmen who still remember Tana as a friend) helps Tana push through. Success means Tana renounces their mythic power — a wrenching act that leaves them diminished, exhausted, and profoundly shaken, but free.

If turned, Tana's first act is to order the academy opened and its students released. Their second act is to dismiss the Directorate — a magical command that deactivates every active compulsion simultaneously, freeing every proctor and censor across the realm in a single moment. Their third act is to sit back down at their desk and put their head in their hands, because the weight of what they have done across five centuries has landed on them all at once and they do not know how to carry it.

**If the party does not have the spellbook:**

Without the lever, Tana cannot be turned. The conversation devolves — Tana's paranoia escalates as the party fails to provide what they want (information, knowledge, something new), and their tone shifts from intellectual interest to cold hostility. They conclude that the party has come to steal from them, and they act accordingly.

**Encounter: The Gray Eminence**

- **Enemy:** Tana White-Lightning, the Gray Eminence. *Stat block: Stat Block Appendix.*
- **Terrain:** The inner sanctum. Circular, 60 feet in diameter. Shelves line every wall, packed with magical items. Tana fights among and with these items — scrolls, wands, and staves drawn from the shelves mid-combat, used once, and discarded. The floor is cluttered with books and scattered items (difficult terrain in a 10-foot radius around the desk). Four pillars support the domed ceiling.
- **Tana's abilities:** Tana cannot memorize spells but has spent five centuries stockpiling scrolls and items to compensate. They fight by activating items and scrolls at an extraordinary rate — their Use Magic Device bonus is among the highest of any creature the party has faced, and their hoard provides access to virtually any arcane spell in the game. Their mythic abilities focus on speed and item use: mythic surge on UMD checks, the ability to activate two items in a single round, and a mythic ability that lets them draw and activate an item as a free action once per round. They are a one-person arsenal.
- **Tactics:** Tana fights at range, using the shelves and pillars for cover while cycling through their most powerful items. They open with area-of-effect spells (scrolls of *chain lightning*, *cone of cold*) to force the party to spread out, then target individual PCs with disabling effects (*hold person*, *feeblemind*, *maze*). If cornered in melee, they activate defensive items (*displacement*, *mirror image*, *emergency force sphere*) to disengage. They are not physically doughty — their strategy is never to be touched.
- **The Hoard as Hazard:** The shelves are packed with volatile magical items. Area-of-effect spells that strike the shelves have a chance of triggering secondary detonations — each shelf section that takes 15+ damage from a single effect releases a random magical surge (roll 1d6: 1–2 = 3d6 fire in 15-ft. burst, 3–4 = *confusion* in 20-ft. radius DC 18, 5 = *dispel magic* targeting all creatures within 20 ft., 6 = 2d6 force damage to all creatures within 10 ft.). The party must balance the need for area-of-effect damage against the risk of collateral detonations.
- **GM note:** Tana is the most tactically sophisticated Warlock the party has faced. Garl was a creature of pain and instinct; Sona was a puppet. Tana is a genius fighting with every resource available. The encounter should feel like facing a wizard who has had five hundred years to prepare for exactly this moment — because they have. Corruption score: +2.*

---

### Scene 9: The Overlook

Whether Tana was turned or fought, the party must reach the Overlook — the mythic place of power, located at the very apex of Castle Perilous in Tana's private garden.

The garden is on the roof of the castle, open to the sky — the only place on the upper tier where the twilight is visible without obstruction. It is beautiful in the way that lonely things are beautiful. The plants are tended by automated magitek systems, not by a gardener's hand, and they grow in perfect, sterile rows that lack the asymmetry of anything loved. At the garden's highest point, a natural rock outcropping rises above the manicured beds — the Overlook, the one feature Tana did not reshape or control.

> *The garden is the saddest thing in Castle Perilous, and the castle is full of sad things. It is perfect. Every hedge is trimmed, every flower in bloom, every path swept clean. And no one has walked these paths in a hundred years. The automated tenders move through the beds on silent tracks, pruning and watering and cultivating with mechanical precision, maintaining a garden for a person who has not come to see it since the corruption took the last of their ability to enjoy beauty for its own sake. At the garden's center, a natural stone formation rises twenty feet above the flowerbeds — raw, uncut, untouched by either magitek or magic. The stone is warm. You can feel it from ten feet away.*

**If Tana was turned:** Tana accompanies the party to the garden — slowly, leaning on a staff, their power gone and their body struggling with the absence. They stand at the base of the Overlook and look up at it with an expression that is difficult to read. They have not visited this place in over a century. The mythic power that once called to them from the stone has been silent since their corruption, and now that they have renounced what the corruption gave them, they hear nothing at all. They gesture for the party to go ahead. This is not their moment anymore.

**If Tana was killed:** The garden is the same, but the automated tenders have stopped. Tana's death severed whatever magical connection powered them, and they stand motionless among the flowerbeds, pruning shears extended, watering mechanisms frozen mid-arc. The garden will not survive without them. Neither will the castle — the wards are already failing, the lights dimming, the great structure losing the will its creator no longer provides. The party climbs to the Overlook in silence.

**The Place of Power**

The rune circle is carved into the Overlook's flat summit — exposed to the sky, the oldest piece of worked stone on the upper tier, older than Castle Perilous by millennia. When the party steps into the circle, the view opens.

From the Overlook, one can see clearly into every part of Auzra — through the mist, past the toxic clouds, across the full expanse of the realm's scattered islands. The party sees the lower tier's domed settlements, small and fragile beneath the yellow haze. They see the middle tier's five chained islands, and if the uprising occurred, they can see the crowds still in the streets, the Hawkmen circling above, the beginning of something new. They see the small mist-layer islands where they arrived, and the Rainbow Road's terminus glowing faintly in the distance.

The place of power activates. The rune circle blazes with light — white-gold, celestial, the color of the Aasimar heritage that Tana carried and the corruption suppressed. The light rises from the Overlook in a column visible from every island in the realm, and the people below — Grey Elf and Hawkman alike — look up and see it, and for a moment, the sky does not feel like a prison. It feels like what it was always meant to be. Open.

The party receives: mythic tier 4 and the fourth fragment of the Song of Heaven.

> *The fourth fragment is air. It has no weight — not like the deep foundation of Tanis or the flowing current of Diaspola. It is a high, clear note that seems to exist above the melody rather than within it, carrying the other fragments upward the way a thermal carries a bird. When it joins the Song, the whole composition lifts — the notes you already carry separate slightly, gaining space and breathing room, and you hear harmonics between them that were not audible before. The Song is half complete. Four of nine. And standing on this stone at the apex of a world made of sky, with the wind pulling at your hair and the light of the Overlook still fading around you, the next four fragments feel less like a burden to be earned than like a melody waiting to be remembered.*

---

### Chapter Conclusion

**World State (Turned):** Auzra is in upheaval — the productive kind. Tana's renunciation of power and dismissal of the Directorate's compulsion has shattered the apparatus of control overnight, and the realm must now rebuild its governance from scratch. The process is messy, contentious, and full of uncertainty. Former Directorate agents, suddenly freed from compulsion, must reckon with what they did under its influence — some join the rebuilding, some withdraw in shame, some deny that the compulsion was real. The academy students are released and returned to their families, an event that produces more weeping than the islands have seen in five centuries. Tana remains on the upper tier, cooperating with transitional authorities and providing their knowledge (what remains accessible to them without mythic power) to help rebuild. They are diminished, haunted by guilt, but determined to be useful. Kerrax and Aelindra co-lead a transitional council that includes both Hawkmen and Grey Elves — the first such partnership since before the corruption. If all three catalysts were completed, the reconciliation, the freed Directorate agent, and the returned academy students provide a foundation strong enough to hold. Auzra's contribution to the Battle of Nexus is significant: Hawkmen aerial warriors, Grey Elf arcanists (former academy students eager to use their skills for something other than scroll production), and a supply of magical items from Tana's hoard, which they freely donate. Tana themselves may attend, though their combat capability is severely diminished without mythic power.

**World State (Killed):** Tana's death collapses the Directorate — without the compulsion's magical anchor, the *geas/quest* effects on every agent expire within hours. The result is chaos, not liberation. The Directorate's structure was the only governance the realm had, and its sudden absence leaves a vacuum. On the middle tier, competing factions form — former Directorate loyalists who insist the old system was working, reformers who want a Grey Elf democracy, Hawkmen who want their old territorial rights restored, and academy students who want vengeance. Kerrax attempts to mediate, but without a transitional authority backed by a living (reformed) Warlock, the factions cannot agree on basics. Auzra's contribution to the Battle of Nexus is limited — a contingent of Hawkmen warriors (Kerrax ensures this regardless of political chaos) and a small number of Grey Elf volunteers. The magical item supply from Castle Perilous is contested and largely unavailable.

**Transition:** The party returns to Nexus via the Rainbow Road. The journey back through Auzra is faster than the inbound trip — if the uprising occurred, the Hawkmen provide an honor escort of flying warriors who accompany the party's gliders (or basket) from Castle Perilous to the terminus island, a formation flight through open sky that feels like a victory lap. On Nexus, Maranthe meets them with news: the demon raids have intensified. Two gate towns that were showing signs of life after the party's visits to Diaspola and Tanis have been attacked. The pattern is clear — Tornsoul is responding to the party's progress, and each world they liberate opens a new front. The party rests, resupplies, and departs for the Arlune gate town.

**Milestone:** Level 10 is awarded at the conclusion of this chapter. Mythic Tier 4 was granted earlier at the Overlook (Scene 9).

---

## Chapter 7: Arlune — The Trademaster's Gambit *(Levels 11–12, Mythic Tier 5)*

### Chapter Summary

Arlune is the fourth world the party visits and a sharp tonal departure from its predecessors. Where Tanis was a tragedy and Auzra was a thriller, Arlune is a crime drama — a world where the most dangerous thing is not a monster or a tyrant but a system of exploitation so deeply embedded in daily life that most of its victims have stopped recognizing it as exploitation at all. The Twilight Sea is beautiful and lethal, a moonlit desert of impossible scale, and the civilization clinging to its mesas and oases runs on debt, obligation, and the quiet understanding that everyone owes something to someone.

The Warlock of Arlune is unique among the ten. Garm the Honest, a Dwarf Rogue, is the only Warlock who has not been fully corrupted — and the only one who genuinely wants to turn. His corruption twisted his defining virtue of trustworthiness into isolating secrecy, and for five centuries he has hidden behind the persona of the Trademaster, an invisible puppet master who maintains a fragile equilibrium among Arlune's seven criminal cartels through an elaborate network of intermediaries, misinformation, and carefully managed chaos. No one alive has met the Trademaster in person. No one knows that the Trademaster and the legendary Garm are the same person. Garm wants to step into the light — he has wanted to for a very long time — but he cannot trust anyone enough to let them help him do it.

The chapter's central challenge is therefore not combat but trust. The party must find a person who does not want to be found, earn the confidence of a person who has not trusted anyone in five hundred years, and destabilize a criminal economy without destroying the people it feeds. The Lucky Coin — Garm's lever, a token made by his old friend Casomir and passed down through his family — provides a crucial shortcut: an emotional key that bypasses Garm's defenses and reaches the person hiding behind the Trademaster's masks. Without it, the party can still reach Garm, but the path is longer, harder, and the outcome less certain.

The chapter should feel like a noir film set in a fantasy desert — morally ambiguous, atmospherically rich, full of people who are simultaneously sympathetic and dangerous. The cartels are not faceless evil organizations; they are institutions built on genuine needs (water, transport, protection) that have curdled into parasitic monopolies. The common people are not helpless innocents; they are survivors who have learned to navigate the system and would not thank the party for tearing it down without offering something better. Garm himself is the most sympathetic Warlock the party has encountered — a good person trapped by his own caution, doing real harm through inaction while convincing himself it is the least bad option.

### GM Guidance

**Tone:** Gritty crime drama. The cartels cause real suffering — hereditary debt, indentured servitude, withheld water and medicine as leverage — and the chapter should not shy away from depicting the human cost of Arlune's criminal economy. The beauty of the Twilight Sea contrasts with the misery of the people who depend on it. The emotional weight is distributed throughout the chapter, concentrated further in the Garm scenes, where the contrast between the Trademaster's cold competence and Garm's underlying loneliness should be palpable.

**The Trademaster as mystery:** One of the chapter's pleasures is the slow reveal that the Trademaster — this terrifying, invisible figure who controls a continent from the shadows — is actually a lonely Dwarf who wants to be found. The GM should play the Trademaster mythology straight in the early scenes: let the party be intimidated, let them hear contradictory rumors, let them feel the weight of a network they cannot see. The reveal that the Trademaster is Garm, and that Garm is not a villain, lands harder if the party has spent several sessions expecting a confrontation and instead gets a conversation.

**Demons on Arlune:** Tornsoul's demons operate on Arlune through two vectors. The first is overt: deep-desert predators — Asuras in their natural forms — that have been attacking caravans and isolated settlements for centuries, driving the population inward toward the Nightmarket and the larger oasis towns. These attacks are attributed to natural desert predators by most of the population, but their coordination and increasing frequency suggest intelligence behind them. The second vector is covert: one of the seven cartels is secretly run entirely by Asuras, its leadership concealed behind layers of intermediaries. The Asura cartel controls the deep-desert routes and uses its position to direct the predator attacks, concentrating Arlune's population for Tornsoul's eventual harvest. Garm suspects something is wrong with this cartel but has never been able to prove it — exposing the Asura infiltration is the third and most dangerous of his tasks for the party.

**Leveling:** The party enters at level 10, mythic tier 4. Level 11 is awarded after completing the first of Garm's three tasks (Scene 5, Task 1). Level 12 and mythic tier 5 are awarded at the chapter's conclusion after activating the Moonstone (Scene 7).

---

### Scene 1: The Gate Town and the Desert Threshold

The Arlune gate town on Nexus is the quietest the party has visited — and the emptiest. Where the Tanis gate town was grim and the Auzra gate town was austere, the Arlune gate town is abandoned. The buildings are intact, their construction a distinctive mix of Dwarven stonework and Gnomish ingenuity — prefabricated modular structures designed for rapid assembly, the kind of architecture built by people who expected to expand. They never got the chance. The gate town was a trading post in the days when the Rainbow Road carried commerce between the worlds, and when the Road shut down, the trade died, and the traders left. What remains is a ghost of commercial ambition: empty stalls with faded price boards, a provisioner's shop with bare shelves, a customs house whose ledgers have not been opened in five centuries. The terminus arch is dark. A painted sign beside it, its lettering cracked and sun-bleached, reads: "THE TWILIGHT SEA — PASSAGE RATES POSTED INSIDE."

> *The gate town is a skeleton of a marketplace. You can see what it was — the layout is unmistakable, stalls and shopfronts arranged for maximum foot traffic, sight lines designed to draw the eye from one vendor to the next, the bones of a commercial district that was built to process a volume of trade that no longer exists. The wind moves through the empty buildings and carries the smell of dust and old wood and the faintest sweetness that might be spice or might be decay. A single figure sits on a barrel outside the only structure that shows signs of recent habitation — a half-elf, thin, sun-darkened, watching the terminus arch with the expression of a person waiting for something they no longer believe will come. When you ask about Arlune, they shake their head and say, "Bring water. Bring coin. Bring something to trade. Don't bring trust — you won't need it, and no one will sell it to you."*

The Rainbow Road to Arlune is unlike the previous transits. The road does not plunge into darkness or rise into sky — it stretches, flattening into a long, straight causeway that extends to the horizon in both directions, the rainbow tiles bleaching as they go, losing color in a slow gradient until the road ahead is silver-white, the color of moonlight on sand. The air warms, dries, and thins. The mist that usually marks the borders between worlds here is replaced by a fine haze of luminous particles — sand, the party will later realize, carried on winds that blow between the worlds. Then the road ends, and the desert begins.

> *The Twilight Sea opens before you and the scale of it stops you mid-stride. There is no sun. There has never been a sun here — the sky is a deep indigo vault, cloudless and infinite, dominated by a moon so large and so close that you can see the craters on its surface with your bare eyes. The moon casts a pale silver light that transforms the desert below into something that should not exist: a landscape of luminous sand, stretching in every direction to a horizon that curves gently downward at the edges, as though the world itself is a shallow bowl. The dunes glow. Not brightly — softly, the way snow glows under starlight, a cool radiance that comes from within the sand itself rather than reflecting from above. The colors are muted — silver, blue-grey, the faintest suggestion of violet in the deeper hollows between dunes — and the silence is so complete that you can hear your own blood moving. The road deposits you on a flat-topped mesa, fifty feet above the desert floor, and from this height you can see for miles in every direction. There is nothing. Sand and stone and moonlight and a stillness that is not peaceful but patient, the stillness of a place that was here before you arrived and will be here long after you leave.*

The gate town on Arlune — if it can be called that — is a waystation called **Dusthaven**, clinging to the mesa's western slope like a barnacle on a hull. It consists of a few dozen structures built from the pale sandstone of the mesa itself: a water house (the most important building, containing a magitek purification system that draws moisture from the deep rock), a provisioner, a sandship dock cut into the mesa's base, a small tavern called the Empty Purse, and a scattering of residences. The population is perhaps sixty — mostly Gnomes, with a handful of Dwarves, a few humans of mixed heritage, and a single elderly Half-Orc who appears to own everything that is not nailed down and several things that are.

Dusthaven is a transit point, not a destination. The people who live here do so because they service the sandship route between the mesa and the Nightmarket, and everything about the settlement reflects this function.
