The water house sells water by the skin at prices that increase with the buyer's apparent desperation. The provisioner stocks desert survival gear — sun cloaks (unnecessary given the absence of a sun, but useful against windborne sand), filter masks, salt tablets, and light sources for the brief dark hours when the moon dips below the horizon. The tavern sells a thin beer brewed from desert lichen and a stronger spirit distilled from something the locals decline to identify.

**The Debt Economy**

The party's first lesson in Arlune's social structure comes immediately: passage to the Nightmarket is not free. A sandship caravan is preparing to depart from Dusthaven's dock — a convoy of three vessels sitting in their berths at the mesa's base, their hulls humming faintly as their magitek cores warm up. They are medium-sized cargo haulers, perhaps sixty feet long and twenty wide, with sealed decks, armored viewports, and the battered look of machines that have been repaired more times than anyone has bothered to count. The lead vessel's drive wheels are visible beneath the stern — heavy cylinders of magitek-reinforced metal, angled to kick sand backward and propel the craft forward once the hull polarization lifts it clear of the ground. A lookout tower rises from the forward deck, and a pair of harpoon mounts — for sand leviathans, the crew explains, though they have not seen one in months — bracket the stern. The caravan master, a Gnome woman named **Tessik**, quotes a passage rate of 200 gold per person — or, if the party prefers, a favor.

> *"Coin is coin," Tessik says, counting something on her fingers that has nothing to do with the conversation. She is small even by Gnome standards, built like a clenched fist, with sun-darkened skin and pale eyes that do not blink often enough. "But favors are worth more than coin out here, because coin runs out and favors compound. You do something for me, I remember. I do something for you, you remember. That's how it works. That's how everything works. You want to ride to the Nightmarket, you can pay, or you can owe me. And before you ask — yes, I will collect. Not today. Maybe not this year. But I will collect."*

The favor, if accepted, is unspecified — Tessik will call it in later (the GM can use this as a plot hook during the Nightmarket scenes or the task sequences, introducing a complication at an inconvenient moment). If the party pays in coin, Tessik is professional but unimpressed — coin means they do not understand how Arlune works, and she adjusts her estimation of them accordingly.

**The Sandship Transit**

The journey from Dusthaven to the Nightmarket takes approximately eighteen hours by sandship. The party experiences most of the transit from inside the vessel — sandships are sealed craft, their interiors divided into crew decks, cargo holds, a small helm station, and an engine room where the magitek core thrums with a low, constant vibration that becomes background noise within the first hour. The sealed design keeps out the fine desert sand that would otherwise infiltrate every mechanism and orifice within minutes of departure, and the few viewports are narrow, armored slits that offer limited but sufficient visibility.

> *The ship lurches, and then it floats. That is the only way to describe the sensation — one moment you are sitting in a metal box on solid ground, and the next the ground is gone and the box is gliding. The hull polarization engages with a sound like a sustained chord on a bass instrument, deep enough to feel in your teeth, and the vessel rises perhaps two feet off the sand and hangs there, humming. Then the drive wheels engage — you hear them spin up beneath the stern, a rising whine that becomes a roar — and the ship moves. Not fast, at first. The acceleration is gradual, the craft finding its rhythm as the drive wheels bite into the sand below and push the vessel forward. But within minutes you are moving at the speed of a galloping horse, and the desert is flowing past the viewports in a blur of silver and shadow.*

The vessels move at roughly the speed of a fast horse — faster on flat hardpan, slower when climbing dune ridges. The drive wheels beneath the stern provide motive force, their angle and speed adjusted by the helmsman through a system of magitek-linked levers. The hull's electro-arcane polarization keeps the vessel floating above the sand surface, eliminating friction but also eliminating traction — steering is handled by outboard rudders, broad plates mounted on articulated arms at the stern and flanks that drag through the sand below and bite into it to change the vessel's heading. Tessik's haulers are workmanlike craft, built for endurance rather than speed, but even so the sensation of speed in an enclosed metal hull with no external reference beyond narrow viewports is disorienting for newcomers. The crew finds this amusing.

On the rare occasions when the party is permitted on the external observation deck — the lookout tower's upper platform, accessed by a ladder from the forward hatch — the scale of the Twilight Sea becomes apparent. The desert stretches to the horizon in every direction, silver under the moonlight, and the three-ship caravan is a cluster of dark shapes trailing plumes of kicked-up sand across an emptiness that makes the vessels look like insects crossing a tablecloth. The crew points out landmarks: a mesa formation called the Teeth, where pirates sometimes lie in ambush; a distant smudge on the horizon that is the Nightmarket's permanent smoke plume; a depression in the sand where, they claim, a sand leviathan surfaced last season and swallowed a cargo ship whole.

*GM note: Sandships are the primary mode of transport on Arlune and will feature throughout the chapter. The key details to establish here: sealed hulls (the interior is the default environment, not the deck), magitek hull polarization for hovering, drive wheels for propulsion, outboard rudders for steering. The aesthetic is functional and battered — these are machines that have been maintained and repaired for centuries, their original elegance buried under layers of field modification, armor patching, and improvised replacement parts. Think Mad Max crossed with magitek engineering: vehicles that should not still be running but are, held together by ingenuity and stubbornness. Sandships range from small two-person scout skiffs to massive haulers and warships with crews of fifty or more — Tessik's caravan represents the middle of the range. Faster vessels may sport outboard catamaran-like structures that provide a wing-in-ground effect, lifting the craft higher above the sand for reduced drag. Military and pirate vessels carry heavier armor plating, arcane shield generators, and weapon mounts beyond the standard harpoons.*

During the transit, the party can learn the basics of Arlune's situation through conversation with the crew:

- **The seven cartels** control everything that matters — water, transport, protection, information, contraband, entertainment, and the deep-desert routes. No one operates in Arlune without dealing with at least one of them, and most people are entangled with several. The cartels do not openly war with each other — a fragile equilibrium has held for centuries, maintained by mutual deterrence and, some say, by the invisible hand of the Trademaster.
- **The Trademaster** is a figure of myth and rumor. Some say the Trademaster is a single person — a criminal mastermind who plays the seven cartels against each other from the shadows. Others say the Trademaster is a title, passed from one shadowy figure to another across the centuries. Still others say the Trademaster is a fiction — a bogeyman invented by the cartels themselves to explain the uncanny way their schemes keep canceling each other out. No one the crew has ever met claims to have met the Trademaster in person. Everyone has a theory. None of the theories agree.
- **The legend of Garm:** Older than the Trademaster myth and more beloved. The common people of Arlune remember their Warlock — Garm the Honest, the Dwarf who held the realm together after the apocalypse. A popular legend holds that Garm will someday return to defeat the crime lords and free the people. The crew members disagree about whether this is a real prophecy or a bedtime story, but the name carries weight — even the cartels do not mock Garm openly. To do so would be bad for business.
- **The deep-desert predators:** Attacks on caravans and outlying settlements have increased sharply in recent years. Creatures from the deep sand — things that the crew describes with the vagueness of people who have not seen them personally but have heard the stories from people who have — are pushing populations toward the larger settlements. The crew does not know why. They do not particularly care to find out. They just want to get to the Nightmarket before dark, because the few hours when the moon dips below the horizon and the desert goes dark are when the attacks happen most often.

*GM note: Scene 1 establishes Arlune's tone and social rules. The debt economy, the cartel system, and the Trademaster mystery are the chapter's three pillars, and the party should encounter all three in miniature before reaching the Nightmarket. Tessik's favor offer is a small-scale version of the obligations that entangle everyone on Arlune — let the party feel the pull of it, the way agreeing to an unspecified future favor is simultaneously reasonable and deeply uncomfortable. The sandship transit provides world-building without forcing exposition — the crew talks because they have eighteen hours to fill, not because they are delivering a briefing. The deep-desert predator mentions foreshadow the Asura cartel reveal in Scene 5, Task 3; do not oversell them here, but plant the seed.*

---

### Scene 2: The Nightmarket

The Nightmarket announces itself hours before the caravan arrives. First the smoke — a low, brown haze hanging above the horizon, the exhaust of a thousand magitek generators, cook-fires, forge-bellows, and alchemical workshops all pouring their waste into the still desert air. Then the sound, carried across the sand by some trick of the flat terrain: a low, undifferentiated roar that resolves, as the ships draw closer, into the layered noise of commerce, argument, music, and machinery. The smell arrives last — spice, metal, sweat, rendered fat, and the distinctive ozone-and-hot-copper scent of overtaxed magitek systems running far past their recommended service intervals.

> *The permanent city rises from the desert floor like a growth of dark stone. It is built into and onto a mesa formation — squat towers, flat-roofed warehouses, and terraced structures carved directly from the rock, connected by covered walkways and reinforced bridges. Magitek conduits run along the exterior walls in bundled clusters, pulsing with faint light. This is the core: the old city, the part that was here before the cartels, before the trade collapse, before everything went wrong. It looks like it has survived several apocalypses, and it has.*
>
> *Around it, spilling outward across the sand in every direction, sprawls the bazaar. There is no boundary between city and market — the stone buildings give way to semi-permanent structures of sheet metal and salvaged hull plating, which give way to canvas-and-pole market stalls, which give way to blankets spread on the sand with goods arranged on top. The farther from the center, the more temporary the structures become, until at the outermost edge the market dissolves into a scattering of lone traders sitting beside their wares in the open desert. The whole thing breathes — expanding when trade is good, contracting when it is not, and shifting laterally as cartels redraw their territorial boundaries through bribery, intimidation, and the occasional arson.*

Tessik's caravan docks at one of the mesa's sandship berths — carved slots in the rock face fitted with magitek clamps that grip the hull and hold it steady while cargo is offloaded. The berths are controlled by the Dune Syndicate (Cartel 2), and the docking fee is non-negotiable. Tessik pays without complaint; arguing with the Syndicate about berth fees is, she explains, how people end up walking home.

The party enters the Nightmarket through the mesa's lower gate — a wide tunnel cut through the rock, crowded with foot traffic, cargo haulers, and the occasional sandship skiff being dragged on a wheeled cradle toward an interior repair bay. The tunnel opens into the market proper, and the noise and density increase tenfold. The party is immediately immersed.

**The Seven Cartels**

Seven criminal organizations control Arlune's economy, infrastructure, and daily life. Their territories overlap in places, their interests conflict constantly, and the equilibrium between them is maintained — though no one can say precisely how — by a combination of mutual deterrence, entrenched corruption, and what some attribute to the Trademaster's invisible hand. The following profiles represent what the party can learn through observation, conversation, and a DC 15 Knowledge (local) or Diplomacy (gather information) check in the Nightmarket. Higher DCs (noted where applicable) yield more sensitive details.

**Cartel 1: The Wellkeepers**

The Wellkeepers control Arlune's water supply — the magitek purification systems that draw moisture from the deep sand and the ancient stone aquifers beneath the mesa formations. Water is the single most essential resource on Arlune, and the Wellkeepers' monopoly on it makes them simultaneously the most powerful and the most hated of the seven cartels. Every settlement, every sandship, every individual who drinks clean water does so because the Wellkeepers allow it, and they charge accordingly. Prices fluctuate with deliberate unpredictability — just enough variation to prevent the population from budgeting around them, ensuring that most people are perpetually one bad month from debt.

The Wellkeepers are led by **Hosta Glimwell**, a middle-aged Gnome woman who speaks softly, dresses plainly, and has never been observed raising her voice. She does not need to. Hosta inherited the cartel from her father, who inherited it from his mother, and the Glimwell family has held the water monopoly for over a century. She operates from a fortified compound in the mesa's upper levels, surrounded by magitek engineers who maintain the purification systems and enforcers who ensure no one interferes with them. Her public position is that the Wellkeepers provide an essential service at a fair price. Her private position, insofar as anyone knows it, is identical — Hosta appears to genuinely believe that the water monopoly is a public good, managed responsibly by the only people competent to do so. This makes her more dangerous than a simple profiteer; she cannot be bought because she does not think she is doing anything wrong.

*DC 20:* The Wellkeepers have been experiencing sabotage at their deep-level pumping stations — equipment failures that their engineers cannot explain, contamination events that seem targeted rather than accidental. Hosta has quietly hired outside security. She suspects one of the other cartels but has not identified which one. (The sabotage is actually the work of the Asura cartel, positioning assets for future use — see Task 1 in Scene 5.)

**Cartel 2: The Dune Syndicate**

The Dune Syndicate controls sandship transport — the trade routes, the docking berths, the repair facilities, and the fuel supply (the refined arcane reagents that power magitek cores). If the Wellkeepers control what people drink, the Syndicate controls where people go. Their shipping monopoly means that any goods moving between settlements pass through Syndicate hands, and the tariffs they levy on cargo are the single largest cost of doing business on Arlune. Independent sandship captains exist, but they operate on Syndicate sufferance — paying route fees, submitting to inspections, and accepting that any cargo the Syndicate wants moved takes priority over anything else in the queue.

The Syndicate is run by **Kellis Brighthammer**, a Half-Orc woman of considerable size and presence who earned her position by being the best sandship captain on Arlune before she decided that owning the routes was more profitable than sailing them. Kellis operates from a converted warship permanently docked at the Nightmarket's largest berth, and she conducts all business aboard — partially for security, partially because she does not trust solid ground. She is direct, pragmatic, and respected even by people who despise the Syndicate's practices. Her word, once given, is reliable. Her prices are not.

*DC 20:* Kellis is privately concerned about the deep-desert routes. Caravans on the longest runs — the routes that pass through the most remote stretches of the Twilight Sea — have been disappearing at an increasing rate. She has lost six ships in the past two years, with no wreckage recovered. She attributes it to pirates but does not fully believe her own explanation; pirates leave wreckage. Whatever is taking her ships is leaving nothing behind.

**Cartel 3: The Ironbound**

The Ironbound are Arlune's martial brotherhood — a warrior order that happens to run a protection racket. They provide security throughout the Nightmarket and the surrounding settlements: armed escorts for caravans, guards for warehouses and market stalls, enforcers for debt collection, and, when the contract demands it, violence directed at whoever the client specifies. Every merchant in the Nightmarket pays the Ironbound a monthly fee, and in exchange the Ironbound ensure that no one robs, assaults, or intimidates that merchant. The system works, after a fashion. The streets of the Nightmarket are safer than they would be without the Ironbound's presence — but the safety is conditional, revocable, and never free. What distinguishes the Ironbound from common street gangs is that they believe, with absolute sincerity, that their work is honorable.

The Ironbound operate under a code they call the **Tenets of the Fist** — a set of martial principles governing conduct in and out of combat that every member memorizes during a grueling two-year induction period. The Tenets forbid striking an unarmed opponent who has not been warned, taking payment for a contract and failing to fulfill it, lying to a client about the terms of an arrangement, and showing cowardice in battle. They also require that debts of honor be repaid, that challenges between members be settled in formal combat, and that the weak be protected — provided the weak have paid their protection fee. The Ironbound do not see the contradiction. The Tenets are internally consistent; it is the world that fails to meet their standard. A merchant who cannot afford protection is not being abandoned — they are simply outside the Ironbound's obligation, and obligation is everything.

Recruitment is rigorous. Prospective members undergo months of physical and mental testing — combat trials, endurance marches through the deep desert, and a period of supervised service where the recruit's conduct is evaluated against the Tenets by a senior brother. Most applicants are rejected. Those who pass are inducted in a formal ceremony that involves ritual scarification and an oath sworn on the brotherhood's founding weapon — a battered magitek war-hammer called the First Fist, said to have been carried by the Ironbound's founder. The result is an organization of highly trained, deeply disciplined fighters who regard themselves as the last honorable institution on Arlune and who treat their protection contracts with the gravity of sacred vows. They are, in practice, still a criminal syndicate but they do not experience themselves as one.

The Ironbound answer to **Qadir Stonefist**, a scarred Dwarf who rose through the brotherhood's ranks over three decades — every scar earned in sanctioned combat, every promotion the result of demonstrated excellence under the Tenets. Qadir is blunt, humorless, and possessed of an iron certainty that the Ironbound's way is the only way worth following. He enforces contracts with an almost ritualistic exactness — the terms of a protection arrangement are followed to the letter, no more and no less, and clients who attempt to renegotiate mid-contract find themselves temporarily unprotected until new terms are agreed upon. He maintains discipline within the brotherhood through the Tenets themselves: brothers who exceed their authority or brutalize civilians without cause face tribunal and punishment ranging from demotion to exile. Brothers who fail to collect, or who show hesitation in fulfilling a contract, face the same.

*DC 20:* Qadir has recently lost several of his best brothers — seasoned warriors who went on deep-desert escort contracts for Cartel 7 and did not return. The official explanation is that they were killed by desert predators. Qadir does not buy it. Warriors of the Ironbound do not simply vanish; the Tenets require that a brother's body be recovered and honored, and the Sandwalkers have produced no remains. Qadir has quietly stopped accepting Cartel 7's contracts and has assigned senior brothers to investigate — an escalation that the other cartels have noticed and that has created considerable tension.

**Cartel 4: The Exchange**

The Exchange trades in information — secrets, blackmail material, intelligence on rival operations, and the identities and movements of anyone worth watching. In a world where survival depends on knowing who owes what to whom, the Exchange's product is arguably as essential as water. Their agents are everywhere: bartenders, dock workers, street vendors, beggars, and the occasional magitek maintenance technician with access to communication conduits. They sell to all comers, including the other cartels, and their neutrality is their greatest asset — the Exchange does not take sides, because taking sides would reduce their market by half.

The Exchange is managed by **Silvana Morne**, an aging Human woman who operates from a nondescript tea shop in the lower bazaar. Silvana looks like someone's grandmother and behaves like one, serving tea and pastries to clients while they negotiate the purchase of information that could destroy careers, collapse alliances, or start wars. Her network is the largest intelligence operation on Arlune — larger, some say, than the Trademaster's, though Silvana would never make such a claim. She is unfailingly polite, genuinely curious about everyone she meets, and entirely without scruple about what she will sell or to whom she will sell it.

*DC 20:* The Exchange has been unable to penetrate Cartel 7's deep-desert operations. Every agent Silvana has sent into the deep desert has either returned with nothing or not returned at all. This is unprecedented — the Exchange has informants in every other cartel, every guild, every significant organization on Arlune. The blank spot in her intelligence map bothers Silvana considerably, and she would pay well for information about Cartel 7's true operations.

**Cartel 5: The Voidrunners**

The Voidrunners handle contraband — stolen magitek, prohibited alchemical substances, weapons that exceed the informal limits the cartels have agreed upon, and anything else that cannot be bought or sold through legitimate channels (a category that, on Arlune, encompasses a great deal). Their name derives from the empty stretches of desert between settlements — the void — through which their smuggling routes run. The Voidrunners maintain a network of hidden caches, dead drops, and way-stations scattered across the Twilight Sea, allowing them to move goods between any two points on Arlune without passing through Dune Syndicate-controlled routes or Ironbound-patrolled territory.

The Voidrunners are led by **Fen Quickstep**, a wiry Gnome man who speaks too fast, moves too fast, and makes decisions too fast for most people to keep up with. Fen is brilliant, erratic, and perpetually convinced that everyone around him is operating at half speed. He runs the Voidrunners less like a criminal organization and more like a trading company that happens to ignore every law, treating risk as a variable to be optimized rather than avoided. His warehouse complex in the deep bazaar — a maze of interconnected structures, hidden passages, and trapped corridors — serves as both headquarters and showroom, where clients can browse stolen magitek components with the assistance of knowledgeable staff.

*DC 20:* The Voidrunners recently acquired a magitek component of unusual power — a sandship engine core that Fen's engineers believe dates to the Rainbow Road era. It is worth a fortune, but Fen has not sold it. He is studying it, convinced it represents a level of magitek engineering beyond anything currently produced on Arlune. He is correct; the engine core is part of Arlune's original infrastructure, and Garm needs it for post-reveal reconstruction. (See Task 2 in Scene 5.)

**Cartel 6: The Silken Court**

The Silken Court controls entertainment, vice, and the indentured labor market. Their operations range from gambling halls and drug dens to fighting pits and pleasure houses, and they maintain a network of indentured workers — people who have fallen too deep into debt to escape and now work off their obligations under Court supervision, a process designed to never quite reach completion. The Court is the cartel that most directly profits from misery, and their establishments are both the most popular and the most despised places in the Nightmarket.

The Silken Court is ruled by **Ysabel Thornheart**, a Half-Elf woman of striking beauty and absolute ruthlessness who built her empire from a single gambling house over the course of several decades. Ysabel is charming, generous with her hospitality, and genuinely entertaining company — she hosts lavish gatherings, patronizes artists and musicians, and donates conspicuously to the charity kitchens. These public virtues exist alongside private practices that include debt manipulation designed to trap workers in permanent indenture, the use of addictive alchemical substances to ensure customer loyalty, and the elimination of anyone who attempts to operate a competing entertainment venue without her permission. Ysabel does not consider these practices contradictory; she considers them business.

*DC 20:* The Silken Court's indentured labor system draws from the poorest populations, many of whom were originally driven to the Nightmarket from outlying settlements by deep-desert predator attacks — attacks that have intensified in recent years. Ysabel has noticed the correlation and suspects someone is deliberately pushing populations toward her labor pool, but she has not pursued the connection because it benefits her. If confronted with evidence that the attacks are engineered, she would be more angry about being manipulated than about the suffering involved.

**Cartel 7: The Sandwalkers**

The Sandwalkers control the deep-desert routes — the paths beyond the established trade lanes, into the trackless interior of the Twilight Sea where the dunes are taller, the creatures are larger, and the distances between oases can be measured in days rather than hours. They hire themselves out as guides, escorts, and scouts for deep-desert expeditions, and they claim territorial authority over a vast stretch of the Twilight Sea that no other cartel has the reach to contest. Their operations are remote, their membership is secretive, and their leadership is never seen in the Nightmarket.

The Sandwalkers' nominal leader is **Vekk**, a figure described differently by everyone who claims to have met him — sometimes a Gnome, sometimes a Human, sometimes a Halfling, always masked or hooded. The other cartels deal with the Sandwalkers through intermediaries who arrive in the Nightmarket periodically, negotiate contracts, and disappear back into the desert. The arrangement works well enough for the other cartels, who have little interest in the deep desert anyway, and the Sandwalkers' territorial claims do not conflict with anyone else's core operations.

*DC 20:* No one from the other cartels has ever visited the Sandwalkers' headquarters. No one has verified that Vekk exists. The Sandwalkers' intermediaries are professional and reliable, but they never stay in the Nightmarket long enough to be observed closely, and they never bring the same face twice. Silvana Morne of the Exchange — whose agents have penetrated every other cartel — has nothing on the Sandwalkers. The blank spot in her network is, as noted above, unprecedented.

*DC 25:* The timing of the deep-desert predator attacks correlates precisely with the Sandwalkers' expansion of territorial claims over the past decade. As settlements on the desert's periphery are abandoned, the Sandwalkers quietly absorb their oases, water sources, and supply caches. They are growing, and no one is paying attention because the growth happens in places no one else wants to go.

*GM note: The Sandwalkers are the Asura cartel. Their leadership — "Vekk" and the rotating intermediaries — are Asuras in disguise, using *alter self* and other illusion abilities to maintain their cover. The deep-desert predator attacks are Asura operations designed to drive populations toward the Nightmarket, concentrating them for Tornsoul's eventual harvest while simultaneously expanding the Asuras' territorial control over Arlune's interior. The Sandwalkers have been operating for decades, predating even Garm's suspicions — they are patient, methodical, and deeply embedded. The DC 25 information should be available to the party only through dedicated investigation or the Exchange's assistance, and it should feel like assembling a pattern from disparate clues rather than a single revelatory disclosure.*

**The Social Landscape**

The Nightmarket is home to roughly fifteen thousand permanent residents, with a transient population that swells to twice that during peak trade periods. Most residents owe debts to at least one cartel — water fees to the Wellkeepers, protection fees to the Ironbound, transport tariffs to the Syndicate — and many owe to several, caught in a web of overlapping obligations that compounds faster than it can be paid down. Debt is hereditary on Arlune; a parent's obligations pass to their children, and the children of those children, until the debt is cleared or the family line ends. Some families have been in debt for generations, the original amount long since paid many times over through interest and penalties, the balance somehow always larger than it was the year before.

The charity kitchens are the only safety net. Scattered throughout the Nightmarket and the smaller settlements, they provide food, clean water, and basic medical care to anyone who cannot afford to buy it — which, on any given day, is roughly a quarter of the population. The kitchens are run by volunteers and funded by anonymous donations routed through a labyrinthine network of intermediary accounts that even the Exchange has been unable to fully trace. The common belief is that the donations come from a consortium of wealthy merchants. The truth — that the Trademaster personally funds the entire network from his accumulated wealth — is known to no one except Garm himself.

The party can observe the social dynamics of the Nightmarket through everyday interactions: a vendor adjusting prices upward when an Ironbound patrol passes, knowing the protection fee is due; a family arguing in hushed voices about which debt to pay this month; a child running an errand for a Silken Court establishment, clearly working off a parent's obligation; a group of sandship crewmates spending their shore pay at a gambling house and not noticing that the games are tilted just enough to ensure they leave lighter than they arrived.

**Gathering Information: The Trademaster**

The party can spend time in the Nightmarket gathering information about the Trademaster. This is best handled as a series of Diplomacy checks (DC 15 base, modified by approach) conducted at various locations — taverns, market stalls, the tea shops that serve as informal meeting places. Each successful check produces a piece of the Trademaster legend, drawn from the following (the GM should select or adapt based on what the party has already heard from Tessik's crew):

- *"The Trademaster is three people working together — a Gnome, a Human, and a Dwarf. They rotate who makes the decisions each month."* (False.)
- *"The Trademaster died fifty years ago. What you're hearing about is a ghost — or maybe a magitek construct running his old playbook."* (False, though the construct theory is creative enough that Garm himself would appreciate it.)
- *"The Trademaster is Kellis Brighthammer. Think about it — who else has the reach? The Syndicate moves everything. She knows where every crate goes."* (False. Kellis has heard this theory and finds it amusing.)
- *"There is no Trademaster. The cartels invented him to explain why they can't seem to destroy each other. It's easier to blame a shadow than admit they're all equally matched."* (False, but the reasoning is sound.)
- *"The Trademaster runs the charity kitchens. Follow the money — whoever feeds the poor for free has a reason, and that reason is leverage."* (Closer to the truth than most theories, though the motivation is wrong. DC 20 to hear this one.)
- *"I met someone who met someone who was hired by the Trademaster. Said the job came through four different intermediaries, and payment arrived in a locked chest delivered by a construct that self-destructed after handoff. The Trademaster doesn't trust anyone to know anything."* (Accurate description of Garm's operational methods.)
- *"The Trademaster is Garm. The actual Garm, from the legends. He never left — he just stopped telling people who he was."* (True, obviously. DC 25 to hear this theory, and the speaker treats it as a crackpot fringe position that no reasonable person believes.)

*GM note: The Trademaster investigation serves two purposes — it gives the party a sense of Garm's operational genius and paranoia, and it lets them feel the weight of his legend without meeting him yet. Do not let the DC 25 theory land as a revelation; it should be presented as one theory among many, easily dismissed. The party should hear it and file it away, and only later realize it was the right answer all along. If the party pursues the charity kitchen lead aggressively (DC 20+), they can trace funding through several intermediary layers but ultimately hit a dead end — the trail disappears into a series of shell accounts managed by constructs that have no operator on file. This is Garm's handiwork, and it is airtight.*

**Encounter: The Charity Kitchen**

The party witnesses or stumbles into a cartel shakedown at one of the Nightmarket's charity kitchens. The encounter can be placed at any point during the party's exploration of the Nightmarket — ideally after they have gathered enough information to understand the social landscape but before they have located the Lucky Coin side quest.

The charity kitchen in question operates from a converted warehouse in the lower bazaar, serving meals twice daily to several hundred of the Nightmarket's poorest residents. When the party arrives (or passes by), a squad of four Ironbound brothers led by a senior brother named **Draven** is in the process of demanding an increased protection fee from the kitchen's head cook, a stout Gnome woman named **Mira Softfoot** who has been running the kitchen for twelve years and has no intention of paying a single coin more than the agreed-upon rate.

> *The Ironbound brother stands at the serving counter with his hands clasped behind his back, watching the line of hungry people waiting for their meal. His posture is parade-ground straight, his armor clean and well-maintained despite the sand that coats everything in the Nightmarket. Three more brothers stand at the kitchen's entrance in a disciplined formation — not lounging, not threatening, simply present. The cook is a small woman who has to look up to meet anyone's eyes, but she is looking up at the senior brother and her expression suggests that altitude is not a factor she considers relevant.*
>
> *"The rate went up," the brother says. "It goes up every quarter. The Tenets require that our obligations be fulfilled as contracted, and the contract specifies quarterly adjustment. You know this."*
>
> *"The rate went up last quarter," the cook replies. "And the quarter before that. At this pace, you will be charging me more than the food costs by midsummer, and I will be feeding no one, and you will be protecting an empty building. Explain to me how that serves anyone's interests."*
>
> *The brother considers this with what appears to be genuine deliberation. "The Tenets do not account for the interests of those outside the contract," he says. "They account for the obligation itself. The rate is the rate."*

The encounter is designed as a social and moral challenge, not necessarily a combat one. What makes the Ironbound's shakedown distinctive — and more unsettling than a simple thug extortion — is that Draven and his brothers believe they are acting honorably. They are fulfilling a contractual obligation with exactness and discipline. They are not rude, not threatening, not cruel. They are simply carrying out their duty, and their duty happens to involve extracting money from a charity kitchen that feeds the hungry. The Tenets provide a framework that makes this not only acceptable but righteous, and that framework is impervious to moral arguments from outside the brotherhood's value system. The party has several options:

- **Intervene directly (Intimidate DC 18 or combat):** The party can confront Draven and his brothers. Draven will not back down from intimidation alone — the Tenets forbid showing cowardice, and retreating from a confrontation without direct orders from Qadir would be a violation. However, if the party issues a formal challenge — framing the confrontation as a matter of honor rather than a threat — Draven will take it seriously. He may agree to settle the dispute through single combat between one of his brothers and a party member (the Ironbound respect martial prowess above all else). If the party simply attacks, the brothers fight with discipline and skill (use the warrior NPC stat block, CR 3, but with better tactics — they fight in formation, cover each other's flanks, and withdraw in good order if clearly outmatched rather than breaking and running). Win or lose, combat with the Ironbound has consequences: the brotherhood remembers, and they do not forgive debts of blood easily.
- **Appeal to the Tenets (Diplomacy DC 20):** The harder but more effective approach. If the party can frame their argument in terms the Ironbound respect — obligation, honor, the protection of those under the brotherhood's care — Draven will listen. The Tenets require that the weak be protected; if the party can argue convincingly that the rate increase violates this principle by destroying the kitchen that keeps the weak alive, Draven faces a genuine conflict between two Tenets (fulfill the contract vs. protect the weak). He will not resolve this himself — he will report the conflict to Qadir for adjudication, suspending the rate increase pending a ruling. This is a significant outcome: it means the party has engaged the Ironbound on their own terms, and Qadir will hear of it.
- **Negotiate pragmatically (Diplomacy DC 18):** The party can argue economics rather than honor — the kitchen's closure would destabilize the local area, reduce the Ironbound's reputation, and create problems that cost more to solve than the increased fee is worth. Draven understands this reasoning but considers it beneath the Tenets; he will accept it only if the party's argument is compelling enough to justify a deviation from standard procedure. A successful negotiation results in the rate holding at its current level — a temporary victory, but one that Mira appreciates deeply.
- **Pay the difference (100 gp):** The party can simply cover the increased fee. Draven accepts this without hesitation — the contract is fulfilled, the obligation is met, honor is satisfied. This is the quickest solution and the one that Mira finds most troubling — she has been on the receiving end of generosity before, and she knows that money given freely tends to create the same kinds of obligation that money owed does. She accepts, but her gratitude is guarded.
- **Observe and do nothing:** The party can watch and learn. Draven collects the increased fee with solemn exactness, Mira pays it because she has no alternative, the hungry people get their meals with slightly smaller portions. The brothers depart in formation, having fulfilled their duty. The world continues as it is. No one judges the party for this — it is how things work on Arlune.

*GM note: This encounter establishes the human cost of the cartel system and gives the party an early opportunity to define their relationship with the Nightmarket's power structures. There is no wrong answer — every choice has consequences that will ripple through subsequent scenes. If the party intervenes, they gain a reputation; if they observe, they gain understanding. Mira Softfoot is a useful recurring NPC — she knows the Nightmarket's poorest residents, she has seen the charity kitchen funding system from the inside (though she does not know its true source), and she can point the party toward people who have information about the Trademaster legend. She is not a quest-giver; she is a person trying to keep other people alive in a system designed to grind them down, and her perspective on Arlune's problems is valuable precisely because it comes from the ground level rather than the shadows.*

---

### Scene 3: The Lucky Coin *(Side Quest)*

The party can encounter this side quest at any point after arriving at the Nightmarket. The hook can come from several sources: Mira Softfoot mentions that the charity supply caravans from the outer settlements have been hit repeatedly by pirates, and the only person doing anything about it is a "mad young Dwarf with more courage than sense" who runs anti-pirate patrols out of the western berths; a Dune Syndicate dock worker complains about the Dwarf captain who refuses to pay route fees and somehow gets away with it; or the party simply notices a battered sandship limping into berth with fresh scoring along its hull and a crew that moves with the tight coordination of people who have been in a fight recently and expect to be in another one soon.

**Torgun Ironfold**

The young Dwarf captain's name is **Torgun Ironfold**, and he is not what anyone expects. By Dwarven standards he is barely an adult — sixty-three years old, which in a species that measures maturity in centuries puts him somewhere between reckless and embarrassing. He is compact even for a Dwarf, wire-muscled, with close-cropped dark hair and a jaw that looks like it was designed for arguments. He wears sandship captain's gear — heavy boots, a scarred leather coat over light mail, sand-goggles pushed up on his forehead — and he moves through the Nightmarket with the particular confidence of someone who has decided that the world's problems are his responsibility and has not yet learned otherwise.

Torgun commands a patrol vessel called the *Fist of Garm* — a mid-sized sandship refitted for speed, with reinforced ramming prow, outboard catamaran structures for wing-in-ground pursuit, and weapon mounts that include a forward-facing magitek cannon and two harpoon launchers. The ship is crewed by fourteen volunteers, most of them young, all of them loyal to Torgun personally rather than to any cartel or guild. They run escort missions for the charity supply caravans that travel between the Nightmarket and the smaller outlying settlements — the same caravans that feed Garm's charity kitchen network, though neither Torgun nor his crew knows the ultimate source of the funding.

> *The captain is arguing with a dock worker when you first see him, and the argument is not going well for the dock worker. The Dwarf is a head shorter than the Human he is yelling at, which does not appear to slow him down. His voice carries across three berths.*
>
> *"I am not paying a route fee to escort supply ships that feed people who cannot feed themselves. If the Syndicate wants a fee, the Syndicate can send its own ships to run escort, and we will see which of us the caravans prefer to have alongside them. Until then, my route fee is zero, and you can tell Kellis I said so."*
>
> *The dock worker, who has clearly had this conversation before, makes a note on his ledger and walks away. The Dwarf watches him go, then turns and notices the party. His expression shifts — the anger drains away and is replaced by something sharper, an assessment. He has heard about the newcomers. Everyone in the Nightmarket has heard about the newcomers.*

Torgun is fiercely proud of his lineage but careful about who he tells. He is Garm's great-great-grandson — the last of a family line that has dwindled over the centuries as Garm's descendants scattered, married into other families, or simply stopped identifying with a Warlock ancestor whose legend had become more myth than history. Torgun's grandmother told him the stories, and he believed them when no one else did. He named his ship after his ancestor. He runs anti-pirate patrols because Garm's legend says the Warlock protected the common people, and Torgun has decided that protecting the common people is his inheritance.

He does not know that the Trademaster is Garm. He does not know that the charity caravans he escorts are funded by his own ancestor. He does not know that the coin he carries — passed from his grandmother, who received it from her mother, who received it from hers — is anything more than a family keepsake. He knows only that the coin shows his ancestor's face, that it was made by Casomir of Nexus, and that it has been in his family since the days before the Rainbow Road fell.

**The Pirate Problem**

Torgun approaches the party — or agrees to meet them, if they seek him out — with a proposition. A specific pirate captain named **Sandrazz** has been targeting the charity supply caravans with increasing boldness. Sandrazz operates a fast raider out of somewhere in the deep dunes, hits caravans on the Nightmarket's western approach, and disappears before the Dune Syndicate's patrol ships (which are slow and undermanned, because the Syndicate does not waste resources on charity convoys) can respond. The attacks have gotten worse over the past three months — Sandrazz has moved from stealing cargo to destroying ships, killing crews, and leaving wreckage as a message. Three caravans have been lost. The outlying settlements that depend on those supplies are running short.

Torgun has tracked Sandrazz's pattern and believes the next attack will come within two days, targeting a supply caravan currently loading at a settlement called Kassa's Hope, three hundred miles west of the Nightmarket. He plans to escort the caravan with the *Fist of Garm* and engage Sandrazz when the pirate strikes. He wants the party aboard — he has heard rumors about their capabilities, and he is not too proud to ask for help when people's lives are at stake.

**The Escort Run**

The journey to Kassa's Hope takes approximately sixteen hours by sandship. Torgun runs a disciplined ship — watches are assigned, stations are maintained, and the crew conducts weapon drills during the transit despite having done them a hundred times before. The party can spend the transit learning about sandship operations (any party member who assists with the drills gains a +2 circumstance bonus to relevant checks during the pirate engagement), talking with the crew, or speaking with Torgun privately.

Torgun is guarded at first — friendly but measured, offering opinions about the cartels and the state of Arlune without revealing anything personal. He is testing the party, though he does it naturally rather than deliberately. He watches how they treat his crew, whether they take the weapon drills seriously, whether they ask about the charity caravans out of genuine concern or strategic interest. If the party demonstrates sincere interest in the people the caravans feed, Torgun relaxes. If they treat the mission as a means to an end, he remains professional but distant.

The caravan — three cargo haulers loaded with food, water purification supplies, and magitek replacement parts — departs Kassa's Hope on schedule. The *Fist of Garm* runs escort position: slightly ahead and to windward, where Torgun's lookout can scan the dunes for dust plumes that signal approaching vessels.

**The Pirate Engagement**

Sandrazz strikes six hours into the return journey, in a stretch of open desert between two mesa formations — the ideal ambush point, with the mesas providing concealment for a vessel running dark. The pirate's ship, the *Razorwind*, is a stripped-down raider built for speed: catamaran outriggers, oversized drive wheels, minimal armor, and a crew of roughly twenty armed with crossbows, grappling hooks, and boarding axes. Sandrazz herself is a scarred Half-Elf woman with a reputation for violence that exceeds even the usual pirate standard.

The engagement unfolds in three phases, each resolved through a combination of narrative description and skill checks:

**Phase 1 — The Chase.** The *Razorwind* emerges from behind the eastern mesa at high speed, angling to cut off the lead cargo hauler. But the *Razorwind* is not alone. A second vessel — a smaller, faster skiff called the *Sand Flea* — breaks from behind the western mesa simultaneously, running a flanking approach on the rearmost cargo hauler. Sandrazz has upgraded her operation. Torgun swears and orders the *Fist of Garm* to intercept the *Razorwind* — the bigger threat — but the split attack means the party must make an immediate tactical decision: concentrate on the *Razorwind* and risk the *Sand Flea* reaching the rear hauler, or split their attention.

**Skill checks (multiple needed — the party should feel stretched):**
- Perception DC 20 to spot the *Sand Flea*'s approach early enough to warn the rear cargo hauler's captain, giving the hauler time to alter course and buy 2 additional rounds before the skiff closes.
- Knowledge (engineering) DC 20 to push the *Fist of Garm*'s magitek core past safe operating thresholds for a burst of speed, closing the gap on the *Razorwind* before it reaches the lead hauler. Failure means the core overheats and the ship's cannon is offline for the first 3 rounds of Phase 2 (the conduit feeds both systems).
- A party member can man the forward magitek cannon and fire on the *Razorwind* to force a course change (ranged attack, AC 15 at this range, with the −2 wind penalty). A hit does not damage the *Razorwind* significantly but forces Sandrazz to jink, buying the intercept another round.
- If the party has a spellcaster, they can target the *Sand Flea* from the observation deck — it is within long range for most offensive spells. Disabling or destroying the skiff here eliminates the flanking threat entirely, but the *Sand Flea* is moving fast and presents a small target (treat as AC 18 for spell targeting purposes due to speed and size).

**Phase 1 outcome determines Phase 2's shape.** If the party handles both threats, the boarding action is a straight fight. If the *Sand Flea* reaches the rear hauler, Phase 2 becomes a split engagement — the party must fight on the *Fist of Garm* while the hauler is being boarded simultaneously, creating pressure to finish fast.

**Phase 2 — The Boarding Action.** Sandrazz, cut off from the cargo haulers (or having damaged one if Phase 1 failed), turns to engage the *Fist of Garm* directly — she recognizes Torgun's ship and has a grudge. The ships close to grappling range. Harpoon lines and grappling hooks fly. The *Razorwind*'s crew attempts to board.

The boarding action takes place across the two ships' upper decks — the narrow external platforms, lookout towers, and weapon mounts that serve as the only exterior spaces on sealed sandships. The footing is treacherous (moving decks, sand spray, harpoon lines crossing between vessels), and the wind at speed makes ranged attacks difficult (−2 penalty to ranged attacks beyond 30 feet).

The engagement unfolds in waves, with simultaneous tactical pressures:

**Wave 1 (Round 1):** Eight pirate boarders (use the warrior NPC stat block, CR 3) swarm across the grappling lines in pairs, targeting the *Fist of Garm*'s weapon mounts and helm access hatch. Their objective is not to fight the party — it is to seize the ship's control points. If they reach the helm hatch (3 rounds of unopposed movement from the grappling points), they will breach the interior and attack the helmsman, causing the *Fist of Garm* to lose steering. The party must decide: engage the boarders directly, or hold position and let Torgun's crew handle them (the crew can manage, but will take casualties — 1d4 crew killed per round the boarders are unopposed at a control point).

**Wave 2 (Round 3):** Sandrazz and her lieutenants board. **Sandrazz** (use the rogue NPC stat block, CR 9) is accompanied by two pirate officers: **Krev** (fighter NPC stat block, CR 7), a massive Human in salvaged armor who fights with a magitek-enhanced boarding axe that deals an additional 1d6 fire damage on hit, and **Whisper** (ranger NPC stat block, CR 7), a Gnome sharpshooter who remains on the *Razorwind*'s lookout tower and fires on the *Fist of Garm*'s deck with a heavy crossbow, targeting anyone who appears to be casting spells or coordinating the defense. Whisper's position makes her difficult to reach in melee — she is across the gap between ships, 40 feet up, and the grappling lines are not stable enough to cross quickly while under fire.

**Sandrazz** fights with tactical intelligence befitting a veteran pirate captain. She uses Sneak Attack ruthlessly, positioning herself to flank with Krev against the party's most dangerous melee combatant. She targets Torgun when possible — she wants him dead, personally — but will not sacrifice a tactical advantage to reach him. She carries 2 alchemist's fire flasks and will use them against clustered enemies or to ignite grappling lines if the fight turns against her (cutting a grappling line the party is using to cross to the *Razorwind* is a particularly vicious move). If reduced below one-quarter hit points, she retreats to the *Razorwind* and orders the grappling lines cut, attempting to disengage. If trapped, she fights to the death.

**The Split Engagement (if the *Sand Flea* reached the rear hauler):** While the boarding action rages on the *Fist of Garm*, the rear cargo hauler is being boarded by the *Sand Flea*'s crew — six pirates and a pirate officer (fighter NPC stat block, CR 5). The hauler's crew are not fighters; they will be overwhelmed in 5 rounds. The party can see the hauler through the sand haze — it is roughly 200 feet away, close enough to hear the screams. Every round the party spends on their own fight is a round the hauler's crew is dying. If the party can dispatch or bypass the boarders on the *Fist of Garm* quickly enough, they can reach the hauler (via harpoon line, flight, or Torgun maneuvering the ship alongside) before the crew is killed. If 5 rounds pass without intervention, the hauler's crew is dead and its cargo is lost. This is the encounter's moral pressure point — it tests whether the party will risk their own safety to save others.

**Terrain hazards during the boarding:**
- **Sand spray:** The drive wheels of both ships throw sand constantly. Anyone on the lower deck platforms must succeed on a DC 14 Reflex save at the start of each round or be blinded for 1 round.
- **Harpoon lines:** Moving across harpoon lines between ships requires an Acrobatics check (DC 18, DC 14 if moving carefully at half speed). Failure means a fall — not to the ground (the ships are only two feet above the sand) but into the churning gap between two vessels moving at speed, taking 4d6 bludgeoning damage from the hulls and drive wheels and requiring a DC 16 Reflex save to grab something before being left behind.
- **Unstable footing:** The external decks are not designed for combat. Any character who takes a 5-foot step on the upper observation platforms must succeed on a DC 12 Acrobatics check or treat the step as normal movement (provoking attacks of opportunity). Charging on the external decks requires a DC 16 Acrobatics check or the character falls prone at the end of the charge.

**Phase 3 — The Aftermath.** The pirate crew breaks and surrenders once Sandrazz is defeated or flees and both her lieutenants are down. Torgun's crew secures the prisoners and assesses damage — the *Fist of Garm* has taken structural hits from the grappling and will need berth repairs. If the cargo haulers were hit, the party can assist with emergency repairs (Knowledge (engineering) DC 18 or relevant crafting skill). The caravan resumes its journey to the Nightmarket at reduced speed.

The casualties matter. If Torgun lost crew during the boarding, the mood on the return journey is somber. These were volunteers — friends, not employees. Torgun takes each loss personally, and the party sees the cost of the work he has chosen. If the rear hauler's crew was killed, the weight is heavier still — Torgun will blame himself for not anticipating the second ship, and the supplies that were lost represent meals that will not be served at the charity kitchens for weeks.

*GM note: This encounter should feel dangerous and require real tactical decision-making from the party. The combination of Sandrazz (CR 9) with two CR 7 lieutenants, the CR 3 boarder wave, the terrain hazards, and the potential split engagement with the cargo hauler creates a fight with multiple simultaneous problems that cannot all be solved at once. The party must prioritize: protect the helm or engage the boarders? Focus fire on Sandrazz or neutralize Whisper's crossbow? Hold position or cross to the hauler? Every choice has a cost. Torgun fights alongside the party during the boarding and is a competent combatant (use the rogue NPC stat block, CR 8), but he defers to the party's tactical decisions and does not overshadow them. He will, however, break formation to protect his crew if they are being killed — the party should notice this and factor it into their planning.*

**The Lucky Coin**

After the engagement, during the return journey to the Nightmarket, Torgun's demeanor toward the party shifts. If they fought well and — more importantly — if they ensured the cargo haulers and their crews survived (rather than pursuing Sandrazz at the expense of the convoy), Torgun's guarded professionalism gives way to something warmer. He invites the party to share a meal in the *Fist of Garm*'s small galley, and over food and drink, he tells them about his family.

> *"My grandmother used to tell me stories," Torgun says, turning something small and metallic over in his fingers. "About Garm the Honest. Not the legend — the person. The things he did after the Road fell, the people he saved, the infrastructure he repaired. She said he was funny, believe it or not. That he laughed easily and loudly and that people liked being around him because he made them feel like things were going to be all right, even when they obviously weren't."*
>
> *He holds up the coin. It catches the lamplight — old metal, worn smooth by generations of handling. One side shows a Dwarf's face, round and bearded, caught mid-laugh. The other shows a pile of treasure, rendered with the kind of precise detail that suggests the artist had strong opinions about coinage.*
>
> *"He's my great-great-grandfather. This was his. A friend made it for him — Casomir, the one who built the Rainbow Road. It's been in my family since before the worlds went dark." He turns the coin over again, studying a face that looks remarkably like his own. "I don't know what good it does. It doesn't open any doors. But it's his, and it means something to me, and my grandmother said that someday I'd know who to give it to."*

Torgun does not give the coin freely. The party must earn it through their actions during the pirate engagement and their conduct toward the people the caravans serve. The key factors are:

- **Did the party prioritize the cargo haulers' safety over pursuit of Sandrazz?** Torgun cares about the mission, not the glory. If the party abandoned the convoy to chase the pirate, he noticed.
- **How did the party treat the crew?** Torgun's people are volunteers, not soldiers. If the party treated them as expendable or gave orders that put crew at unnecessary risk, Torgun's trust diminishes.
- **Did the party take the weapon drills seriously?** A small detail, but Torgun reads it as an indicator of character — people who prepare for danger respect the danger, and people who respect the danger respect the people who face it.

If the party's conduct was honorable, Torgun offers the coin after telling his story. A Diplomacy check (DC 22) can also secure the coin if the party's conduct was mixed — Torgun wants to believe in them, and a sincere appeal to the mission's importance can tip the balance. If the party's conduct was poor (abandoned the convoy, got crew killed through negligence), no check can secure the coin in this scene — Torgun will reconsider later only if the party demonstrates a change of character during subsequent scenes.

**The Coin**

The Lucky Coin is a small silver piece, old and worn but unmistakable. The laughing Dwarf on the obverse is clearly a portrait — the face is specific, individual, full of life in a way that decorative coinage never achieves. Anyone who has seen Dwarven portraiture recognizes the craftsmanship as exceptional. The pile of treasure on the reverse is rendered with an engraver's obsessive precision, each coin and gem in the heap distinct and countable. The coin radiates a faint aura of transmutation magic (*detect magic*, CL 20th) — not a functional enchantment, but the residual imprint of having been crafted by one of the most powerful magitek engineers in history. Casomir made this coin with his own hands, and his magic lingers in the metal.

The coin's significance is entirely social, not mechanical. It has no game effect. What it does is open doors: showing it to the right people in the Nightmarket triggers the chain of contacts that leads to Garm (see Scene 4). Without it, finding the Trademaster is vastly harder, and Garm's initial reaction to the party is hostile rather than curious.

**If the Party Skips This Scene**

The Lucky Coin side quest is optional. If the party does not pursue the pirate problem, does not encounter Torgun, or fails to earn the coin, Scene 4 (Finding the Trademaster) becomes significantly more difficult. The hard path through Scene 4 is viable but slower, more dangerous, and produces a meeting with Garm that begins from a position of suspicion rather than hope. The GM should not force the party into this side quest, but should ensure the hooks are visible — Mira Softfoot's mention of the supply caravan attacks, dock gossip about the Dwarf captain, the battered ship limping into berth — and let the party decide.

*GM note: Torgun is the chapter's emotional anchor outside of Garm himself. He represents what Garm could have been — open, brave, stubbornly idealistic — and what Garm's corruption cost him: the ability to be known. Torgun wears his ancestor's name on his ship and carries his face in his pocket and has no idea that the person he idolizes is alive and watching him from the shadows. Garm knows about Torgun (the Trademaster knows about everything in Arlune), and the knowledge is one of the things that makes his isolation unbearable. Play Torgun as genuine — not naive, not foolish, but young enough to believe that doing the right thing is its own justification and old enough to know that it costs. The pirate fight should be exciting, but the real scene is the galley conversation afterward, and the coin changing hands should feel like a trust being placed, not a reward being collected.*

---

### Scene 4: Finding the Trademaster

This scene is the chapter's investigation and social core. Garm is invisible. His network is vast. He does not want to be found — and for five centuries, no one has found him who he did not wish to. The party's experience of this scene depends entirely on whether they possess the Lucky Coin from Scene 3.

**The Trademaster's Methods**

Before running this scene, the GM should understand how Garm operates, because the party will encounter the architecture of his secrecy at every turn.

The Trademaster always works through intermediaries. His system relies on multiple layers of agents, each knowing only the identity of their immediate contact above and below them in the chain. No agent has ever met the Trademaster. No agent can confirm the Trademaster exists. The contacts are not permanent employees — Garm works exclusively through mercenaries and freelancers, hired for specific jobs, paid generously, and released. No one stays in his employ long enough to learn anything useful, and the payment itself arrives through mechanisms designed to prevent tracing: locked chests delivered by magitek constructs that self-destruct after handoff, anonymous accounts routed through Voidrunner shell operations, barter arrangements settled through third parties who do not know what they are facilitating.

The Trademaster maintains a massive misinformation apparatus. Planted rumors, false sightings, contradictory descriptions, decoy operations — all designed to make the Trademaster impossible to identify or locate. At any given time, at least a dozen false leads are circulating through the Nightmarket, each plausible enough to consume weeks of investigation before dead-ending. The apparatus is self-sustaining; at this point, the population generates its own misinformation through speculation, and Garm merely nudges it when a particular theory gets too close to the truth.

He occasionally takes to the field personally, operating under the guise of "one of the Trademaster's agents" — a cover that allows him to interact directly with situations that require his attention while maintaining his anonymity. He never uses the same identity more than a few times. He has been a dozen different people in the last century, and none of them exist. When he is not in the field, he is a nondescript Gnome merchant named **Tallis** who operates a small curio shop in the lower bazaar, sells inexpensive trinkets, and is so thoroughly unremarkable that no one has ever thought to look at him twice. The curio shop is real. The merchandise is real. Tallis has been a fixture of the lower bazaar for forty years, and his neighbours consider him pleasant, boring, and utterly without secrets.

No one knows that the Trademaster secretly funds the charity kitchen network. No one knows that the Trademaster has no standing criminal organization — the widely believed rumor of a vast syndicate is itself a piece of misinformation that Garm cultivated decades ago, and it serves him well because it makes the other cartels respect a threat that does not exist. No one knows that the fragile equilibrium between the seven cartels is maintained not by mutual deterrence alone but by Garm's constant, invisible intervention — nudging, sabotaging, redirecting, and occasionally eliminating threats to the balance through operations so layered that the cartels blame each other for the results.

The net effect: the Trademaster is simultaneously everywhere and nowhere. Everyone in Arlune has a theory about who he is. All of the theories are wrong.

**The Coin Path**

If the party possesses the Lucky Coin, finding Garm is a matter of days rather than weeks. The coin is not magical — it does not glow, it does not point the way, it does not speak. What it does is mark the bearer as someone who has earned the trust of Garm's last living descendant, and in Garm's network, that matters.

The chain begins when the party shows the coin to the right person. Several NPCs in the Nightmarket can recognize the coin for what it is and start the contact chain:

- **Mira Softfoot** (the charity kitchen cook from Scene 2) recognizes the Dwarf face on the coin — she has seen old portraits of Garm in historical texts and makes the connection. She does not know the Trademaster is Garm, but she knows someone who collects Warlock-era relics and might know what the coin means. She provides an introduction to a curio dealer in the lower bazaar (this is, of course, Garm's own shop, though Mira does not know that).
- **Silvana Morne** (the Exchange, Cartel 4) recognizes the coin immediately — not its history, but its significance as an artifact that predates the cartels. She offers to buy it (2,000 gp, negotiable upward). If the party refuses to sell, she respects their decision and offers information instead: the coin matches a description in her files, flagged years ago by a contact who claimed it was connected to the Trademaster's inner circle. She provides a dead-drop location where the party can leave a message.
- **Torgun**, if the party returns to him, can point them toward a specific vendor in the lower bazaar — "an old Gnome who sells junk, but my grandmother used to visit him whenever she came to the Nightmarket. She said he was trustworthy." This is Garm's curio shop. Torgun does not know who Tallis really is.

Each of these paths converges on the same point: a contact chain that leads, through two or three intermediaries, to a meeting. The intermediaries are varied — a sandship mechanic who passes the party a folded note with a time and location, a street vendor who sells them a specific trinket that turns out to contain a coded message inside, a charity kitchen worker who tells them to be at a particular intersection at moonrise. Each link in the chain has been used before and will not be used again. The party cannot trace the chain backward; each intermediary knows only the contact who recruited them and the message they were given.

The meeting takes place three days after the chain is initiated, in a location of Garm's choosing — a private room in a mid-tier bazaar tea house, the kind of place where quiet conversations are expected and no one looks twice at unfamiliar faces. The party is instructed to come unarmed; they will not be searched, but the instruction itself is a test of compliance. Garm will know whether they are armed (he has agents watching the approaches) and will adjust his assessment of them accordingly.

**The Hard Path**

Without the Lucky Coin, finding the Trademaster is a grueling exercise in investigation, dead ends, and escalating risk. The party must navigate the Nightmarket's information ecosystem without a key, and every inquiry they make is observed, recorded, and sold to the highest bidder.

The investigation proceeds through a series of Diplomacy (gather information) and Knowledge (local) checks, each representing days of legwork:

- **Initial inquiries (DC 18, 2 days):** The party can ask around about the Trademaster. The results are the same rumors from Scene 2's Gather Information section — myths, theories, contradictions. Nothing actionable. The process does, however, attract attention: the Exchange notes the party's interest and may approach with an offer to sell information (see below), and the other cartels begin watching the newcomers more carefully.
- **Following the charity kitchen trail (DC 22, 3 days):** If the party investigates the charity kitchen funding — the most productive lead available — they can trace the donation chain through several intermediary layers. Each layer requires its own check: the first intermediary is a merchant's guild that passes funds through a shared account (DC 18 to identify); the second is a magitek construct that manages a series of shell accounts (DC 22 to trace); the third is a dead end — the construct's operating instructions are sealed and self-erasing, and the accounts terminate in a name that does not correspond to any living person. The trail goes cold. However, the party's investigation of the construct attracts Garm's attention — he monitors the charity network's security closely, and anyone sophisticated enough to trace it three layers deep is worth watching.
- **Purchasing information from the Exchange (1,000–5,000 gp):** Silvana Morne will sell the party what she knows, which is considerable but incomplete. Her intelligence includes: a confirmed list of people who are *not* the Trademaster (every cartel leader, every major merchant, every political figure of note — she has investigated and eliminated them all); a pattern analysis of the Trademaster's interventions over the past decade, showing the timing and targets of operations that disrupted cartel power plays; and the location of three dead-drop sites that her agents have identified as part of the Trademaster's communication network. The dead-drops are real but monitored — using them draws the party deeper into Garm's network, which is the point.
- **Using the dead-drops (DC 20, 1 day each):** The party can leave messages at the dead-drop locations. The first message receives no response. The second message receives a counter-message — a single question, written on treated paper that dissolves after reading: "Why should I trust you?" The party's answer to this question determines whether Garm agrees to a meeting. A sincere answer that references the Rainbow Road, the Warlocks, or the mission to heal the worlds triggers a response within 48 hours. A deceptive or threatening answer triggers silence — and the dead-drop network goes dark, the locations abandoned within hours.

The hard-path meeting, if it occurs, takes place in a location of Garm's choosing — but it is not the comfortable tea house of the coin path. It is a subterranean chamber beneath a derelict warehouse in the Nightmarket's industrial quarter, accessible through a series of narrow tunnels that force the party to move single-file. Garm is there, but so are contingencies: magitek alarm wards, escape routes, and at least two mercenary teams positioned in adjacent tunnels. The meeting is tense. Garm is suspicious, guarded, and ready to disappear at the first sign of hostility.

**The Meeting**

Regardless of the path taken, the party eventually sits across from a person who does not look like a Warlock, does not look like a criminal mastermind, and does not look like the most powerful person on Arlune.

> *The Gnome is small, even by Gnome standards, and dressed in the shapeless linen layers that half the bazaar vendors wear. His face is round, weathered, and entirely forgettable — the kind of face you see a hundred times a day in the Nightmarket and never remember. He sits with his hands folded on the table, a cup of tea in front of him that he has not touched. His eyes, when they find yours, are the only thing about him that does not belong to a shopkeeper. They are old. Not physically — Gnomes age slowly, and he could be any age between fifty and five hundred — but in the way they watch you, the way they take in every detail of your posture, your weapons, your expressions, and file it all away behind a mask of mild pleasantness.*
>
> *"So," he says. His voice is unremarkable. "You've been looking for me. Or rather, you've been looking for the Trademaster, which is the same thing, though I'd appreciate it if you didn't spread that around. I've spent quite a lot of effort on not being found, and I'd hate to think it was wasted."*

**Garm in Conversation**

Garm is shrewd, careful, and deeply lonely. Five centuries of enforced secrecy have made him brilliant at reading people but incapable of trusting them. He conducts the meeting as an interrogation disguised as a conversation — he asks questions that sound casual and listens to the answers with the attention of someone whose life has depended on reading lies for longer than most civilizations last. He wants to know why the party is here, what they know about the Warlocks, and what they intend to do. He does not volunteer information until he is satisfied that the party is sincere.

If the party has the Lucky Coin, the meeting's emotional register shifts. Garm recognizes Casomir's work the moment the coin is produced — the catch in his breath is the first genuine emotion he has shown anyone in decades. He takes the coin, turns it over, studies the laughing face that is unmistakably his own, and for a long moment he says nothing. When he speaks again, the mask of mild pleasantness is thinner. The shopkeeper is still there, but something underneath is showing through — something tired, and relieved, and afraid to hope.

> *"Casomir made this," he says quietly, turning the coin in his fingers. "After our first job together. We cleaned out a bandit camp in the hills above Nexus — this was before any of it, before the Road, before the Compact. We were young and stupid and we thought we were going to be rich." He almost smiles. "The treasure was worthless. Copper and costume jewelry. Casomir thought that was the funniest thing he'd ever seen. He made the coin that night — said it was to remind me that the real treasure was the friends we made along the way. He was always saying things like that. Sincerely. Without any trace of irony. It was infuriating."*

Without the coin, Garm is colder. He tests the party through the conversation — presenting false information to see if they correct him (testing their knowledge), making provocative statements to gauge their reactions (testing their temperament), and at one point excusing himself for several minutes to consult with unseen advisors (testing their patience and whether they attempt to eavesdrop or explore the meeting space while he is absent). The hard-path meeting takes twice as long and produces a more fragile trust — Garm agrees to work with the party, but his commitment is conditional and his contingency plans remain active.

**Garm's Emotional Core**

Garm's corruption is unique among the Warlocks. His defining virtue — honesty, trustworthiness — has been inverted not into dishonesty but into pathological secrecy. He is incapable of being known. Every interaction is filtered through layers of identity, misdirection, and operational security. He has not told anyone his real name in centuries. He has not allowed anyone to see his true face (he uses a *hat of disguise* constantly, maintaining the Tallis persona even in private). He has built a system so perfectly designed to protect him that it has become his prison.

The Lucky Coin cracks through this because it is proof that someone remembers the person inside the system. Casomir remembered. Casomir's gift survived. Torgun carried it. And now strangers are sitting across from Garm, holding the face of the man he used to be, and asking him to be that man again.

If the party handles the meeting well — with sincerity, patience, and respect for what Garm has endured — his walls come down, not all at once but visibly. He stops testing them. He stops lying (or at least stops lying about things that matter). He tells them the truth: he wants to turn. He has wanted to for a long time.
