But the cartel equilibrium he built is the only thing keeping Arlune from civil war. If he reveals himself, the cartels will either try to kill him or scramble to fill the power vacuum. He needs the party's help to weaken the most dangerous cartels and expose the Asura cartel before he can safely step into the open.

He proposes three tasks, to be completed in sequence, each building on the last.

**Garm's Proposal**

> *"I have been the Trademaster for a very long time," Garm says. "Long enough that the Trademaster has become more real than I am. The cartels, the equilibrium, the invisible hand — I built all of it to keep Arlune from tearing itself apart after the Road fell. And it worked. People are alive who would not be alive without it. But the system requires me to be invisible, and invisible people cannot lead. They can only manage. And I am very, very tired of managing."*
>
> *He sets the tea cup aside — still untouched — and leans forward. "I want to come in from the cold. I want to stand in the Nightmarket and say my name out loud and not have to disappear afterward. But if I do that now, today, with the cartels intact and the equilibrium in place, the whole thing collapses. The cartels will tear each other apart fighting over the vacuum, and the common people will be caught in the middle. I have seen what that looks like. I will not allow it to happen again."*
>
> *"So. Three tasks. You weaken the cartels that need weakening. You expose the threat that no one else can see. And when the ground is prepared, I step into the open and we build something better. Together."*

Garm outlines the three tasks in general terms during this meeting; the specifics — targets, approaches, intelligence — are provided at the start of each task in Scene 5. The party should understand the arc: break the water monopoly (freeing the most essential resource from cartel control), recover stolen infrastructure (securing the tools for reconstruction), and expose the Asura cartel (eliminating the hidden enemy). Each task serves Garm's strategic goals and simultaneously proves the party's capability and commitment.

If the party agrees, Garm provides a contact method — a specific phrase spoken to a specific vendor in the bazaar, changed weekly — and instructs them to report back after each task. He does not accompany them. He does not offer direct assistance beyond intelligence. He watches from the shadows, as he always has, and he waits to see if trust is a thing he can still afford.

*GM note: This scene lives or dies on the GM's portrayal of Garm. He is not a quest-giver dispensing objectives from behind a desk. He is a person who has spent five hundred years alone inside a machine of his own construction, and the party is the first real chance he has had to escape it. Play him as intelligent, cautious, and deeply human underneath the operational brilliance. The Casomir memory — if the coin is present — should land with weight; it is the first time Garm has spoken about his friend to anyone, and the emotion is not performed, it is involuntary. The coin path meeting should feel like watching someone decide, in real time, to trust again. The hard path meeting should feel like watching someone decide whether they can afford to. Both are compelling — but the coin path is warmer, and the party should feel the difference.*

---

### Scene 5: Garm's Tasks

Garm's three tasks are presented linearly — each must be completed before the next becomes available. The tasks escalate in difficulty, scope, and stakes, and each builds on the intelligence and relationships established by the previous one. Garm provides a briefing for each task through his contact network (a sealed dossier delivered by a magitek construct to the party's lodgings, containing maps, personnel files, and operational intelligence that demonstrate the Trademaster's reach). Between tasks, the party has downtime in the Nightmarket — time to rest, resupply, follow up on side leads, and observe the effects of their work on the city.

**Task 1: The Water Monopoly**

Garm's briefing arrives the morning after the party agrees to help. The dossier is thorough: floor plans of the Wellkeepers' central purification complex, shift schedules for the guard rotations, engineering schematics for the magitek purification systems, and a concise tactical assessment in Garm's own hand.

> *"The Wellkeepers control water. Water controls everything. As long as Hosta Glimwell's family holds the purification systems, the Nightmarket's population exists at her discretion. This is the first lock that needs to break. Do not destroy the purification infrastructure — Arlune cannot survive without it. Liberate it. The systems are magitek; they can be operated by anyone with the training. The engineers who maintain them work under Wellkeeper contract, not out of loyalty. Free the systems, and the engineers will follow."*

**The Wellkeepers' Complex**

The central purification facility occupies a hollowed-out section of the mesa beneath the Nightmarket's upper tier — a sprawling underground installation built around a natural aquifer, expanded and reinforced over centuries with magitek infrastructure. The complex has three levels: the upper level (administrative offices, guard barracks, the main entrance from the mesa's interior), the middle level (the purification chambers themselves — rows of magitek filtration units connected by pressurized conduit networks, maintained by a staff of twenty engineers working in shifts), and the lower level (the aquifer access tunnels — natural caverns that have been shored up with magitek reinforcement, where the raw water is drawn from the deep stone).

The complex is guarded, but not fortified for war. The Wellkeepers have relied on their monopoly's essentiality as their primary defense — no one attacks the water supply, because everyone needs the water supply. The guard force consists of roughly thirty Wellkeeper enforcers (warrior NPC stat block, CR 3) supplemented by Ironbound contractors on the upper level (four brothers, CR 3, on rotating watch). The Ironbound are contracted security, not fanatics — they will honor their contract but they will not die for it, and if the situation deteriorates beyond their terms of engagement they will withdraw in good order.

**Approaches:**

The party has several viable approaches, and Garm's intelligence supports all of them:

- **Infiltration:** The party can enter the complex disguised as engineering contractors (Garm provides forged credentials, Disguise DC 18 to pass the upper-level checkpoint) or through the aquifer tunnels (Knowledge (dungeoneering) DC 18 to navigate, Stealth DC 20 to avoid the lower-level patrols). The infiltration route allows the party to reach the purification chambers without fighting through the guard force, enabling them to secure the magitek systems and present the Wellkeeper engineers with a fait accompli.
- **Social engineering:** The party can approach the Wellkeeper engineers directly. Garm's dossier identifies three engineers who are sympathetic — underpaid, overworked, and privately disgusted by the cartel's pricing practices. A Diplomacy check (DC 20) or a demonstration of the party's intent (such as evidence that the water monopoly can be broken without destroying the infrastructure) can turn one or more engineers into allies who provide interior access and technical assistance.
- **Direct assault:** The party can fight their way in through the main entrance. This is the loudest and most disruptive option — it puts the entire Nightmarket on alert, damages the party's reputation with the other cartels (who will see them as destabilizing), and risks collateral damage to the purification systems. It works, but it costs.

**The Aghasura**

Regardless of the approach taken, the party discovers something unexpected in the complex's lowest level. In the deepest aquifer tunnel — a natural cavern that extends beneath the mesa's foundation into uncharted stone — an aghasura (CR 11) has been nesting. The creature has been there for months, positioned by the Sandwalker cartel (the Asura cartel) as a contingency. Its purpose: if the water monopoly ever falls, the aghasura is to contaminate the aquifer with its poison, rendering the water supply deadly and creating a crisis that drives the population further into cartel dependence.

The aghasura has been patient. It has not moved from its position in the deepest cavern, and its attraction aura has been drawing vermin and small creatures to it as a food supply. The Wellkeeper engineers know something is wrong in the deep tunnels — equipment left in the lower caverns has been found corroded, and two maintenance workers who ventured too deep did not return — but they attribute the problems to natural hazards and have simply stopped going below the third sub-level.

**Encounter: The Aghasura**

The party encounters the aghasura when they reach the aquifer's deepest access point — either during infiltration (if they took the tunnel route), during a sweep of the complex after securing the upper levels, or because the engineers ask them to investigate the deep-tunnel disappearances.

- **Enemies:** 1 aghasura (CR 11).
- **Terrain:** A natural cavern roughly 80 feet in diameter with a 30-foot ceiling. The aquifer pool dominates the center — dark water, deep, cold. The aghasura is coiled on a rock shelf above the pool, motionless, its attraction aura active. Stalactites provide partial cover for ranged attackers but also partial concealment for the Huge serpentine creature. The only entrance is a 10-foot-wide tunnel from the third sub-level.
- **Tactics:** The aghasura fights as an ambush predator. It remains motionless as the party enters, relying on its attraction aura to draw them closer (Will DC 21 or be compelled to approach). Once targets are within 15-foot reach, it strikes — dual scimitars with poison (Fort DC 23, 1d4 Con damage per round for 6 rounds), bite with grab, and swallow whole against any creature it grapples. If the party clusters, it uses *cloudkill* (DC 19) to fill the cavern entrance, cutting off retreat and forcing the fight to continue in poisonous gas. It uses *deeper darkness* to eliminate light sources if the party is relying on them, fighting by scent in the dark.
- **Complications:** The aquifer pool is the Nightmarket's water supply. If the aghasura is killed above or in the pool, its body contaminates the water with residual poison — the engineers can purify it, but the process takes 48 hours, during which the Nightmarket's water ration is halved. The party can avoid this by luring the aghasura away from the pool (its reach and size make this difficult but not impossible — it cannot fit through the 10-foot tunnel easily, and it knows this, which is why it chose the cavern as a lair).

**Aftermath**

With the aghasura destroyed and the purification complex secured, the party can hand control of the water systems to the engineers, who are more than willing to operate independently of the Wellkeepers. Hosta Glimwell's monopoly is broken. The news travels through the Nightmarket within hours — free water, distributed by the engineers themselves, no cartel surcharge. The common people's reaction is stunned disbelief followed by a wave of emotion that the party can witness from the bazaar: people laughing, people crying, people filling containers at the public taps and not being charged.

Hosta herself does not resist militarily — she is pragmatic enough to see the situation clearly, and her guard force is insufficient to retake the complex against a party capable of killing an aghasura. She withdraws to her personal compound and begins maneuvering politically, which will cause problems for Garm later but is not the party's immediate concern.

The Asura connection — the aghasura in the aquifer — is the first concrete evidence that something deeper than cartel politics is at work on Arlune. If the party reports this to Garm, his reaction is telling: he suspected something was wrong with the deep infrastructure but had no proof. The discovery confirms his instinct that the Sandwalker cartel is not what it appears to be, and it sharpens his intelligence on Task 3.

**Milestone: Level 11 awarded after completing Task 1.**

**Task 2: The Stolen Engine**

Garm's second briefing arrives the day after the party reports completion of Task 1. The dossier is thinner this time — the Voidrunners are harder to penetrate than the Wellkeepers, and Garm's intelligence on their interior operations is limited.

> *"Fen Quickstep's people acquired something they should not have — a magitek engine core that dates to the era when the Rainbow Road was still operational. It is not a weapon. It is a piece of Arlune's original infrastructure, and I need it for what comes after. The reconstruction of this world's public systems — water distribution, food generation, sandship maintenance — requires components that no longer exist. This engine core is one of them. Recover it. The Voidrunners' warehouse complex is in the deep bazaar. I can tell you where the entrances are, but not what is inside — Fen redesigns his interior defenses on a quarterly cycle, and my last reliable information is three months old."*

**The Voidrunner Warehouse**

Fen Quickstep's headquarters is a maze — literally. The warehouse complex consists of seven interconnected buildings in the deep bazaar, joined by tunnels, false walls, concealed passages, and trapped corridors that Fen redesigns regularly to prevent infiltration. The exterior is unremarkable: corrugated metal walls, cargo doors, the standard industrial architecture of the bazaar's working district. The interior is a paranoid's masterpiece.

Garm's intelligence provides the locations of three entrances (the main cargo door, a rooftop access hatch, and a sewer connection that runs beneath the complex) and a general floor plan that is explicitly noted as potentially outdated. The party must navigate the interior by observation, skill, and improvisation.

**The Heist**

This task is designed as an infiltration/heist scenario rather than a combat dungeon. The Voidrunners are criminals, not soldiers, and Fen's warehouse relies on traps, locks, and misdirection rather than overwhelming force. The party should feel like they are cracking a vault, not storming a fortress.

**Exterior Security:** The warehouse is watched by Voidrunner sentries on the surrounding rooftops (Perception +15). The main cargo door is locked and trapped (Disable Device DC 25 to bypass the lock, DC 22 to disarm the alarm trap). The rooftop hatch is less secure (DC 20 lock, no trap) but requires climbing the building (Climb DC 15, or flight). The sewer connection is untrapped but partially flooded and requires navigating a tight crawlspace (Escape Artist DC 18 for Medium creatures, Small creatures pass without a check).

**Interior Hazards:** The warehouse interior is a gauntlet of countermeasures:
- **Trapped corridors:** Pressure plates trigger magitek alarms (Perception DC 22 to spot, Disable Device DC 22 to disarm) that seal the corridor and flood it with a disorienting alchemical fog (Fort DC 18 or be nauseated for 1d4 rounds).
- **False rooms:** Several chambers are designed to look like storage areas but are actually dead ends rigged with delayed collapse traps (Reflex DC 20 to escape, 4d6 bludgeoning damage).
- **Patrol constructs:** Fen employs magitek constructs (use the animated object stat block, CR 5, Small size, fast and quiet) that patrol the corridors on randomized routes. They do not attack — they raise alarms, and the alarm brings Voidrunner response teams (4 warriors, CR 5, arriving in 1d4+1 rounds).
- **The shifting layout:** Fen's quarterly redesign means some of Garm's information is wrong. One of the three entrance routes has been sealed since Garm's last intelligence update (GM's choice which one — the party discovers this after committing to the route). The party must adapt on the fly.

**The Engine Core**

The engine core is stored in Fen's personal vault — a reinforced chamber in the complex's deepest sub-level, behind a magitek lock that requires both a physical key (which Fen carries on his person) and a spoken passphrase. The vault door is resistant to brute force (hardness 15, 200 hp, immune to energy damage) but can be bypassed with *knock* (CL check DC 26) or a DC 30 Disable Device check.

Fen is in the complex during the heist — he lives here, sleeps here, and rarely leaves. He is in his personal workshop adjacent to the vault, studying the engine core, and he is not alone. A japalisura (CR 12) has been contracted by the Sandwalker cartel as a "security consultant" for the Voidrunners — Fen believes it is a mercenary specialist hired through intermediaries. In reality, the japalisura is monitoring the engine core on behalf of the Asura cartel, ensuring that this piece of Rainbow Road-era infrastructure remains out of circulation.

**Encounter: The Japalisura**

The encounter triggers when the party attempts to access the vault or is discovered in the sub-level. Fen raises the alarm and retreats; the japalisura engages.

- **Enemies:** 1 japalisura (CR 12), plus Fen Quickstep (rogue NPC stat block, CR 7, fights defensively and attempts to flee) and 2 Voidrunner guards (warrior NPC stat block, CR 5).
- **Terrain:** The sub-level is a series of connected chambers — Fen's workshop (cluttered with magitek components, providing cover), the vault antechamber (open, 30 feet square), and two flanking storage rooms. The japalisura uses the workshop's clutter for cover and the storage rooms' doorways as chokepoints. Ceiling height is 15 feet.
- **Tactics:** The japalisura fights from range — it climbs to the ceiling (Climb +21) and fires both composite shortbows simultaneously (+20/+20/+15/+15/+10, 1d6+4 damage per hit), using *flame arrow* to ignite its ammunition. It uses the workshop's magitek components as improvised obstacles, shooting out supports to collapse shelving onto melee combatants (Reflex DC 16, 2d6 bludgeoning). It has *see invisibility* active at all times and will use *true seeing* if the party employs significant illusion or concealment. If forced into melee, it switches to handaxes and fights defensively until it can create distance. If reduced below 40 hp, it uses *dimension door* to retreat to the surface and flee the Nightmarket entirely — reporting to the Sandwalker cartel that the engine core has been compromised.
- **Fen Quickstep:** Fen is not suicidal. He is brilliant, erratic, and attached to the engine core, but he is also a pragmatist who recognizes when he is outmatched. If the party defeats the japalisura and confronts Fen, a Diplomacy check (DC 22) or Intimidate check (DC 18) convinces him to open the vault voluntarily. He demands to know what the engine core is for — and if told the truth (infrastructure reconstruction), he is grudgingly impressed. "That's the first sensible thing anyone on this world has tried to do with a piece of magitek in my lifetime."

**Aftermath**

The engine core is a cylindrical device roughly two feet long, etched with arcane circuitry of a complexity that modern magitek engineers cannot fully decipher. It pulses with a low, warm light when handled — not hot, not cold, but alive in a way that modern magitek components are not. The party can feel the difference: this was built when the Rainbow Road was active, when arcane energy flowed freely between the worlds, and the quality of the craftsmanship reflects an era that no longer exists.

Garm receives the engine core with visible emotion — he handles it with the care of someone holding a piece of his own past. He does not explain what he intends to do with it in detail, but the party can see that this is not merely a strategic asset. It is a promise that the world he is building will be better than the one he has been managing.

The japalisura's presence — if the party noticed and reported it — deepens Garm's suspicion of the Sandwalker cartel. A six-armed archer with divination abilities and infernal arrows is not a standard Nightmarket mercenary. Garm's network begins a focused investigation of Cartel 7, and the intelligence gathered feeds directly into Task 3.

**Task 3: The Seventh Cartel**

Garm's third briefing is different. It arrives not as a sealed dossier but as a personal meeting — Garm contacts the party through his network and meets them himself, in the tea house (coin path) or the underground chamber (hard path). His usual composure is strained. The intelligence his network has gathered since Task 2 has confirmed what he suspected but could not prove.

> *"The Sandwalkers are not a cartel. They are an occupation force."*
>
> *Garm spreads a map across the table — hand-drawn, detailed, annotated in his own precise script. The map shows the Twilight Sea, with the Nightmarket at center and the deep-desert territories radiating outward. Red marks cover the periphery: settlements abandoned over the past decade, oases claimed by the Sandwalkers, caravan routes that have gone silent. The pattern is unmistakable.*
>
> *"The deep-desert predator attacks. The disappearing caravans. The settlements emptying out. It is coordinated. The Sandwalkers have been driving populations toward the Nightmarket for decades — concentrating them. Every refugee who arrives in the bazaar, every family that abandons a peripheral settlement and moves to the core, makes the population denser, more dependent, more controllable. And the Sandwalkers expand into the vacuum."*
>
> *He taps a point on the map's eastern edge — a cluster of oasis symbols deep in the trackless dunes. "This is their headquarters. My network has finally confirmed its location. What my network cannot confirm is what is inside, because everyone I have sent to investigate has not come back."*
>
> *He looks at the party, and for the first time, the Trademaster's legendary composure shows a crack. "The creatures you killed in the water facility and the Voidrunner warehouse — those were demons. I have been running this world for five centuries and I did not know there were demons in it. That failure is mine, and it ends now."*

**The Deep-Desert Journey**

The Sandwalker headquarters is located approximately five hundred miles east of the Nightmarket, in a region of the Twilight Sea that no established trade route crosses. If the party completed the Lucky Coin side quest, Torgun Ironfold volunteers the *Fist of Garm* and its crew for the mission — he has been itching for a real fight since the pirate engagement, and the deep desert is exactly the kind of danger he was born to charge into headfirst. Torgun's ship is faster and better armed than anything Garm could provide, and his crew's loyalty and combat experience make them ideal for an operation this far from support. If the party did not complete the side quest, Garm provides a fast scout vessel crewed by four of his most trusted operatives (mercenaries who have worked for the Trademaster long enough to be reliable, though they still do not know his true identity). Either way, the journey takes approximately thirty hours at full speed.

The deep desert is different from the routes the party has traveled before. The dunes are taller — some cresting a hundred feet — and the sand itself changes color as they move east, shifting from pale silver to a deep, bruised purple that seems to absorb the moonlight rather than reflect it. The crew grows quieter as the terrain changes. This is uncharted territory, beyond the maps, beyond the caravan routes, beyond the reach of any cartel except the one they are hunting.

> *The moon hangs low on the horizon, casting long shadows across dunes that rise and fall like the swells of a frozen ocean. The sand here is darker than anything you have seen on Arlune — a deep violet that seems to drink the light. The ship's magitek core hums at a higher pitch, working harder against the denser sand. No one speaks. The crew watches the dunes through the viewports with the focused attention of people who expect something to come up from below.*

**The Oasis Fortress**

The Sandwalker headquarters is built around a deep-desert oasis — one of the largest on Arlune, fed by an underground river that surfaces in a natural basin surrounded by rock formations. The oasis is real; the fortress built around it is a horror. What appears from a distance to be a standard desert settlement — low walls, sandship berths, storage buildings — reveals itself on approach as something alien. The architecture is wrong. The angles are too precise, the proportions inhuman, and the material is not stone or metal but a dark, glassy substance that resembles obsidian but radiates a faint warmth. The buildings were not constructed by mortal hands.

The party's approach options:

- **Direct approach:** The sandship runs straight at the fortress. The party arrives fast but visible — the Asuras will have time to prepare.
- **Stealthy approach:** The party disembarks from the sandship several miles out and approaches on foot through the dunes, using the terrain for concealment. Stealth checks are opposed by the fortress sentries' Perception, with a −4 penalty to the sentries due to the dark sand and the distance.
- **Distraction:** The sandship makes a visible approach while the party circles on foot from the opposite direction. This requires coordination (a successful Perception or Sense Motive DC 18 from the party to time the approach correctly) but splits the Asura defenses.

**Encounter: The Oasis Fortress**

The fortress is defended by the Sandwalker cartel's true leadership — Asuras operating without disguise in their own stronghold.

**Exterior defenses:** 4 upasundas (CR 9) patrol the fortress perimeter in pairs. They are elite melee combatants — six-armed, wielding three weapons each without penalty, with all-around vision (immune to flanking) and improved evasion. They fight aggressively and without fear, engaging immediately upon detecting intruders. If the party approaches stealthily and eliminates a pair without raising an alarm (the paired patrol route means the two pairs are separated by approximately 200 feet), the interior garrison does not immediately respond.

**Interior:** The fortress interior is a single large chamber built around the oasis pool — roughly 150 feet in diameter, with a domed ceiling 40 feet high. The pool occupies the center (30-foot diameter, 20 feet deep, the water dark and warm). Surrounding the pool are low structures that serve as quarters, armouries, and communication nodes. The architecture's alien quality is more pronounced inside — the walls curve in ways that suggest they were grown rather than built, and faint patterns in the glassy material pulse with a slow rhythm that resembles breathing.

**The boss:** The fortress is commanded by a nikaramsa (CR 14) — a Large, twin lion-headed Asura corruptor who has served as the Sandwalker cartel's hidden leadership for decades. The nikaramsa has never appeared in the Nightmarket; the rotating intermediaries who represent the Sandwalkers are aakarshaki (CR 4) using *alter self* to assume mortal forms. The nikaramsa commands from the fortress, directing operations through its extensive illusion spell-like abilities (*veil*, *invisibility*, *magic aura*), manipulating mortal agents through *dream*, and coordinating the deep-desert predator operations that have been driving populations toward the Nightmarket. Its Pervert Miracle ability — which allows it to intercept and invert divine healing cast in its vicinity — makes it a uniquely dangerous opponent for parties that rely on clerical support.

**The garrison:** In addition to the nikaramsa commander, the fortress interior houses 2 adhukaits (CR 7) — dual-bodied shock troops that serve as the nikaramsa's personal guard — and 3 aakarshaki (CR 4) that handle infiltration and communication duties. If the exterior upasunda patrols raised an alarm, all interior forces are prepared and positioned; if not, the aakarshaki are in their mortal disguises and the adhukaits are in quarters, buying the party a surprise round.

**Battle map:** The oasis chamber is the set-piece arena. The nikaramsa takes to the air when combat begins (Fly 50 ft., perfect maneuverability), using the domed ceiling's 40-foot height to stay above melee range while its ground forces engage the party. The adhukaits engage in melee, using their Dance of Disaster ability to weave through the party's formation and their all-around vision to avoid flanking. The aakarshaki attempt to use their Soothing Call to lure individual party members toward the oasis pool (Will save to resist), where the dark water conceals a submerged passage — a creature pulled into the pool is dragged into a flooded tunnel and must escape or drown.

- **Nikaramsa tactics:** The nikaramsa is an intelligent commander that fights from the air. It opens with *blasphemy* (DC 22) if the party clusters near the entrance — at CL 14th, this can daze, weaken, or paralyze characters whose HD fall below the threshold. It then engages in melee from flight, diving to strike with its twin bites (+20, 1d8+5 each), claws (+20, 1d6+5 each), and twin tongues (+20, 1d6+5 each, 20-foot reach, with trip). The tongues are its most dangerous weapons: their 20-foot reach lets it strike from above without landing, and a successful trip from the air drops characters prone on the chamber floor where the adhukaits can capitalize. Its **Pervert Miracle** ability is the encounter's defining complication — if a party member casts a divine healing spell (cure wounds, remove curse, neutralize poison, etc.) within 60 feet, the nikaramsa can use an immediate action to attempt an opposed Charisma check against the caster. Success inverts the spell: a *cure serious wounds* becomes *inflict serious wounds*, a *remove curse* becomes *bestow curse*, a *neutralize poison* becomes *poison*. The GM should warn the party through environmental storytelling — the fortress's glassy walls are etched with inverted holy symbols, and a DC 20 Knowledge (religion) or Knowledge (planes) check on entering the chamber identifies the nikaramsa's nature and warns of its anti-divine capabilities. If reduced below 50 hp, the nikaramsa uses *limited wish* to replicate *dimension door* and retreats deeper into the fortress, forcing a pursuit through trapped corridors to finish the fight. If cornered, it summons 1d3 upasundas (50% chance of success) as a final gambit.
- **Adhukait tactics:** The adhukaits are shock troops. They charge the party's front line, using Power Attack and their twin-bodied attacks to deal heavy damage to a single target. Their all-around vision means they cannot be flanked, and their Dance of Disaster (move action, move up to speed without provoking attacks of opportunity once per round) lets them reposition to threaten the party's back line after the initial charge.
- **Aakarshaki tactics:** The aakarshaki are support — they stay at range, using their Soothing Call to create openings and their stinger attacks on any target that moves within reach. They are fragile (CR 4) and the party should be able to dispatch them quickly, but their crowd-control ability is dangerous if ignored.

**The Revelation**

The fortress contains evidence of the Asura operation's scope and purpose. Documents (in Infernal, requiring translation), communication records, and a scrying apparatus that displays a map of Arlune with marked positions of Asura operatives throughout the settled regions. The evidence confirms:

- The Sandwalkers have been operating on Arlune for over a century — longer than Garm suspected.
- The deep-desert predator attacks are directed operations: Asura strike teams driving populations toward the core settlements.
- The population concentration is deliberate — a long-term strategy serving a master beyond Arlune. The documents reference "the Architect's design" and "the gathering" in terms that suggest the concentrated population is being prepared for something, though the documents do not specify what. (The "Architect" is Tornsoul, the tenth Warlock. The documents do not name her directly — only the title. The GM should not reveal Tornsoul's identity through this evidence; at this stage, the party knows only that the Asura operations across the Nine Worlds are orchestrated by a single intelligence, not who that intelligence is.)
- Asura agents remain embedded in the Nightmarket — at least three aakarshaki in mortal disguises, operating independently of the fortress. The party cannot identify them from the records alone, but Garm's network can begin hunting them with this intelligence.

The party reports to Garm. In this interaction, he wears his true form for the first time, that of a red-haired Dwarf with a short-cropped beard. His reaction to the evidence is controlled fury — five centuries of maintaining the equilibrium, and he did not see the enemy within it. The intelligence confirms what the party has been piecing together across multiple worlds: the Asura presence is not random or opportunistic. It is coordinated, directed by a single intelligence operating across the Nine Worlds, and the corruption of the Warlocks is part of a design that none of them — not even Garm — fully understood. Garm absorbs this. He is quiet for a long time. When he speaks, it is with the finality of someone who has made a decision he cannot take back.

> *"I have spent five hundred years hiding. I told myself it was necessary — that the equilibrium required it, that the people needed the Trademaster more than they needed Garm. And perhaps that was true, once. But I have been hiding from a war I did not know was being fought, and while I hid, the enemy dug in beneath my feet."*
>
> *He stands. It is the first time you have seen him stand to his full height — he is not tall, even for a Dwarf, but there is something in his posture that was not there before. Something that looks like the person on the coin.*
>
> *"No more hiding. It is time for Garm the Honest to come home."*

This speech marks the transition to Scene 6. Garm begins preparing for his public reveal.

*GM note: The three tasks should feel distinct in tone: Task 1 is an infiltration with a horror reveal at the bottom, Task 2 is a heist with a combat climax, and Task 3 is a military assault against an alien stronghold. The escalation is deliberate — the party moves from fighting a single Asura in a confined space, to confronting one with mortal allies in a trapped maze, to assaulting a fortified position held by a coordinated Asura garrison. The Asura thread ties all three tasks together: each encounter reveals a deeper layer of the hidden enemy's presence, and by the end of Task 3 the party should understand that the hidden enemy's reach extends into every corner of the Nine Worlds. The milestone after Task 1 gives the party a power bump before the more difficult encounters in Tasks 2 and 3, and the absence of a milestone after Tasks 2 and 3 is intentional — the level 12 milestone comes at the chapter's conclusion in Scene 7, serving as the capstone for the entire Arlune arc.*

---

### Scene 6: The Trademaster Revealed

This scene has two paths — the turning path (if the party completed the three tasks and earned Garm's trust) and the combat path (if the party approaches the Moonstone without Garm's trust or is hostile). The turning path is the chapter's climax and the more complex of the two; the combat path is a contingency for parties that chose violence or failed to reach Garm through the investigation.

**The Turning Path — The Battle of the Market Square**

Garm spends three days preparing after the party returns from the oasis fortress. His network — the invisible architecture of intermediaries, mercenaries, and magitek constructs that has kept Arlune in equilibrium for five centuries — activates for the last time. Not to maintain the balance. To break it.

The party sees flashes of the preparation during the three-day window: Garm's contacts move through the Nightmarket with unusual urgency, visiting specific locations, speaking to specific people, placing specific objects in specific positions. Market stalls are relocated. Cargo crates appear on rooftops. A construction crew repairs a damaged section of the square's perimeter wall with unusual speed and unusual materials. None of this attracts attention from the cartels, because none of it looks like anything other than the normal churn of the Nightmarket's daily business. That is the point. Five centuries of practice have made Garm very good at hiding things in plain sight.

Garm contacts the party on the third evening through his usual channel and provides a final briefing. His demeanor has changed. The caution is still there — it will always be there — but underneath it is something that was not present before: anticipation.

> *"Tomorrow at midday, I am going to stand in the Market Square and tell the people of Arlune who I am. The remaining cartels will not take this well. They have spent the last week watching their power erode — the water monopoly broken, the Voidrunners humiliated, the Sandwalkers exposed as demons — and they know that someone is behind it. They do not know who. When I step into the open, they will know, and they will act."*
>
> *He pauses, and the ghost of a smile crosses his face — the first you have seen that reaches his eyes. "I have been the Trademaster for five hundred years. I have spent every one of those years anticipating the moment when someone would try to kill me in the Market Square. I am, it turns out, very well prepared for it."*
>
> *"The cartels will send their thugs. Gods know the demons likely have their own plans. I have positioned my people throughout the square — mercenaries, constructs, allies who owe me favors I have been saving for exactly this moment. My forces will handle the syndicate fighters. I need you to handle the demons. And when it is over, I need the people to see me standing, alive, in the square. That is all that matters. If they see me, the rest follows."*

**The Square**

The Market Square is the Nightmarket's central open space — a roughly 200-foot-diameter plaza paved with ancient stone, surrounded on all sides by the bazaar's permanent structures. A raised stone platform at the square's eastern end serves as a stage for public announcements, auctions, and the occasional execution. The square is the most public space on Arlune — on any given day, several hundred people pass through it, and at midday it is crowded with vendors, shoppers, and the general foot traffic of a city that never sleeps.

Garm arrives at the square at midday, still in his Tallis disguise — the nondescript Gnome merchant, unremarkable, invisible. He climbs the stone platform. The crowd pays no attention. A small Gnome climbing onto the announcement stage is not noteworthy; people do it constantly to sell goods, settle arguments, or make fools of themselves.

Then Garm removes the *hat of disguise*, and five centuries of hiding end in a single breath.

> *The Gnome is gone. In his place stands a Dwarf — stocky, broad-shouldered, with a grey-streaked beard and a face that anyone who has ever seen one of the many paintings depicting him would recognize immediately. It is the same face: the round cheeks, the strong jaw, the eyes that crinkle at the corners as though waiting for a joke to land. But this version of the face is older, and tired, and lit with something that looks like it might be joy or might be terror or might be both at once.*
>
> *The crowd nearest the platform notices first. A murmur. Then a second wave, as people turn to see what the murmur is about. Then a third, as the recognition spreads — not everyone knows the face, but enough do, and those who do are already speaking the name.*
>
> *Garm opens his mouth to speak.*
>
> *He does not get the chance.*

**The Ambush**

The remaining cartels strike first. They have been watching the square — the Ironbound spotted the unusual cargo placements, the Dune Syndicate noted the construction crew, and the Exchange sold the intelligence to everyone simultaneously. The cartels do not know that the Trademaster is about to reveal himself, but they know that something is happening in the square, and they have agreed (for the first time in decades) that it should not be allowed to happen.

Simultaneously, the Asura response arrives. The Asura presence on Arlune was not limited to the Sandwalker cartel — a separate operative, a japalisura (CR 12) held in reserve for exactly this contingency, has been embedded in the Nightmarket since before the party arrived. When Garm drops his disguise, the japalisura drops its own and moves to kill the Warlock before he can speak.

The ambush unfolds in a single chaotic moment:

- From the north and south, syndicate fighters pour into the square — a coalition of Ironbound brothers, Dune Syndicate enforcers, and Silken Court thugs, roughly forty combatants in total. They are not coordinated; the three cartels distrust each other even now, and their fighters arrive in three separate groups that immediately begin jostling for position.
- From the east, the japalisura reveals itself on a rooftop overlooking the platform, accompanied by 3 upasundas (CR 9) that have been posing as Ironbound brothers. The japalisura opens fire on Garm with its dual bows from elevation. The upasundas leap from the rooftop into the crowd, scattering civilians and carving a path toward the platform.

**Garm's counterstrike triggers simultaneously.**

Cargo crates on the rooftops burst open, revealing magitek cannon emplacements manned by Garm's mercenaries. The "construction crew's" repaired wall section unfolds into a barricade that channels the syndicate fighters into a killzone. Market stalls along the square's perimeter tip over to reveal armored fighting positions. And from beneath the square itself — through grates, through maintenance hatches, through the sewers — Garm's reserve force emerges: forty mercenaries and a dozen magitek constructs (medium-sized combat platforms armed with crossbow arrays and melee blades), all pre-positioned over the preceding three days.

The square becomes a battlefield. Garm's allies engage the syndicate fighters. The magitek constructs form a perimeter around the platform, protecting Garm while he directs his forces with shouted commands and hand signals — the Trademaster's invisible network made suddenly, violently out in the open.

**The Party's Fight**

The party's objective is the Asura force. Garm's mercenaries and constructs can handle the syndicate thugs — they are outnumbered but better positioned, better equipped, and operating under a plan that Garm has been refining for five centuries. The Asuras are the threat that Garm cannot counter with planning alone.

**Enemies:**
- 1 japalisura (CR 12) — positioned on the eastern rooftop, 30 feet above the square, firing into the crowd with dual bows and *flame arrow*
- 3 upasundas (CR 9) — in the square itself, cutting through civilians and Garm's mercenaries toward the platform

**Terrain:** The Market Square is a chaotic battlefield. Key features:
- **The platform (east):** Raised stone, 5 feet above the square floor, 30 feet square. Garm stands here, protected by 4 magitek constructs. The platform provides high ground but also makes anyone on it visible to the japalisura on the rooftop.
- **The rooftop (east, above the platform):** The japalisura's firing position. 30 feet above the square, accessible by climbing the building's exterior (Climb DC 20) or through the building's interior (requires entering a ground-floor door and ascending three flights of stairs through a crowded, panicking building). Flight is the fastest route.
- **Crowd chaos:** Hundreds of civilians are in the square. Movement through the crowd is at half speed unless the character uses Acrobatics (DC 15) to weave through or Intimidate (DC 15) to scatter people out of the way. The upasundas ignore this — they cut through the crowd without slowing, leaving wounded civilians in their wake.
- **Barricades and fighting positions:** Garm's pre-positioned cover provides the party with options. The barricades (hardness 10, 30 hp) block line of sight to the rooftop from certain angles, and the fighting positions provide cover (+4 AC) for ranged attacks.
- **Syndicate melee:** The background battle between Garm's mercenaries and the syndicate fighters is an environmental hazard. Stray crossbow bolts, fleeing combatants, and the occasional magitek cannon blast create a dynamic battlefield. Characters moving through the syndicate engagement zones risk being caught in the crossfire (10% chance per round of being targeted by a stray attack, +8 to hit, 1d8+2 damage).

**Upasunda tactics:** The three upasundas are elite melee combatants closing on the platform. They fight as a pack — all three target the same enemy, using their six-armed multiweapon fighting to overwhelm defenses. Their all-around vision makes them immune to flanking, and their improved evasion reduces the effectiveness of area spells. They do not retreat. They are here to kill Garm, and they will go through anything in their path to reach him. If the party intercepts them in the crowd, the fight happens amid panicking civilians — area-effect spells and abilities risk collateral damage, and the party must balance tactical effectiveness against civilian casualties.

**Japalisura tactics:** The japalisura fires from the rooftop, targeting Garm, the party's spellcasters, and anyone attempting to reach its position. Its dual bows with *flame arrow* deal heavy damage at range (+20/+20/+15/+15/+10, 1d6+4 plus 1d6 fire per hit), and its elevated position makes melee engagement difficult. It has *see invisibility* active and will use *true seeing* if the party employs concealment. If a party member reaches the rooftop, the japalisura switches to handaxes and fights in melee — it is competent but not exceptional in close combat, and a dedicated melee character should be able to match it. Its priority is eliminating Garm; if it gets a clear shot at the platform, it takes it, even if it means disengaging from a melee opponent (provoking attacks of opportunity).

**Garm in combat:** Garm fights alongside the party if the Asuras breach the platform perimeter. He is a mythic Dwarf Rogue — fast, precise, and devastatingly effective with a pair of short swords. He fights as a flanking partner, setting up sneak attacks and calling out tactical information ("The archer's reloading — you have four seconds"; "The six-armed one on the left is favoring its right side — broken rib, hit it there"). He does not panic. He has been anticipating this fight for five hundred years, and the experience of finally being in it — openly, as himself, without disguise — is intoxicating. He is, for the first time in centuries, Garm the Honest.

**The Battle's Resolution**

The syndicate fighters break first. Garm's pre-positioned forces were designed to contain and demoralize, not annihilate, and the combination of magitek cannon fire, armored constructs, and professional mercenaries hitting from prepared positions shatters the cartels' ad-hoc coalition within minutes. The Ironbound retreat in disciplined formation (the Tenets require withdrawal over pointless death); the Dune Syndicate enforcers scatter; the Silken Court thugs break and run outright.

The Asuras are harder. The upasundas fight until destroyed — they do not retreat, do not surrender, and do not respond to morale effects. The japalisura fights until reduced below 40 hp, at which point it uses *dimension door* to flee the Nightmarket entirely. If the party prevents its escape (through *dimensional anchor*, grappling, or positioning that blocks its teleportation line of effect), it fights to the death.

When the last Asura falls and the last syndicate fighter flees, the square is wrecked. Market stalls are shattered, the ancient paving stones are scorched and cracked, and the barricades are riddled with crossbow bolts and scorch marks. The crowd — which scattered during the fighting — begins to return, drawn by the silence that follows violence and by the rumor that has been spreading through the bazaar like wildfire since Garm dropped his disguise.

The Trademaster is here. The Trademaster is *Garm*.

**The Turning**

Garm climbs back onto the platform. He is battered — a cut above one eye, his armor scored, his breath coming hard. The magitek constructs form a ring around the platform's base, but they are standing down, weapons lowered. The mercenaries hold position but do not advance. The square fills with people.

> *The Dwarf stands on the platform and looks out at the crowd — the vendors, the children, the debtors, the workers, the sandship crews, the beggars who eat at the charity kitchens he has secretly funded for centuries. The crowd looks back. The silence is vast.*
>
> *"My name is Garm," he says. His voice carries across the square — not loud, but clear, in the way that a voice is clear when the person speaking has been waiting five hundred years to use it. "Some of you know the name. Some of you have heard the stories. The stories are mostly wrong, but the name is right. I am Garm the Honest, and I have been the Trademaster for five hundred years."*
>
> *The silence deepens. The crowd is not breathing.*
>
> *"I built the system that runs this world. The cartels, the equilibrium, the invisible hand — I built all of it, because it was the best I could do at the time, and because I was too afraid to do anything better. I am done being afraid."*

If the party has the Lucky Coin, Garm produces it during the speech — holding it up so the crowd can see Casomir's work, the laughing face that matches his own.

> *"A friend made this for me, a long time ago. He told me the real treasure was the friends you make along the way." Garm's voice catches, just slightly. "He meant it. He always meant everything he said. I have spent five centuries learning how to hide, and it took a coin and a group of strangers to remind me that hiding is not the same as being safe."*

The corruption lifts. It does not break dramatically or shatter like glass — it dissolves, like a fog burning off in sunlight. The pathological secrecy, the compulsive hiding, the inability to be known — it simply stops. Garm takes a breath, and it is the first honest breath he has drawn in five hundred years. The crowd watches, and they do not understand what they are seeing, but they can feel it — something shifting in the air, something old and heavy lifting, something becoming possible that was not possible a moment ago.

The crowd's reaction builds slowly: murmurs, then voices, then a roar. The legend is true. Garm has come home.

*GM note: The turning scene should be the emotional climax of the chapter. The battle provides the spectacle, but the speech provides the meaning. Garm's corruption was never about power or cruelty — it was about fear, and the turning is about a person who has been afraid for five hundred years finally deciding to stop. The Lucky Coin callback, if available, ties the moment back to Casomir and to Torgun and to the party's own journey through the Nine Worlds. Let the players feel the weight of it. They earned this.*

**The Combat Path — Warlock Fight**

If the party approaches the Moonstone without Garm's trust, or if they betrayed his confidence during the task sequence, or if they were hostile during the Scene 4 meeting, this path triggers instead. Garm does not reveal himself publicly. He fights.

The combat path triggers when the party reaches the Moonstone's hidden valley (see Scene 7 for location details). Garm has been watching the Moonstone for decades, and any approach by unauthorized individuals triggers his contingency plan.

**The terrain:** The desert surrounding the Moonstone valley is Garm's killing ground. He has spent years preparing it — concealed pits filled with sand that collapses into 20-foot drops (Perception DC 25 to spot, Reflex DC 20 to avoid, 2d6 falling damage plus buried in loose sand), magitek tripwire alarms that activate *deeper darkness* in a 60-foot radius, and pre-positioned crossbow emplacements manned by mercenary teams (4 teams of 3 mercenaries each, warrior NPC stat block, CR 5, positioned at the valley's cardinal approaches).

**Garm's tactics:** Garm fights as a mythic Dwarf Rogue at CR 13 (use the rogue NPC stat block advanced to CR 13 with mythic abilities). He does not engage directly until the party has been softened by the terrain and the mercenaries. He uses *invisibility* (from a ring), *dimension door* (from a scroll cache hidden at multiple points in the valley), and his extensive knowledge of the terrain to hit and run — emerging from concealment to deliver sneak attacks, then vanishing before the party can retaliate. He fights to survive, not to kill; his goal is to drive the party away from the Moonstone, not to destroy them. He calls out during the fight — not taunts, but warnings.

> *"You do not want to do this. I have been preparing for this fight longer than most of you have been alive. Leave the valley. Come back with a better offer. I am not unreasonable — I am just very, very careful."*

If the party persists and corners Garm (reducing his escape routes by eliminating the mercenary teams and locating his scroll caches), he fights to the death rather than flee the Moonstone. He does not surrender. He does not beg. He fights like a person who has weighed the options and decided that dying here is preferable to being dragged into someone else's plan without trust.

**If Garm is killed:** The party gains access to the Moonstone but loses Garm as an ally. His corruption score adds +1 to the party's total (his corruption was the lightest of the Warlocks — he was never truly lost, only hidden). The Nightmarket does not learn what happened. The Trademaster simply stops operating, and the equilibrium begins its slow collapse. Arlune's contribution to the Battle of Nexus is minimal.

*GM note: The combat path should feel like a tragedy, not a victory. Garm is the one Warlock who wanted to turn, and the party is killing him because they could not or would not earn his trust. Play Garm's warnings during the fight as genuine — he is not baiting the party, he is giving them every chance to reconsider. If a player expresses regret during the fight or attempts to parley mid-combat, Garm listens — he is desperate enough to hope, even now. A DC 25 Diplomacy check during the fight can halt the combat and redirect to a negotiated turning, though the trust deficit means Garm's cooperation is conditional and his contribution to the Battle of Nexus is reduced. Do not make this easy, but do not make it impossible.*

---

### Scene 7: The Moonstone

**If Turned**

Garm escorts the party personally, and Torgun insists on providing the transport. The *Fist of Garm* meets them at the western berths, its crew buzzing with a tension that has nothing to do with the mission and everything to do with the fact that Torgun has just learned the truth. Garm told him privately the night before — not through intermediaries, not through coded messages, not through any of the layers of operational security that defined the Trademaster's existence, but face to face, in Torgun's quarters aboard the ship, as himself. The details of that conversation are between them. What the party sees is the aftermath: a young Dwarf captain who is quieter than usual, whose eyes are red-rimmed, and who looks at the old Dwarf standing on his deck with an expression that shifts between awe and fury and grief and something that might, given time, become peace.

Torgun does not speak to Garm for the first several hours of the journey. He runs his ship. He gives orders. He conducts himself with the same brash competence he has always shown. But there is a stiffness in his shoulders and a deliberateness in his movements that tells the party he is holding something together by force of will. Garm, for his part, does not push. He stands at the viewport in the crew galley and watches the desert pass, and he waits, because waiting is the one thing five centuries of hiding have taught him to do.

It is Torgun who breaks first. Sometime during the second day, the party hears raised voices from the helm — Torgun's, sharp and cracking, and Garm's, low and steady. The argument lasts twenty minutes. What follows is silence, then the sound of the helm door opening, and then Torgun and Garm emerge together. Torgun's eyes are red again. Garm's are too. Neither of them explains. But when Garm moves to the galley to make tea, Torgun follows him, and they sit at the small table together, and for the first time, they talk.

The party can join them or give them privacy. If they join, they hear the conversations that five centuries of separation have stored up — Garm asking about Torgun's childhood, his grandmother, the stories she told; Torgun asking about the family he never knew, the decisions Garm made, the weight of hiding from your own blood. The conversations are not easy. Some of them are painful. But they are honest, and they continue for the rest of the journey.

Garm also talks to the party during the transit. Not about strategy, not about the cartels, not about operational security — about himself. About Casomir, and the early days, and the first time they walked the Rainbow Road together. About the war, and the Compact, and the weight of accepting power he never wanted. About the centuries after, when the Road fell and the worlds went dark and he came home to find Arlune tearing itself apart.

> *"I watched him grow up," Garm says, glancing toward the helm where Torgun is calling course adjustments to his crew. "From a distance. Through intermediaries. I knew his name before he knew mine. I saw him take command of this ship and name it after me, and I — " He stops. Starts again. "I wanted to walk up to him and say, 'I am your great-great-grandfather, and I am proud of you.' I could not. The Trademaster could not afford to have a family. So I watched, and I funded his supply runs through three layers of shell accounts, and I made sure the cartels left him alone, and I told myself that was enough."*
>
> *He turns the Lucky Coin over in his fingers — the laughing Dwarf, the pile of treasure, the metal worn smooth by generations of hands that were not his.*
>
> *"It was not enough."*

The journey takes four days. The terrain grows increasingly remote — the trade routes end, the sand darkens, and the dunes rise to heights that block the moonlight for stretches at a time. Garm navigates from memory; there are no maps to this place, because he destroyed every map that showed it. Torgun handles the ship with quiet skill, threading the *Fist of Garm* through dune fields that would strand a lesser captain.

**If Killed**

The party must find the Moonstone through Garm's records, recovered from his safehouse (the curio shop in the lower bazaar, which contains a hidden basement with decades of intelligence files, operational plans, and personal journals). A DC 25 Linguistics or Knowledge (geography) check, cross-referencing Garm's journals with navigational data from his sandship log, identifies the Moonstone's approximate location in the deep desert. Alternatively, Silvana Morne of the Exchange can assist — for a price (5,000 gp or a significant favor), she provides the intelligence her network has gathered on the Trademaster's movements over the decades, which includes a pattern of regular trips into the deep eastern desert that always end at the same coordinates.

The journey without Garm is lonely and tense. The party must hire or commandeer a sandship, navigate uncharted desert using incomplete directions, and deal with the deep-desert hazards (the same environmental conditions from Task 3's journey apply). There is no guide, no conversation, no warmth. The desert is indifferent, and the party is alone in it.

**The Hidden Valley**

The Moonstone's valley is invisible from the surface. The dunes rise to form a natural bowl — a depression in the desert floor roughly half a mile in diameter, its rim high enough to block line of sight from every direction. The only approach is a narrow cut in the southern rim, barely wide enough for a sandship to pass through single-file. The sand inside the valley is white — not the pale silver of the Twilight Sea but a pure, bleached white that seems to glow even when the moon is below the horizon. The air is still. The silence is absolute.

> *The valley opens before you like a held breath. The sand is white — impossibly, luminously white, as though the moonlight has soaked into the ground over centuries and the ground has forgotten how to let it go. At the valley's center stands the Moonstone.*
>
> *It is larger than you expected. A monolith of opalescent stone, roughly fifty feet tall and twenty feet wide at the base, tapering to an irregular point that catches the moonlight and refracts it into slow-moving patterns on the white sand. The stone's surface is smooth but not featureless — deep within its translucent mass, shapes move. Not creatures, not shadows — patterns, like the interference lines of sound waves made visible. They shift with a rhythm that is almost, but not quite, perceptible as music.*
>
> *The sand around the monolith's base is undisturbed. Nothing has walked here in a very long time.*

**The Moonstone's Power**

The Moonstone is a place of power unlike any the party has previously encountered. The places of power on Diaspola, Tanis, and Auzra were sites of raw mythic energy — impressive but comprehensible, their effects clearly magical. The Moonstone is something older. The opalescent stone predates the Rainbow Road, predates the Compact, predates recorded history on Arlune. Garm (if present) does not know who made it or when. He knows only that it was here when the first settlers arrived, and that it has been healing the world's wounds ever since.

When the moon passes directly overhead — an event that occurs once every twenty-four hours and lasts approximately thirty minutes — the Moonstone activates. The opalescent surface blazes with light, not the pale silver of moonlight but something warmer and more complex, a radiance that contains colors the party has never seen and that they will not be able to describe afterward. Anyone standing within thirty feet of the monolith during this activation has all diseases, curses, level drain, and ability damage permanently removed. The healing is not magical in the conventional sense — *detect magic* registers nothing, *dispel magic* has no effect, and *antimagic field* does not suppress it. The Moonstone heals because it is a place where the world is closer to what it was meant to be, and in its presence, the damage that the world has accumulated simply undoes itself.

**Mythic Tier 5**

The party activates the Moonstone as a mythic place of power by standing within its radiance when the moon passes overhead. The activation is not a ritual — there is no incantation, no sacrifice, no test. The Moonstone responds to those who have earned the right to stand before it, and the party's journey through Arlune — the trust they built, the systems they dismantled, the hidden enemy they exposed — is proof enough.

The mythic power settles into the party like warmth after cold. It is the fifth infusion, and by now the party can feel the accumulation — the sense of becoming something more than they were, of carrying a weight that is not a burden but a responsibility. The power of the Moonstone is patience. It is the strength to wait, to watch, to endure, and to act when the moment is right. It is Garm's strength, and it is the desert's strength, and now it belongs to the party.

**The Fifth Fragment of the Song of Heaven**

The Song of Heaven's fifth fragment manifests as the Moonstone's radiance fades. It does not arrive as sound — not at first. The party feels it before they hear it: a vibration in the white sand beneath their feet, a tremor in the air that is not wind, a resonance in the opalescent stone that makes the moving patterns within the monolith slow and align.

> *The radiance dims. The Moonstone's light pulls inward, contracting from a blaze to a glow to a single point of warmth deep inside the stone — and then the point opens, and the Song comes through.*
>
> *It arrives as a silver thread winding beneath everything you have heard before. The four fragments you carry — the deep foundational chord from Diaspola, the rising melody of Tanis, the mournful descent of Auzra's loss, the bright defiant cry of the previous world — all of them are present, all of them sounding at once in your memory and your bones. But now there is something underneath them. Something that was always there, inaudible until this moment, the way the stars are always present in the sky but invisible until the sun sets.*
>
> *The silver thread does not add a new melody. It reveals the connections between the ones you already carry. The foundational chord resonates with the rising melody in a way you never noticed — they share a harmonic, a single note that bridges them. The mournful descent and the defiant cry mirror each other, inverse reflections of the same phrase. The four fragments are not four separate songs. They are four voices in a single composition, and the fifth fragment is the moment you hear the composition instead of the voices.*
>
> *The feeling is vertiginous. The Song is now more than half complete — five of nine — and the sense of a larger pattern becoming visible is unmistakable. You cannot hear what the completed Song will be. But you can feel its shape in the spaces between the fragments, the way you can feel the shape of a word you have forgotten: present, patient, waiting to be remembered.*

The fragment is *mystery* — the hidden structure that holds the others together, the architecture beneath the music, the reason the Song exists at all. It is the Song heard by moonlight, heard in context, heard for the first time as something larger than its parts.

If Garm is present, he hears the Song too — the first Warlock to hear it willingly, without fighting, without dying. He stands in the Moonstone's fading light and listens, and the expression on his face is one the party has not seen before: wonder, unguarded and complete.

### Chapter Conclusion

**World State (Turned):** Garm establishes a democratic council drawn from the guild leaders, charity kitchen organizers, and independent sandship captains — the people who kept Arlune functioning while the cartels fought over the profits. The transition is messy and contested. The remaining cartels (the Sandwalkers destroyed, the Wellkeepers' monopoly broken, the Voidrunners humiliated) resist but fracture without the Trademaster's invisible hand balancing them. The Ironbound, bound by the Tenets, respect the outcome of the Market Square battle and negotiate terms with the new council — Qadir Stonefist recognizes a legitimate authority when one emerges through combat, and the Ironbound's discipline makes them valuable partners in maintaining order during the transition. The Dune Syndicate fragments into competing factions. The Silken Court retreats into the entertainment sector, shedding its worst practices under pressure from the newly empowered populace. The Exchange adapts, as it always does — Silvana Morne sells intelligence to the new council as readily as she sold it to the cartels.

Some cartel members join the new government. Others retreat to the deep desert to become pirates. The charity kitchens, no longer secretly funded, become the backbone of a public welfare system. The magitek purification infrastructure, freed from Wellkeeper control, is distributed and maintained by the engineers who always ran it. The transition is imperfect. It will take years, possibly decades. But it is real.

Garm remains as an advisor — diminished in mythic power but finally free to be himself. He visits Torgun openly. He walks the Nightmarket without disguise. He is warm, funny, self-deprecating, and utterly incapable of shutting up — the person his corruption hid from the world turns out to be someone people genuinely enjoy being around. He is, as Casomir always said, good company.

Arlune's contribution to the Battle of Nexus: a sandship fleet (transport and logistics for the allied forces), desert fighters (hardened mercenaries and independent captains who answer Garm's call), magitek engineers (specialists who can maintain and repair the allied forces' equipment in the field), and Garm himself — reduced in mythic power but invaluable as a strategic advisor. If the party completed the Lucky Coin side quest, Torgun Ironfold and the crew of the *Fist of Garm* serve as the fleet's vanguard.

**World State (Killed):** The cartel equilibrium collapses. Without the Trademaster's hidden hand, the six remaining organizations (the Sandwalkers already destroyed or exposed) go to war. The Nightmarket becomes a battleground — territorial disputes erupt into open violence, the water supply becomes contested when the Wellkeepers attempt to reassert their monopoly, and the charity kitchens close when their anonymous funding stops arriving. The common people suffer. Refugees stream out of the Nightmarket toward the smaller settlements, reversing the population concentration that the Asuras engineered but creating new humanitarian crises in towns that lack the infrastructure to support them.

Arlune's contribution to the Battle of Nexus is minimal: a handful of independent sandship captains who answer the call out of personal conviction, and possibly Torgun Ironfold and his crew if the party completed the Lucky Coin side quest. Torgun comes regardless of the political situation — he fights for Garm's memory, not for a government that does not exist.

**Milestone:** Level 12 is awarded at the conclusion of this chapter. Mythic Tier 5 was granted earlier at the Moonstone (Scene 7).

**Transition:** The party returns to Nexus via the Rainbow Road. The journey back through Arlune is different depending on the outcome. If Garm was turned, the return to the gate town is a triumphal progress — news of the Trademaster's identity has spread across the continent, and settlements along the route send sandship escorts, offer supplies, and cheer the party's passage. Garm accompanies them to the gate town and sees them off with a handshake and a promise: "When you need me, I will come. You will not need to ask twice." If Garm was killed, the return is quiet and fast — the party has no friends on Arlune beyond Torgun, and the sooner they leave, the better.

On Nexus, Maranthe meets them with grim news. The demon raids are no longer raids — they are assaults, coordinated and escalating, targeting the gate towns and the infrastructure the party has been building. Two gate towns have been hit simultaneously, their defenses breached for the first time. The pattern is unmistakable: whatever intelligence is directing the Asura operations across the Nine Worlds has shifted from infiltration to open aggression. The war that has been building since the party's first steps on the Rainbow Road is arriving, and the next world — Vatra — is the one where the fire burns hottest.

The party rests, resupplies, and departs for the Vatra gate town.

---
